Generate
\mathsf{ct}_{j,b}\leftarrow\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek}_{j,b},m_{j})
for
j\in[\ell]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
.

•

Output
\mathsf{ct}=\{\mathsf{ct}_{j,b}\}_{j\in[\ell],b\in\{0,1\}}
.

\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{dk}(x),\mathsf{ct})\rightarrow\tilde{m}
:

On input a marked key
\mathsf{dk}(x)=(x,\{\mathsf{dk}^{\prime}_{j}\}_{j\in[\ell]})
or a master secret key
\mathsf{msk}=\{\mathsf{dk}_{j,b}\}_{j\in[\ell],b\in\{0,1\}}

and
\mathsf{ct}=\{\mathsf{ct}_{j,b}\}_{j\in[\ell],b\in\{0,1\}}
, the decryption algorithm proceeds as follows.

•

If the first input is a master secret key, set
\mathsf{dk}^{\prime}_{j}=\mathsf{dk}_{j,0}
and
x[j]=0
for all
j\in[\ell]
.

•

Run
m^{\prime}_{j}\leftarrow\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{dk}^{\prime}_{j},\mathsf{ct}_{j,x[j]})
for
j\in[\ell]
.

•

Output
m^{\prime}=m^{\prime}_{1}\|m^{\prime}_{2}\|\ldots\|m^{\prime}_{\ell}

Correctness and one-wayness of the above scheme immediately follows from correctness and IND-CPA security of
\mathsf{PKE}
, respectively. Below, we prove parallel mark extractability.

Security proof

Theorem 3.1.

The above construction satisfies parallel mark extractability if
\mathsf{PKE}
satisfies IND-CPA security.

Proof.

To prove the theorem, we first define the extraction algorithm
\mathpzc{Ext}
as follows.

\mathpzc{Ext}(\{\mathsf{ek}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]},(U{A},\rho{A}))\to\{x^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
:

On input
\{\mathsf{ek}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
and the description of the adversary
\mathpzc{A}=(U{A},\rho{A})
, it proceeds as follows.

•

Parse the encrption key as

\mathsf{ek}_{i}=\{\mathsf{ek}_{i,j,b}\}_{j\in[\ell],b\in\{0,1\}}
for each
i\in[n]
.

•

Choose
m_{i,j,0}\leftarrow\{0,1\}
and set
m_{i,j,1}=1-m_{i,j,0}
for
i\in[n]
and
j\in[\ell]
.

•

Generate
\mathsf{ct}_{i,j,b}\leftarrow\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek}_{i,j,b},m_{i,j,b})
for
i\in[n]
,
j\in[\ell]
, and
b\in\{0,1\}
.

•

Set
\mathsf{ct}_{i}=\{\mathsf{ct}_{i,j,b}\}_{j\in[\ell],b\in\{0,1\}}
for
i\in[n]
.

•

Run the adversary
\mathpzc{A}
on input
\{\mathsf{ct}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
(i.e., run the unitary
U{A}
on input
(\{\mathsf{ct}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]},\rho{A})
and measure the output register) to obtain
\{m^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}

•

Parse
m^{\prime}_{i}=m^{\prime}_{i}[1]\|\cdots\|m^{\prime}_{i}[\ell]
for
i\in[n]
.

•

Let
x^{\prime}_{i,j}
be the unique bit such that
m^{\prime}_{i}[j]=m_{i,j,x^{\prime}_{i,j}}
for
i\in[n]
and
j\in[\ell]
.

•

Let
x^{\prime}_{i}=x^{\prime}_{i,1}\|\ldots\|x^{\prime}_{i,\ell}
for
i\in[n]
.

•

Output
\{x^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
.

We then prove the theorem by a sequence of hybrids.
For the sake of the contradiction, let us consider an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
against the parallel predictability game
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{WPKE},\mathpzc{A},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{pre}}(1^{\lambda})
.
In the following, the event that
\mathpzc{A}
wins in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{\rm xx}
is denoted by
\mathsf{E}_{\rm xx}
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
:

This is the parallel predictability game between the challenger and an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
.
Namely, the game proceeds as follows.

1.

The challenger runs
\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})
to obtain
\mathsf{ek}_{i}
for
i\in[n]
as follows.

•

Generate
(\mathsf{ek}_{i,j,b},\mathsf{dk}_{i,j,b})\leftarrow\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})
for
j\in[\ell]
, and
b\in\{0,1\}
.

•

Set
\mathsf{ek}_{i}=\{\mathsf{ek}_{i,j,b}\}_{j\in[\ell],b\in\{0,1\}}
.

It then sends
\{\mathsf{ek}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

2.

Given
\{\mathsf{ek}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
, the adversary
\mathpzc{A}
sends arbitrarily chosen
x_{1},...,x_{n}\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
to the challenger.

3.

The challenger sets
\mathsf{dk}_{i}(x_{i})=(x_{i},\{\mathsf{dk}_{i,j,x_{i}[j]}\}_{j\in[\ell]})
for
i\in[n]
and sends
\{\mathsf{dk}_{i}(x_{i})\}_{i\in[n]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

4.

For
i\in[n]
, the challenger generates
m_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}
and

\mathsf{ct}_{i,j,b}\leftarrow\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek}_{i,j,b},m_{i}[j])
for
j\in[\ell]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
, sets

\mathsf{ct}_{i}=\{\mathsf{ct}_{i,j,b}\}_{j\in[\ell],b\in\{0,1\}}
and sends
\{\mathsf{ct}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

5.

Receiving
\{\mathsf{ct}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
from the challenger,
\mathpzc{A}
outputs
\{m^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
.

6.

The challenger sets the output of the game to be
1
(indicating that
\mathpzc{A}
wins) if
m^{\prime}_{i}=m_{i}
for all
i\in[n]
and
0
otherwise.

By definition, we have
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{0}=1]=\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{WPKE},\mathpzc{A},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ow}}(\lambda)
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
:

In this hybrid, we change the way of generating
\mathsf{ct}_{i,j,1-x_{i}[j]}
for
i\in[n]
and
j\in[\ell]
.
Specifically, it is generated as

\mathsf{ct}_{i,j,1-x_{i}[j]}\leftarrow\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek}_{i,j,1-x_{i}[j]},1-m_{i}[j]).

(62)

Note that
\mathsf{ct}_{i,j,x_{i}[j]}
is generated as
\mathsf{ct}_{i,j,x_{i}[j]}\leftarrow\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek}_{i,j,x_{i}[j]},m_{i}[j])
similarly to the previous hybrid.

Since
\mathsf{dk}_{i,j,1-x_{i}[j]}
is not given to the adversary for any
i\in[n]
and
j\in[\ell]
, by the IND-CPA security of
\mathsf{PKE}
, we have

\absolutevalue{\Pr[\event_0]-\Pr[\event_1]}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.
This can be shown using a hybrid argument over all positions of
i
and
j
. The reduction algorithm embeds its challenge instance into
\mathsf{ek}_{i,j,1-x_{i}[j]}
by randomly guessing
x_{i}[j]
, where guess is correct with probability of
1/2
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
:

In this hybrid, we change the way of generating
\mathsf{ct}_{i,j,b}
for
i\in[n]
,
j\in[\ell]
, and
b\in\{0,1\}
, and the winning condition.
In Step
4
, the challenger chooses
m_{i,j,0}\leftarrow\{0,1\}
and sets
m_{i,j,1}=1-m_{i,j,0}
for
i\in[n]
and
j\in[\ell]
. Then the challenger generates

\mathsf{ct}_{i,j,b}\leftarrow\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek}_{i,j,b},m_{i,j,b})

(63)

for
i\in[\ell]
,
j\in[n]
, and
b\in\{0,1\}
.
In Step
6
, the challenger sets the output of the game to be
1
if
m^{\prime}_{i}[j]=m_{i,j,x_{i}[j]}
for all
i\in[n]
and
j\in[\ell]
and
0
otherwise.

This is just a conceptual change, and thus we have
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{1}=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{2}=1]
.
It is also easy to see that we have

\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{2}=1]=\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{WPKE},\mathpzc{A},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ow}}(\lambda)
.

Combining the above, we obtain
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{WPKE},\mathpzc{A},\mathpzc{Ext},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ext}}(\lambda)\geq\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{WPKE},\mathpzc{A},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ow}}(\lambda)-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
as desired.
∎

3.2
Watermarkable Unpredictable Functions

Here, we define and construct watermarkable unpredictable functions (UPF).
Its definition is very similar to watermarkable PKE above; if
an adversary is given
\mathsf{key}(x)
and generates a (possibly quantum) “predictor" that can guess the output on a uniformly random input with probability
\epsilon
, then we can extract
x
from the predictor with probability
\epsilon-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
, and moreover the extraction should work even in the parallel setting.
Based on the idea of
[
KMY25
]
, we can construct such a watermarkable UPF from two-key equivocal PRFs
[
HJO+16
]
, which in turn can be built from OWFs.
To deal with evaluation queries in the security proof, which were not considered in
[
KMY25
]
, we employ a simple trick
using random bits.

Syntax

A watermarkable unpredictable functions
\mathsf{WUPF}=(\mathsf{KG},\mathsf{Mark},\mathsf{Eval})
with the input space
\{0,1\}^{\ell(\lambda)}
and the mark space
\mathcal{X}=\{\mathcal{X}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda}
consists of the following algorithms.
In the following, we omit the security parameter
\lambda
for
\mathcal{X}_{\lambda}
and
\ell(\lambda)
to denote
\mathcal{X}
and
\ell
when it is clear from the context.

\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow(\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{xk})
:

The key generation algorithm is a PPT algorithm that takes a security parameter
1^{\lambda}
, and outputs a master secret key
\mathsf{msk}
and an extraction key
\mathsf{xk}
.

\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{msk},x)\rightarrow\mathsf{key}(x)
:

The marking algorithm is a deterministic polynomial time algorithm that takes a master secret key
\mathsf{msk}
and a mark
x\in\mathcal{X}
, and outputs a marked key
\mathsf{key}(x)
.

\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{key}(x),s)\rightarrow t
:

The evaluation algorithm is a PPT algorithm that takes a
master secret key
\mathsf{msk}
or
marked key
\mathsf{key}(x)
and an input
s\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
, and outputs an output
t
.

Correctness:

For every
s\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
, we require that

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{key}(x),s)=\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{msk},s)\\
\mbox{holds for all~}x\in\mathcal{X}\end{array}\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{xk})\leftarrow\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})\\
\mathsf{key}(x)=\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{msk},x)~\mbox{for all }x\in\mathcal{X}\end{array}\right]=1-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

One can consider a relaxed correctness requirement, where the equation
\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{key}(x),s)=\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{msk},s)
only needs to hold for a fixed
x
, as opposed to all
x
simultaneously as above. Nonetheless, our construction fulfills the stronger requirement.

Correctness against adversarially chosen mark and input:

We also consider a strengthened version of the correctness. Here, we require the correctness to hold, even if the adversary chooses the mark and the input to the function adversarially. To define the notion, we define the correctness experiment involving an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
, denoted as
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathsf{WUPF},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{corre}}(1^{\lambda})
.

1.

The challenger runs
\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow(\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{xk})
.

2.

The adversary
\mathpzc{A}
is given input
1^{\lambda}
and starts to run.
Throughout the game, it has an oracle access to an oracle
\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)
, which takes as input
\hat{s}\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
and returns
\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{msk},\hat{s})
.

3.

At some point,
\mathpzc{A}
chooses arbitrary
x\in\mathcal{X}
.

4.

Then, the challenger runs
\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{msk},x)\rightarrow\mathsf{key}(x)
and return
\mathsf{key}(x)
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

5.

The adversary continues to have access to the oracle.
Finally, it outputs an input
s
. The output of the experiment is then set to be
1
if
\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{msk},s)\neq\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{key}(x),s)
.

We say that
\mathsf{WUPF}
has correctness against adversarially chosen input if for all QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathsf{WUPF},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{corre}}(1^{\lambda})\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(68)

Parallel mark extractability:

Here, we introduce the notion of “parallel mark extractability", which is similar to that for watermarkable PKE.
We first define the following parallel prediction experiment involving an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
and a challenger, parameterized by an arbitrary polynomial
n=\text{poly}(\lambda)
, denoted as
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{WUPF},\mathpzc{A},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{pre}}(1^{\lambda})
.

1.

The challenger runs
\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow(\mathsf{msk}_{i},\mathsf{xk}_{i})
for
i\in[n]
.

2.

The adversary
\mathpzc{A}
is given input
1^{\lambda}
and
1^{n}
and starts to run.
The adversary has an oracle access to the function
\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{msk}_{i},\cdot)
for each
i\in[n]
,
which on input
\hat{s}\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
returns
\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{msk}_{i},\hat{s})
.
The adversary can make a query on any input at any timing arbitrarily many times, until
\mathpzc{A}
receives the challenge at Step
7
.

3.

At some point,
\mathpzc{A}
chooses arbitrary
x_{1},...,x_{n}\in\mathcal{X}
and sends them to the challenger.

4.

For
i\in[n]
, the challenger runs
\mathsf{key}_{i}(x_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{msk}_{i},x_{i})
and sends
\{\mathsf{key}_{i}(x_{i})\}_{i\in[n]}
to the adversary.

5.

At some point,
\mathpzc{A}
declares that it is ready for the challenge.

6.

The challenger picks uniformly random
s_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}
for
i\in[n]
and sends
\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

7.

\mathpzc{A}
receives
\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
from the challenger and looses access to the evaluation oracles.
It then outputs
\{t^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
.

8.

The challenger sets the output of the experiment to be
1
(indicating that
\mathpzc{A}
wins) if
t^{\prime}_{i}=\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{msk}_{i},s_{i})
for all
i\in[n]
and otherwise outputs
0
.

We define the advantage of a QPT adversary
\mathcal{A}
to be

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{WUPF},\mathpzc{A},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{pre}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\Pr[\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{WUPF},\mathpzc{A},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{pre}}(1^{\lambda})=1].

(69)

We also define parallel mark extraction experiment defined by a QPT extractor
\mathpzc{Ext}
, an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
, and a challenger, parameterized by an arbitrary polynomial
n=\text{poly}(\lambda)
, denoted as
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{WUPF},\mathpzc{A},\mathpzc{Ext},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ext}}(1^{\lambda})
.
The parallel extraction experiment is the same as parallel inversion experiment except for the last 3 steps. Namely, we replace Step
6
,
7
, and
8
with the following:

6’

Take the adversary
\mathpzc{A}
right before Step
6
of
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{WUPF},\mathpzc{A},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{pre}}(1^{\lambda})
.

Since
\mathpzc{A}
is a quantum non-uniform QPT adversary, we can
parse
\mathpzc{A}
as a pair of a unitary circuit
U{A}
and a quantum state
\rho{A}
, where to run
\mathpzc{A}
on the inputs
\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
,
we apply
U{A}
to
\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
and
\rho{A}
and then measure the corresponding registers to obtain the output
\{t^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
.

7’

We run
\mathpzc{Ext}(\{\mathsf{xk}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]},(U{A},\rho{A}))\to\{x^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
.

8’

The experiment outputs
1
(indicating that the adversary wins) if
x^{\prime}_{i}=x_{i}
for all
i\in[n]
and
0
otherwise.

We define the advantage of a QPT adversary
\mathcal{A}
with respect to
\mathpzc{Ext}
to be

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{WUPF},\mathpzc{A},\mathpzc{Ext},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ext}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\Pr[\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{WUPF},\mathpzc{A},\mathpzc{Ext},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ext}}(1^{\lambda})=1].

(70)

We say that
\mathsf{WUPF}
satisfies parallel mark extractability if there exists a QPT algorithm
\mathpzc{Ext}
such that for all
n={\mathrm{poly}}(\lambda)
, for all QPT algorithm
\mathcal{A}
,
the following holds:

\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{WUPF},\mathpzc{A},\mathpzc{Ext},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ext}}(\lambda)\geq\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{WUPF},\mathpzc{A},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{pre}}(\lambda)-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(71)

Remark
3.2 (Public extraction v.s. secret extraction).

In our definition, unlike in watermarkable PKE in
Section

3.1
, the extraction key
\mathsf{xk}
must be kept hidden from the adversary.
This is often referred to as “secret extraction" in the watermarking literature and suffices for our purpose.
While secret extraction would suffice also for PKE, we chose to formalize it with the public extraction since our construction satisfies it.

Remark
3.3 (Security as a plain UPF).

We note that the above definition of a watermarkable UPF does not necessarily imply the standard notion of unpredictability as a plain UPF.
Nevertheless, unpredictability can be added by a simple conversion: run a plain UPF in parallel and append its output alongside the original one.
The marked key of the resulting scheme consists of that of the original one along with the secret key of the plain UPF.

We also introduce the notion of equivocality. This property will be used for the construction of watermarkable digital signatures using watermarkable UPF.

Equivocality:

We define the equivocality experiment involving an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
, a equivocation simulation algorithm
\mathsf{Sim}
, and a challenger, denoted as
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathsf{WUPF},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{equiv}}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{coin})
.

1.

The challenger runs
\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow(\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{xk})
if
\mathsf{coin}=0
and
\mathsf{Sim}(1^{\lambda})\to\mathsf{key}^{*}
if
\mathsf{coin}=1
.

2.

The adversary
\mathpzc{A}
is given input
1^{\lambda}
and starts to run.
Throughout the game, it has an oracle access to an oracle
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
, which takes as input
s\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
and works as follows:

\mathcal{O}(s)=\begin{cases}\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{msk},s)&\text{if }\mathsf{coin}=0\\
\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{key}^{*},s),&\text{if }\mathsf{coin}=1\\
\end{cases}

(72)

3.

At some point,
\mathpzc{A}
chooses arbitrary
x\in\mathcal{X}
.

4.

If
\mathsf{coin}=0
, the challenger runs
\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{msk},x)\rightarrow\mathsf{key}(x)
and return
\mathsf{key}(x)
to
\mathpzc{A}
.
If
\mathsf{coin}=1
, the challenger returns
\mathsf{key}^{*}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

5.

The adversary continues to have access to the oracle.
Finally, it outputs its guess
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
for
\mathsf{coin}
. The output of the experiment is then set to be
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
.

We say that
\mathsf{WUPF}
has equivocality if there exists a classical PPT algorithm
\mathsf{Sim}
such that for all QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{WUPF},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{equiv}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\absolutevalue{\Pr[\expa{\WPRF,\qA}{equiv} (1^\secp,0) = 1] - \Pr[\expa{\WPRF,\qA}{equiv} (1^\secp,1) = 1] }\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(73)

Remark
3.4.

We can see that the equivocality implies the correctness against adversarially chosen mark and input, since if the adversary is able to find
x
and
s
such that
\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{msk},s)\neq\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{key}(x),s)
with non-negligible probability, these can be used for distinguishing the case of
\mathsf{coin}=0
and
\mathsf{coin}=1
in the equivocality experiment.
On the other hand, we do not know whether the correctness against adversarially chosen mark and input implies the correctness,
since the latter requires the equation to hold for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
simultaneously, which is somewhat strong condition.

Construction

The above defined watermarkable UPF can be constructed from any two-key equivocal PRF
\mathsf{TEPRF}=\mathsf{TEPRF}.(\mathsf{KG},\mathsf{Eval})
, which in turn can be constructed from any OWF. (See
Definition

2.3
for the definition of two-key equivocal PRF.)
Let
\ell
be the bit-length of the inputs for
\mathsf{TEPRF}
.
We assume
\ell=\omega(\log\lambda)
.
The bit-length of the marks for the construction can be set to an arbitrary polynomial
w=w(\lambda)
.
The bit-length of the input of the construction then becomes
\ell w
.

\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow(\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{xk})
:

On input the security parameter
1^{\lambda}
, the key generation algorithm proceeds as follows.

•

Sample
s^{*}_{1},s^{*}_{2},...,s^{*}_{w}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}

and
c\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{w}
.

•

Run
\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda},s^{*}_{j})\rightarrow(\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{j,0},\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{j,1})
for
j\in[w]
.

•

Output
\mathsf{msk}=(c,\{\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{j,b}\}_{j\in[w],b\in\{0,1\}})

and
\mathsf{xk}=(c,\{s^{*}_{j}\}_{j\in[w]},\{\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{j,b}\}_{j\in[w],b\in\{0,1\}})
.

\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{msk},x)\rightarrow\mathsf{key}(x)
:

Upon input the master secret key
\mathsf{msk}=(c,\{\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{j,b}\}_{j\in[w],b\in\{0,1\}})
and a mark
x\in\{0,1\}^{w}
, it outputs

\mathsf{key}(x)=\{\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{j,x[j]\oplus c[j]}\}_{j\in[w]}
where
x[j]
and
c[j]
are the
j
-th bit of
x
and
c
respectively.

\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{key}(x),s)\rightarrow t
:

On input the marked key
\mathsf{key}(x)
or the master secret key
\mathsf{msk}
, the evaluation algorithm proceeds as follows.

•

If the first input is a master secret key, parse it as
\mathsf{msk}=(c,\{\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{j,b}\}_{j\in[w],b\in\{0,1\}})
. Then, set
\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}^{\prime}_{j}=\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{j,0}
for all
j\in[w]
.

•

If the first input is a marked key
\mathsf{key}(x)
, parse it as
\mathsf{key}(x)=\{\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}^{\prime}_{j}\}_{j\in[\ell]}
.

•

Parse the input as
s=s_{1}\|s_{2}\|\ldots\|s_{w}
.

•

For
j\in[w]
, compute
t_{j}\leftarrow\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}^{\prime}_{j},s_{j})
, where each block is of length
\ell
. Then, output
t=t_{1}\|t_{2}\|\ldots\|t_{w}
.

Correctness

For an input
s=s_{1}\|\cdots\|s_{w}
, unless
s_{i}=s^{*}_{i}
for some
i\in[w]
, it holds that
\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{key}(x),s)=\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{msk},s)
for all
x
. The event
s_{i}=s^{*}_{i}
for some
i
occurs with probability
2^{-\ell}
, which is negligible if
\ell=\omega(\log\lambda)
.

Security Proof

The following theorem asserts the parallel mark extractability of the above construction.

Theorem 3.5.

The above construction satisfies parallel mark extractability if
w(\lambda)=\omega(\log\lambda)
and
\mathsf{TEPRF}
satisfies the different values on target input property and the differing point hiding property.

Before presenting the proof, we briefly outline the intuition.
Our construction builds on
[
KMY25
]
, with the main difference that we introduce “equivocating bits”
c
to simulate evaluation oracles.
The core idea for simulating the evaluation oracle is to evaluate the function using the marked key instead of the master secret key, where the latter is not available to the reduction.
This approach seems to work, since the adversary cannot issue evaluation queries on points that would distinguish the simulated oracle from the real one by the differing point hiding property of the underlying
\mathsf{TEPRF}
.
A difficulty arises, however, because the marks are determined by the adversary through its evaluation queries.
If we follow the naive strategy, this makes it impossible to simulate the evaluation oracle before the marks are fixed.
To overcome this, we employ the “equivocating bits”
c
.
They allow us to use
\{\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{i,j,c^{*}_{i}[j]}\}_{j\in[w]}
for random
c^{*}
as if it were a marked key, even before the actual mark
x
is chosen.
Once
x
is fixed, we can equivocate by interpreting
c^{*}
as
c\oplus x
.

Proof.

To prove the theorem, we first define the extraction algorithm
\mathpzc{Ext}
as follows.

\mathpzc{Ext}(\{\mathsf{xk}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]},(U{A},\rho{A}))\to\{x^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
:

Given the extraction keys
\{\mathsf{xk}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
and the description of the adversary
\mathpzc{A}=(U{A},\rho{A})
, it proceeds as follows.

•

Parse the extraction key as

\mathsf{xk}_{i}=(c_{i},s^{*}_{i}=\{s^{*}_{i,j}\}_{j\in[w]},\{\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{i,j,b}\}_{j\in[w],b\in\{0,1\}})
for each
i\in[n]
,
where
c_{i}\in\{0,1\}^{w}
,
s^{*}_{i,j}\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
.

•

Run the adversary
\mathpzc{A}
on input
(s^{*}_{1},\ldots,s^{*}_{n})
(i.e., run the unitary
U{A}
on input
((s^{*}_{1},\ldots,s^{*}_{n}),\rho{A})
and measure the output register) to obtain
t^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,t^{\prime}_{n}
.

•

Define
x^{\prime}_{i}=x^{\prime}_{i}[1]\|\cdots\|x^{\prime}_{i}[w]
as

\displaystyle x^{\prime}_{i}[j]=d_{i}[j]\oplus c_{i}[j],\quad\mbox{where}\quad d_{i}[j]=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }t^{\prime}_{i,j}=\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{i,j,0},s^{*}_{i,j})\\
1&\text{otherwise }.\end{cases}

(74)

•

Output
\{x^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
.

We then prove the theorem by a sequence of hybrids.
For the sake of the contradiction, let us consider an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
against the parallel prediction game
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{WUPF},\mathpzc{A},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{pre}}(1^{\lambda})
.
In the following, the event that
\mathpzc{A}
wins in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{\rm xx}
is denoted by
\mathsf{E}_{\rm xx}
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
:

This is the parallel predictabilion game between the challenger and an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
.
Namely, the game proceeds as follows.

1.

The challenger runs
\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})
to obtain
(\mathsf{msk}_{i},\mathsf{xk}_{i})

for
i\in[n]
as follows.

•

Sample
s^{*}_{i,1},s^{*}_{i,2},...,s^{*}_{i,w}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}

and
c_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{w}
.

•

Run
\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda},s^{*}_{i,j})\rightarrow(\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{i,j,0},\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{i,j,1})
for
j\in[w]
.

•

Set
\mathsf{msk}_{i}=(c_{i},\{\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{i,j,b}\}_{j\in[w],b\in\{0,1\}})

and
\mathsf{xk}_{i}=(c_{i},\{s^{*}_{i,j}\}_{j\in[w]},\allowbreak\{\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{i,j,b}\}_{j\in[w],b\in\{0,1\}})
.

2.

The adversary
\mathpzc{A}
is given input
1^{\lambda}
and
1^{n}
and starts to run.
Throughout the game, the adversary has an oracle access to the function
\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{msk}_{i},\cdot)
, until it receives the challenge inputs.
Recall that given an input
\hat{s}=\hat{s}_{1}\|\hat{s}_{2}\|\ldots\|\hat{s}_{w}
,
\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{msk}_{i},\hat{s})
computes

\displaystyle\hat{t}_{j}=\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{i,j,0},\hat{s}_{j})

(75)

for
j\in[w]
and returns
\hat{t}=\hat{t}_{1}\|\hat{t}_{2}\|\ldots\|\hat{t}_{w}
.

3.

At some point,
\mathpzc{A}
chooses arbitrary
x_{1},...,x_{n}\in\mathcal{X}
and sends them to the challenger.

4.

For
i\in[n]
, the challenger sets
\mathsf{key}_{i}=\{\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{i,j,x_{i}[j]\oplus c_{i}[j]}\}_{j\in[w]}
and sends
\{\mathsf{key}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
to the adversary.

5.

At some point, the adversary declares that it is ready for the challenge.

6.

The challenger picks uniformly random
s_{i}=s_{i,1}\|\cdots\|s_{i,w}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell w}
for
i\in[n]
and sends
\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
to the adversary.

7.

Receiving
\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
from the challenger,
\mathpzc{A}

outputs
\{t^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
.
Note that after receiving
\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
from the challenger,
\mathpzc{A}
looses access to the oracles.

8.

The challenger sets the output of the game to be
1
(indicating that the adversary wins) if
t^{\prime}_{i}=t_{i}
for all
i\in[n]
and otherwise outputs
0
, where we define
t_{i}=\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{msk}_{i},s_{i})
.
By the definition of
\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{msk}_{i},s_{i})
,

t_{i}=t_{i,1}\|t_{i,2}\|\ldots\|t_{i,w}\quad\mbox{where}\quad t_{i,j}=\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{i,j,0},s_{i,j}).

(76)

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
:

In this hybrid, we change the game so that the challenger does not choose
c_{i}

at Step
1
any more.
Therefore,
\mathsf{msk}_{i}
and
\mathsf{xk}_{i}
are undefined.
Instead,
c^{*}_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{w}
is chosen in Step
1
and
\mathsf{key}_{i}
is defined as

\mathsf{key}_{i}=\{\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{i,j,c^{*}_{i}[j]}\}_{j\in[w]}
in
Step
4
.
We can see that
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
is equivalent to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
if
c^{*}_{i}
is replaced with
c^{*}_{i}=c_{i}\oplus x_{i}
.
This change is a conceptual and thus we have
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{1}]=\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{0}]
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
:

In this hybrid, we change the winning condition of the adversary. Namely, in Step
8
, the challenger outputs
1
if

t^{\prime}_{i}=t_{i}
for all
i\in[n]
, where we now define
t_{i}
as
t_{i}=\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{key}_{i},s_{i})
.
By the definition of
\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{key}_{i},s_{i})
, we have

t_{i}=t_{i,1}\|t_{i,2}\|\ldots\|t_{i,w}\quad\mbox{where}\quad t_{i,j}=\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{i,j,c^{*}_{i}[j]},s_{i,j}).

(77)

By the equality of
\mathsf{TEPRF}
, the value of
t_{i,j}
is different from the one in the previous hybrid only when
c^{*}_{i}[j]=1
and
s_{i,j}=s^{*}_{i,j}
.
However, the latter occurs only with negligible probability since
s_{i,j}
is chosen uniformly at random, independently from anything else.
We therefore have
|\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{1}]-\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{2}]|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
:

In this hybrid, we change the way the evaluation oracles answer the queries.
Namely, in this hybrid, the
i
-th evaluation oracle on input
\hat{s}=\hat{s}_{1}\|\hat{s}_{2}\|\ldots\|\hat{s}_{w}
computes
\hat{t}_{j}
for
j\in[w]
as

\hat{t}_{j}=\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{i,j,c^{*}_{i}[j]},\hat{s}_{j})

(78)

instead of as
Equation

75
, and returns

\hat{t}=\hat{t}_{1}\|\hat{t}_{2}\|\ldots\|\hat{t}_{w}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

We claim that this hybrid is indistinguishable from the previous hybrid.
To see this, let us observe that
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
differ only when
\mathpzc{A}
makes an evaluation query for
\hat{s}
with
\hat{s}_{j}=s^{*}_{i,j}
to the
i
-th evaluation oracle for some
i\in[n]
and
j\in[w]
.
However, we can show that such an event occurs only with negligible probability by a straightforward reduction to the hard to find differing input property of
\mathsf{TEPRF}
, by noting that we only need
\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{i,j,c^{*}_{i,j}}
for each
i,j
for simulating
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
(i.e.,
\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{i,j,1-c^{*}_{i,j}}
is not needed).
We therefore have
|\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{2}]-\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{3}]|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
:

In this hybrid, we set the challenge inputs to be
s^{*}_{i}=s^{*}_{i,1}\|s^{*}_{i,2}\|\cdots\|s^{*}_{i,w}
for
i\in[n]
at Step
6
.
Accordingly, to check the winning condition in Step
8
,
t_{i}
is set as
t_{i}=\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{key}_{i},s^{*}_{i})
for
i\in[n]
.
Note that
s_{i,1},s_{i,2},...,s_{i,w}
are no longer necessary to run the game.

We claim that this hybrid is indistinguishable from the previous hybrid.
This can be shown by a reduction to the differing-point-hiding property of
\mathsf{TEPRF}
, where we replace the challenge input for each position of
i,j
one by one.
The reduction starts by choosing random
(s_{i,j}^{0},s^{1}_{i,j},c^{*}_{i}[j])
and submitting this to its challenger and is given
\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{i,j,c^{*}_{i}[j]}
, where the differing point is either
s_{i,j}^{0}
or
s^{1}_{i,j}
.
The reduction uses
s_{i,j}^{1}
as a challenge input.
In the case
\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{i,j,c^{*}_{i}[j]}
is generated using
s_{i,j}^{0}
, the challenge input for the position is simulated as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
(i.e., the differing point and challenge input are independent for the position), while
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
(i.e., the differing point and challenge input are the same) otherwise.
The rest of the reduction is straightforward, since
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
can be run only using
\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{i,j,c^{*}_{i}[j]}
for each
i,j
.
We therefore have
|\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{3}]-\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{4}]|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}
:

In this hybrid, we switch the way the evaluation oracles are answered to the original one. Namely, on input
\hat{s}
, the
i
-th evaluation oracle computes
\hat{t}_{j}
as
Equation

75
again.

Similarly to the case of
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
, we can show that this hybrid is indistinguishable from the previous hybrid by the hard to find differing point property of
\mathsf{TEPRF}
.
Here again, we have to bound the probability that
\mathpzc{A}
makes an evaluation query for
\hat{s}
with
\hat{s}_{j}=s^{*}_{i,j}
for some
j\in[w]
. One additional subtlety that arises here is that the reduction algorithm does not know
s^{*}_{i,j}
and thus it cannot simulate the challenge input for the adversary.
However, this is not a problem because it is sufficient for the reduction to simulate the game right before the challenge input is given to
\mathpzc{A}
, since the evaluation queries are made only before that.
We therefore have
|\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{5}]-\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{6}]|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{7}
:

In this hybrid, we undo the changes introduced in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
.
Namely, the challenger chooses
c_{i}
for
i\in[n]
in Step
1
again. The challenger then sets
\mathsf{key}_{i}=\{\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{i,j,x_{i}[j]\oplus c_{i}[j]}\}_{j\in[w]}
for
i\in[w]
in Step
4
and uses it for checking the winning condition in Step
8
.
We can easily see that this is just a conceptual change and thus we have

\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{7}]=\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{6}]
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{8}
:

In this hybrid, we further change the winning condition in Step
8
again.
Namely, from the output
\{t^{\prime}_{i}=t^{\prime}_{i,1}\|\cdots\|t^{\prime}_{i,w}\}_{i\in[n]}
of
\mathpzc{A}
, it computes

\{x^{\prime}_{i}=x^{\prime}_{i}[1]\|\cdots\|x^{\prime}_{i}[w]\}_{i\in[n]}
as follow:

\displaystyle x^{\prime}_{i}[j]=d_{i}[j]\oplus c_{i}[j],\quad\mbox{where}\quad d_{i}[j]=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }t^{\prime}_{i,j}=\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{i,j,0},s^{*}_{i,j})\\
1&\text{otherwise }.\end{cases}

(79)

Then, the challenger sets the output of the game to be
1
if
x^{\prime}_{i}=x_{i}

for all
i\in[n]
.

We claim that this change does not reduce the winning probability of
\mathpzc{A}
.
To see this, we recall that when the adversary wins the game in the previous hybrid,
t^{\prime}_{i,j}=\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{i,j,x_{i}[j]\oplus c_{i}[j]},s^{*}_{i,j})
holds for all
i
and
j
.
In such a case, we have
x^{\prime}_{i}=x_{i}
for all
i
, since we have
d_{i}[j]=x_{i}[j]\oplus c_{i}[j]
by the different values on target property of
\mathsf{TEPRF}
.
We therefore have
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{8}]\geq\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{7}]
.

From the above discussion, we can see that
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{8}]\geq\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{0}]-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
holds.
Furthermore, one can see by inspection that
\mathsf{Hyb}_{8}
corresponds to the parallel extraction experiment
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{WUPF},\mathpzc{A},\mathpzc{Ext},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ext}}(\lambda)
for
\mathpzc{A}
with respect to
\mathpzc{Ext}
we defined.
The theorem therefore follows.
∎

We also prove the equivocality of the construction.

Theorem 3.6.

The above construction satisfies equivocality if
\mathsf{TEPRF}
satisfies hard to find differing point property.

Proof.

To prove the theorem, we first define the equivocation simulator
\mathsf{Sim}
as follows.

\mathsf{Sim}(1^{\lambda})\to\mathsf{key}^{*}
:

Given the security parameter
1^{\lambda}
, it proceeds as follows.

•

Sample
s^{*}_{1},s^{*}_{2},\ldots,s^{*}_{w}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}
and
c^{*}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{w}
.

•

Run
\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda},s^{*}_{j})\rightarrow(\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{j,0},\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{j,1})
for
j\in[w]
.

•

Set
\mathsf{key}^{*}=\{\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{j,c^{*}[j]}\}_{j\in[w]}
.

We then prove the theorem by a sequence of hybrids.
For the sake of the contradiction, let us consider an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
against the equivocality game
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathsf{WUPF},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{equiv}}(1^{\lambda})
.
In the following, the event that
\mathpzc{A}
outputs
1
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{\rm xx}
is denoted by
\mathsf{E}_{\rm xx}
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
:

This is the equivocality game between the challenger and an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
with
\mathsf{coin}=0
.
Namely, the game proceeds as follows.

1.

The challenger runs
\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})
to obtain
(\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{xk})
as follows.

•

Sample
s^{*}_{1},s^{*}_{2},\ldots,s^{*}_{w}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}
and
c\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{w}
.

•

Run
\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda},s^{*}_{j})\rightarrow(\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{j,0},\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{j,1})
for
j\in[w]
.

•

Set
\mathsf{msk}=(c,\{\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{j,b}\}_{j\in[w],b\in\{0,1\}})
and
\mathsf{xk}=(c,\{s^{*}_{j}\}_{j\in[w]})
.

2.

The adversary
\mathpzc{A}
is given input
1^{\lambda}
and starts to run.
Throughout the game, the adversary has an oracle access to the function
\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)
.
Recall that given an input
\hat{s}=\hat{s}_{1}\|\hat{s}_{2}\|\ldots\|\hat{s}_{w}
,
\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{msk},\hat{s})
computes

\displaystyle\hat{t}_{j}=\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{j,0},\hat{s}_{j})

(80)

for
j\in[w]
and returns
\hat{t}=\hat{t}_{1}\|\hat{t}_{2}\|\ldots\|\hat{t}_{w}
.

3.

At some point,
\mathpzc{A}
chooses arbitrary
x\in\mathcal{X}
and sends it to the challenger.

4.

The challenger sets
\mathsf{key}=\{\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{j,x[j]\oplus c[j]}\}_{j\in[w]}
and sends
\mathsf{key}
to the adversary.

5.

The adversary continues to have access to the oracle.
Finally, it outputs its guess
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
for
\mathsf{coin}
. The output of the experiment is then set to be
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
:

In this hybrid, we choose
c^{*}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{w}
at Step
1
of the game. Furthermore, at Step
4
of the game, the challenger returns
\mathsf{key}=\{\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{j,c^{*}[j]}\}_{j\in[w]}
to the adversary.
We can see that this change is conceptual, since no information of
c
is revealed except for Step
4
of the game in the previous hybrid and thus
c
effectively works as a one-time pad.
We therefore have
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{1}]=\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{0}]
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
:

In this hybrid, we change the evaluation oracle so that
\hat{t}_{j}
for
j\in[w]
is defined as

\hat{t}_{j}=\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{j,c^{*}[j]},\hat{s}_{j}).

(81)

We claim that this change is unnoticed by
\mathpzc{A}
except for a negligible probability. To see this, we first observe that by the equality of
\mathsf{TEPRF}
, the response from the oracle in this hybrid differs from that of the previous game only when the
\mathpzc{A}
queries on input
\hat{s}
such that
\hat{s}_{j}=s^{*}_{j}
for some
j\in[w]
.
However, we can show that such an event occurs only with negligible probability by a straightforward reduction to the hard to find differing input property of
\mathsf{TEPRF}
, by noting that we only need
\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{j,c^{*}[j]}
for each
j
for simulating
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
(i.e.,
\mathsf{TEPRF}.\mathsf{key}_{j,1-c^{*}[j]}
is not needed).
We therefore have
|\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{1}]-\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{2}]|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

We can observe that
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
equals to the equivocality game between the challenger and an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
with
\mathsf{coin}=1
.
Furthermore, from the above discussion, we know
|\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{0}]-\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{2}]|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
. Therefore, the theorem follows.
∎

3.3
Watermarkable Digital Signatures

The definition of watermarkable digital signatures (DS) is very similar to that of watermarkable PKE in
Section

3.1
; if
an adversary is given a marked signing key
\mathsf{sigk}(x)
and generates a (possibly quantum) "signer" that can sign on uniformly random message with probability
\epsilon
, then we can extract
x
from the signer with probability
\epsilon-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
, and moreover the extraction should work even in the parallel setting.
We generalize the idea of
[
KMY25
]

and show a generic construction of watermarkable DS from coherently signable constrained signatures and the watermarkable UPF, which in turn can be based on the SIS assumption.
Similarly to the case of watermarkable UPF, we must also account for evaluation queries in the security proof, a scenario not addressed in
[
KMY25
]
.
This introduces some technicalities in the proof, which can be resolved by exploiting the equivocality property of watermarkable PRF.

Syntax

A watermarkable DS scheme
\mathsf{WDS}=(\mathsf{KG},\mathsf{Mark},\mathsf{Sign},\mathsf{Vrfy})
with the message space
\mathcal{M}=\{\mathcal{M}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda}
and the mark space
\mathcal{X}=\{\mathcal{X}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda}
consists of the following algorithms.
In the following, we omit the security parameter
\lambda
for
\mathcal{X}_{\lambda}
and
\ell(\lambda)
to denote
\mathcal{X}
and
\ell
when it is clear from the context.

\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{xk})
:

The key generation algorithm is a PPT algorithm that takes a security parameter
1^{\lambda}
, and outputs a verification key
\mathsf{vk}
, master secret key
\mathsf{msk}
, and an extraction key
\mathsf{xk}
.

\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{msk},x)\rightarrow\mathsf{sigk}(x)
:

The marking algorithm is a PPT algorithm that takes a master secret key
\mathsf{msk}
and a mark
x\in\mathcal{X}
, and outputs a marked signing key
\mathsf{sigk}(x)
.

\mathsf{Sign}(\mathsf{sigk}(x),m)\rightarrow\sigma
:

The signing algorithm is a PPT algorithm that takes a master secret key
\mathsf{msk}
or marked signing key
\mathsf{sigk}(x)
and a message
m\in\mathcal{M}
, and outputs a signature
\sigma
.

\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},m,\sigma)\rightarrow\top/\bot
:

The verification algorithm is a PPT algorithm that takes a verification key
\mathsf{vk}
, a message
m\in\mathcal{M}
, and a signature
\sigma
, and outputs
\top
or
\bot
.

Correctness:

For every
x\in\mathcal{X}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},m,\sigma)=\top\\
\qquad\quad\wedge\\
\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},m,\sigma^{\prime})=\top\end{array}\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{xk})\leftarrow\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})\\
\mathsf{sigk}(x)\leftarrow\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{msk},x)\\
\sigma\leftarrow\mathsf{Sign}(\mathsf{sigk}(x),m)\\
\sigma^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{Sign}(\mathsf{msk},m)\end{array}\right]=1-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Coherent signability

We say that a watermarkable DS scheme is coherently-signable if for any polynomial
L=L(\lambda)
, there is a QPT algorithm
\mathpzc{QSign}
that takes a quantum state
\ket{\psi}
and a classical message
m\in\mathcal{M}
and outputs a quantum state
\ket{\psi'}
and a signature
\sigma
, satisfying the following:

1.

For any family
\{x_{z}\in\mathcal{X}\}_{z\in\{0,1\}^{L}}
, for any

z\in\{0,1\}^{L}
,

m\in\mathcal{M}
,
(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{xk})\in\mathrm{Supp}(\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda}))
, and
\mathsf{sigk}(x_{z})\in\mathrm{Supp}(\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{msk},x_{z}))
, the output distribution of
\mathpzc{QSign}(\ket{z}\ket{\sigk(x_z)},m)
is identical to that of
\mathsf{Sign}(\mathsf{sigk}(x_{z}),m)
.

2.

For any families
\{\alpha_{z}\in\mathbb{C}\}_{z\in\{0,1\}^{L}}
such that
\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{L}}|\alpha_{z}|^{2}=1
and
\{x_{z}\in\mathcal{X}\}_{z\in\{0,1\}^{L}}
and for any
m\in\mathcal{M}
, the following holds:
For overwhelming probability over the choice of

(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{xk})\leftarrow\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})
and for all
\mathsf{sigk}(x_{z})\in\mathrm{Supp}(\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{msk},x_{z}))
, we have

\displaystyle\|\ket{\psi}\bra{\psi}-\ket{\psi'}\bra{\psi'}\|_{tr}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda),\quad\mbox{where}\quad\ket{\psi}=\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{L}}\alpha_{z}\ket{z}\ket{\sigk(x_z)},\quad(\ket{\psi'},\sigma)\leftarrow\mathpzc{QSign}(\ket{\psi},m).

(89)

Remark
3.7.

The coherent-signability is required so that the DS-SKL scheme we construct from the watermarkable DS has reusability of quantum signing key, which states that the quantum signing key can be repeatedly used to sign on multiple messages.

Parallel mark extractability:

Here, we introduce the notion of “parallel mark extractability", which is similar to that for watermarkable PKE.
We first define the following parallel forgery experiment involving an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
and a challenger, parameterized by an arbitrary polynomial
n=\text{poly}(\lambda)
, denoted as
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{WDS},\mathpzc{A},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{for}}(1^{\lambda})
.

1.

The challenger runs
\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow(\mathsf{vk}_{i},\mathsf{msk}_{i},\mathsf{xk}_{i})
for
i\in[n]
and
sends
\{\mathsf{vk}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

2.

Throughout the game,
\mathpzc{A}
has an oracle access to the signing oracle
\mathsf{Sign}(\mathsf{msk}_{i},)
for each
i\in[n]
, until it receives the challenge messages in Step
7
.
When queried on input
\hat{m}\in\mathcal{M}
, the oracles
\mathsf{Sign}(\mathsf{msk}_{i},)
runs
\mathsf{Sign}(\mathsf{msk}_{i},\hat{m})\to\hat{\sigma}
and returns
\hat{\sigma}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

3.

At some point, the adversary sends
x_{1},...,x_{n}\in\mathcal{X}
to the challenger.

4.

The challenger computes the marked signing key
\mathsf{sigk}_{i}(x_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{msk}_{i},x_{i})
for
i\in[n]
and sends
\{\mathsf{sigk}_{i}(x_{i})\}_{i\in[n]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

5.

After receiving the marked signing keys,
\mathpzc{A}
continues to make queries to the signing oracles.
At some point, it declares that it is ready for the challenge.

6.

The challenger picks uniformly random
m_{i}\leftarrow\mathcal{M}
for
i\in[n]
and sends
\{m_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

7.

Receiving
\{m_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
from the challenger,
\mathpzc{A}

outputs
\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
.
Note that after receiving
\{m_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
from the challenger,
\mathpzc{A}
looses access to the signing oracles.

8.

The experiment outputs
1
(indicating that
\mathpzc{A}
wins) if
\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},m_{i},\sigma_{i})=\top
for all
i\in[n]
and otherwise outputs
0
.

We define the advantage of a QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}
to be

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{WDS},\mathpzc{A},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{for}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\Pr[\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{WDS},\mathpzc{A},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{for}}(1^{\lambda})=1].

(90)

We also define parallel mark extraction experiment defined by a QPT extractor
\mathpzc{Ext}
, an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
, and a challenger, parameterized by an arbitrary polynomial
n=\text{poly}(\lambda)
, denoted as
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{WDS},\mathpzc{A},\mathpzc{Ext},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ext}}(1^{\lambda})
.
The parallel extraction experiment is the same as parallel inversion experiment except for the last 3 steps. Namely, we replace Step
6
,
7
, and
8
with the following:

6’

Take the adversary
\mathpzc{A}
right before Step
6
of
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{WDS},\mathpzc{A},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{for}}(1^{\lambda})
.
Since
\mathpzc{A}
is a quantum non-uniform QPT adversary, we can
parse
\mathpzc{A}
as a pair of a unitary circuit
U{A}
and a quantum state
\rho{A}
, we can
parse
\mathpzc{A}
as a pair of a unitary circuit
U{A}
and a quantum state
\rho{A}
, where to run
\mathpzc{A}
on the inputs
\{m_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
,
we apply
U{A}
to
\{m_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
and
\rho{A}
and then measure the corresponding registers to obtain the output
\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
.

7’

We run
\mathpzc{Ext}(\{\mathsf{xk}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]},(U{A},\rho{A}))\to\{x^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
.

8’

The experiment outputs
1
(indicating that the adversary wins) if
x^{\prime}_{i}=x_{i}
for all
i\in[n]
and
0
otherwise.

We define the advantage of a QPT adversary
\mathcal{A}
with respect to
\mathpzc{Ext}
to be

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{WDS},\mathpzc{A},\mathpzc{Ext},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ext}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\Pr[\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{WDS},\mathpzc{A},\mathpzc{Ext},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ext}}(1^{\lambda})=1].

(91)

We say that
\mathsf{WDS}
satisfies parallel mark extractability if there exists a QPT algorithm
\mathpzc{Ext}
such that for all
n={\mathrm{poly}}(\lambda)
, for all QPT algorithm
\mathcal{A}
,
the following holds:

\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{WDS},\mathpzc{A},\mathpzc{Ext},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ext}}(\lambda)\geq\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{WDS},\mathpzc{A},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{for}}(\lambda)-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(92)

Remark
3.8.

Similarly to the case of UPF, the above definition only supports secret extraction. However, it is sufficient for our purpose.

Remark
3.9 (Security as a plain DS).

We note that the above definition of a watermarkable DS does not necessarily imply the standard EUF-CMA security as a plain DS.
Nevertheless, the EUF-CMA security can be added by a simple conversion: run a plain DS in parallel and append its signature alongside the original one.
The new verification algorithm verifies both the signatures and accepts only when both are valid.
The marked key of the resulting scheme consists of that of the original one along with the signing key of the plain DS.

Construction

The above defined watermarkable digital signatures can be constructed from any watermarkable UPF and coherently-signable constrained signatures.
Our construction abstracts the core idea of the watermarkable DS scheme implicit in
[
KMY25
]
.
Observe first that UPF can already be viewed as a MAC; what remains is simply to augment it with a public verification algorithm.
To achieve this, we equip the signer with a constrained signing key that allows signatures to be generated only on valid input–output pairs of the UPF.
Consequently, in order to produce a valid signature on a message (treated as an input to the UPF), the signer must also provide a corresponding valid output of the PRF as part of the signature.
This structure enables us to reuse the same extractor as in the UPF setting.

Formally, our construction of watermarkable digital signature scheme
\mathsf{WDS}=(\mathsf{KG},\mathsf{Mark},\mathsf{Sign},\mathsf{Vrfy})
uses watermarkable UPF
\mathsf{WUPF}=\mathsf{WUPF}.(\mathsf{KG},\mathsf{Mark},\mathsf{Eval})
and coherently-signable constrained signatures
\mathsf{CS}=\mathsf{CS}.(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{Constrain},\mathsf{Sign},\mathsf{Vrfy})
(See
Definition

2.5
for the definition of coherently-signable constrained signatures.) as building blocks, both of which in turn can be constructed from SIS.
As additional requirements, we need
\mathsf{WUPF}
to satisfy equivocality and
\mathsf{CS}
to satisfy signing privacy.
Our construction
\mathsf{WDS}
supports mark space
\{0,1\}^{w}
and the message space
\{0,1\}^{\ell}
.
For the construction, we need the underlying
\mathsf{WUPF}
to support the mark space
\{0,1\}^{w}
and the input space
\{0,1\}^{\ell}
.
We denote the bit-length of the output of the
\mathsf{WUPF}
function by
v
.
We then need
\mathsf{CS}
to support the
\mathsf{WUPF}
evaluation circuit defined below,
whose input length is
\ell+v
and the depth is bounded by a fixed polynomial.

\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{xk})
:

On input the security parameter
1^{\lambda}
, the key generation algorithm proceeds as follows.

•

Run
\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})\to(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{xk})
.

•

Run
\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow(\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{msk})
.

•

Output
\mathsf{vk}=\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{vk}
,

\mathsf{msk}=(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{msk})
,
and
\mathsf{xk}=\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{xk}
.

\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{msk},x)\rightarrow\mathsf{sigk}(x)
:

Upon input the master secret key
\mathsf{msk}=(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{msk})
and a mark
x\in\{0,1\}^{w}
, it proceeds as follows.

•

Run
\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk},x)=\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{key}(x)
.

•

Construct a circuit
f[\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{key}(x)]
that takes as input a pair
(m,t)\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}\times\{0,1\}^{v}

and outputs
1
if
\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{key}(x),m)=t
and
0
otherwise.

•

Run
\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{Constrain}(\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{msk},f[\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{key}(x)])\to\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{sigk}(x)
.

•

Output
\mathsf{sigk}(x)=(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{key}(x),\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{sigk}(x))
.

\mathsf{Sign}(\mathsf{sigk}(x),m)\rightarrow\sigma
:

On input the marked key
\mathsf{sigk}(x)
or the master secret key
\mathsf{msk}
and the message
m\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
, the evaluation algorithm proceeds as follows.

•

If the first input is a master secret key, parse it as
\mathsf{msk}=(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{msk})
. Then, set
\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{key}=\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}
and compute

\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{Constrain}(\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{msk},f_{=1})\to\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{sigk}
, where
f_{=1}
is a constant function that always outputs
1
.

•

If the first input is a marked key
\mathsf{sigk}(x)
, parse it as

\mathsf{sigk}(x)=(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{key},\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{sigk})
.

•

Compute
t=\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{key},m)
and
\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{Sign}(\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{sigk},(m,t))\to\mathsf{CS}.\sigma
.

•

Output
\sigma=(t,\mathsf{CS}.\sigma)
.

\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},m,\sigma)
:

On input the message
m
and the signature
\sigma=(t,\mathsf{CS}.\sigma)
, it runs
\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{vk},(m,t),\mathsf{CS}.\sigma)
and outputs whatever it outputs.

Correctness

We first observe that the signature
\sigma=(t,\mathsf{CS}.\sigma)
generated by the master secret key passes the verification with overwhelming probability by the correctness of
\mathsf{CS}
, since
f_{=1}(m,t)=1
for any
m
and
t
.
We then observe that the signature
\sigma=(t,\mathsf{CS}.\sigma)
generated by the signing key
\mathsf{sigk}(x)
also passes the verification
with overwhelming probability by the correctness of
\mathsf{CS}
, since

f[\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{key}(x)](m,t)=1
holds when
t=\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{key}(x),m)
.

Coherent Signability

To show the coherent signability, we first define the quantum signing algorithm
\mathpzc{QSign}
.
It take as input a quantum state
\ket{\psi}
and a message
m
.
We can assume that
\ket{\psi}
is of the form
\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{L}}\alpha_{z}\ket{z}\ket{\sigk(x_z)}=\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{L}}\alpha_{z}\ket{z}\ket{\WPRF.\key(x_z)}\ket{\CS.\sigk(x_z)}
.

1.

Apply the following isometry to
\ket{\psi}
:

\displaystyle\ket{z}\ket{\WPRF.\key(x_z)}\ket{\CS.\sigk(x_z)}\mapsto\ket{z}\ket{\WPRF.\key(x_z)}\ket{\CS.\sigk(x_z)}\ket{\WPRF.\Eval(\WPRF.\key(x_z), m) }.

(93)

2.

Measure the last register and discard it. Let the result of the measurement be
t
and the resulting state after discarding the register be
\ket{\phi}
.

3.

Run
\mathsf{CS}.\mathpzc{QSign}(\ket{\phi},(m,t))\to(\ket{\psi'},\mathsf{CS}.\sigma)
and output
\ket{\psi'}
and
\sigma=(t,\mathsf{CS}.\sigma)
.

We first show that the above defined algorithm satisfies the first property of coherent signability, which states that applying
\mathpzc{QSign}
to a classical message results in a signature distributed identically to when
\mathsf{Sign}
is applied.
This straightforwardly follows from the fact that
\mathsf{CS}.\mathpzc{QSign}
equals to
\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{Sign}
when the input is classical.

We then show the second property, which states that the application of
\mathpzc{QSign}
on a superposition of a marked signing key almost does not change the state.
To see this, we first observe that after applying the first step, the state becomes

\displaystyle\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{L}}\alpha_{z}\ket{z}\ket{\WPRF.\key(x_z)}\ket{\CS.\sigk(x_z)}\ket{\WPRF.\Eval(\WPRF.\key(x_z), m) }

(94)

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{L}}\alpha_{z}\ket{z}\ket{\WPRF.\key(x_z)}\ket{\CS.\sigk(x_z)}\ket{\WPRF.\Eval(\WPRF.\msk, m) },

(95)

where the equation holds with overwhelming probability over the randomness of
\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
from the correctness of
\mathsf{WUPF}
.
14
14

14

Recall that we require somewhat strong evaluation correctness condition for
\mathsf{WUPF}
, which requires that with overwhelming probability over the choice of
\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}
and
\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{xk}
, the evaluation result by the master secret key equals that of the marked key with mark
x

for all

x
.

Therefore, we have
\ket{\phi}=\ket{\psi}
with overwhelming probability.
We next see that the application of
\mathsf{CS}.\mathpzc{QSign}
almost does not change the state
\ket{\phi}
.
To see this, we consider a function family
\{g_{z^{\prime}}\}_{z^{\prime}}
where for
z^{\prime}=(z,\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{key}(x_{z}))
,
g_{z^{\prime}}
is defined as
g_{z^{\prime}}=f[\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{key}(x_{z})]
and apply the coherent signability of
\mathsf{CS}
.
We therefore have that
\ket{\psi'}
is negligibly close to
\ket{\psi}
in trace distance as desired.

Security Proof

The following theorem asserts the parallel mark extractability of the above construction.

Theorem 3.10.

Assume that
\mathsf{WUPF}
satisfies the parallel mark extractability as watermarkable unpredictable function and equivocality and
\mathsf{CS}
satisfies selective single-key security and function privacy.
Then, the above construction satisfies parallel mark extractability as watermarkable digital signatures.

Before presenting the proof, we briefly outline the intuition.
At a high level, one can extract the mark from a signer,
since a signature
\sigma=(t,\mathsf{CS}.\sigma)
contains the
\mathsf{WUPF}
value
t
and the extractor for
\mathsf{WUPF}
can be applied to perform the extraction.
The only way for the adversary to evade the extraction is to produce a signature

(t,\mathsf{CS}.\sigma)
such that
\mathsf{CS}.\sigma
is a valid signature on
(m,t)
,
but
t\neq\mathsf{WUPF}(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{sigk}(x),m)
.
However, generating such a signature constitutes a forgery against
\mathsf{CS}

and is therefore computationally infeasible,
since the adversary only obtains a constrained key that permits signing
on pairs
(m,t)
satisfying
t=\mathsf{WUPF}(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{sigk}(x),m)
.

Turning this intuition into a formal proof is nontrivial,
because the mark is chosen adaptively by the adversary
(whereas in
[
KMY25
]
it is sampled uniformly at random).
In particular, when reducing to selectively single-key forgery,
the reduction must commit to a function
f
at the beginning of the game.
In our setting, however, this function necessarily depends on the mark
x
,
since it hardwires the
\mathsf{WUPF}
key for that mark, which is chosen adaptively by the adversary.
As a result, the reduction cannot commit to the function at the outset.
To overcome this difficulty, we rely on the equivocality of
\mathsf{WUPF}
,
which provides a simulated key that is indistinguishable from a marked key
even before
x
is known.
This simulated key can be chosen independently of
x

and then embedded into the function.

An additional subtlety arises from the presence of a signing oracle,
which is not introduced in
[
KMY25
]
.
For simulating the signing oracle, we use the constrained key of
\mathsf{CS}
, similarly to the case of
\mathsf{WUPF}
.
Here, however, the function hardwired into the constrained key
differs from the one in the real execution,
and thus could be detected by the adversary.
The function privacy of
\mathsf{CS}
guarantees this is not the case and such a difference is hidden from the adversary.

Proof.

To show the proof, we first define the extraction algorithm
\mathpzc{Ext}
as follows.

\mathpzc{Ext}(\{\mathsf{xk}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]},(U{A},\rho{A}))\to\{x^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
:

Given the extraction keys
\{\mathsf{xk}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
and the description of the adversary
\mathpzc{A}=(U{A},\rho{A})
, it proceeds as follows.

•

Parse the extraction key as

\mathsf{xk}_{i}=\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{xk}_{i}
for each
i\in[n]
.

•

From the adversary
\mathpzc{A}
, construct a predictor
\mathpzc{P}=(U{P},\rho{P})
for
\mathsf{WUPF}
as follows.

\mathpzc{P}(m_{1},\ldots,m_{n}):

Given
m_{1},\ldots,m_{n}
, it first runs
\mathpzc{A}
on input
m_{1},\ldots,m_{n}
to obtain
\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
.
It then parses
\sigma_{i}=(t_{i},\mathsf{CS}.\sigma_{i})
for each
i\in[n]
.
It finally outputs
(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
.

•

Run
\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathpzc{Ext}(\{\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{xk}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]},(U{P},\rho{P}))\rightarrow\{x^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
.

•

Output
\{x^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
.

We then prove the theorem by a sequence of hybrids.
Let us consider an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
against the parallel forgery game
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{WDS},\mathpzc{A},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{for}}(1^{\lambda})
.
We gradually change the game into the parallel extraction game
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{WDS},\mathpzc{A},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ext}}(1^{\lambda})
without changing the winning probability of
\mathpzc{A}
more than negligibly.
In the following, the event that
\mathpzc{A}
wins in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{\rm xx}
is denoted by
\mathsf{E}_{\rm xx}
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
:

This is the parallel predictability game between the challenger and an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
.
Namely, the game proceeds as follows.

1.

The challenger runs
\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})
to obtain
\mathsf{vk}_{i}=\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{vk}_{i}
,

\mathsf{msk}_{i}=(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{msk}_{i})
,
and
\mathsf{xk}_{i}=\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{xk}_{i}
for
i\in[n]
.
\mathpzc{A}
is given
\{\mathsf{vk}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
.

2.

Throughout the game, the adversary has an oracle access to the signing oracle
\mathsf{Sign}(\mathsf{msk}_{i},)
for each
i\in[n]
, until it receives the challenge messages in Step
7
.
When queried on input
\hat{m}
, the oracles
\mathsf{Sign}(\mathsf{msk}_{i},)
proceeds as follows:

•

Compute
\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{Constrain}(\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},f_{=1})\to\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{sigk}_{i}
, where
f_{=1}
is a constant function that always outputs
1
.

•

Compute
\hat{t}=\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},\hat{m})
.

•

Run
\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{Sign}(\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{sigk}_{i},(\hat{m},\hat{t}))\to\mathsf{CS}.\hat{\sigma}
.

•

Return
\hat{\sigma}=(\hat{t},\mathsf{CS}.\hat{\sigma})
.

3.

At some point,
\mathpzc{A}
chooses arbitrary
x_{1},...,x_{n}\in\mathcal{X}
and sends them to the challenger.

4.

For
i\in[n]
, the challenger sets the marked signing keys
\mathsf{sigk}_{i}\coloneqq(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{key}_{i}(x_{i}),\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{sigk}_{i}(x_{i}))
and sends
\{\mathsf{sigk}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
to the adversary, where

\displaystyle\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{key}_{i}(x_{i})=\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},x_{i})\text{ and }\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{sigk}_{i}(x_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{Constrain}(\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},f[\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{key}_{i}(x_{i})]).

(96)

5.

After receiving the marked signing keys,
\mathpzc{A}
continues to make queries to the signing oracles.
At some point, it declares that it is ready for the challenge.

6.

The challenger picks uniformly random
m_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}
for
i\in[n]
and sends
\{m_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
to the adversary.

7.

Receiving
\{m_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
from the challenger,
\mathpzc{A}

outputs
\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
.
Note that after receiving
\{m_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
from the challenger,
\mathpzc{A}
looses access to the signing oracles.

8.

Given
\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
, the challenger parses it as
\sigma_{i}=(t_{i},\mathsf{CS}.\sigma_{i})
for
i\in[n]
and proceeds as follows.

•

It checks whether
\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{vk},(m_{i},t_{i}),\mathsf{CS}.\sigma_{i})=\top
holds for all
i\in[n]
.
If it holds, then it sets the output of the game to be
1
(indicating that the adversary wins). Otherwise, it outputs
0
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
:

In this hybrid, we use the simulated key of
\mathsf{WUPF}
to answer signing key queies and to simulate the marked signing keys.
Concretely, we replace Step
1
, Step
2
, and Step
4
with the following:

1’

The challenger runs
\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})\to(\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{vk}_{i},\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{msk}_{i})

and
\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{Sim}(1^{\lambda})\to\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{key}^{*}_{i}
for
i\in[n]
.
Then,
\mathpzc{A}
is given
\{\mathsf{vk}_{i}=\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{vk}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
.

2’

Throughout the game, the adversary has an oracle access to the signing oracles, until it receives the challenge messages in Step
7
.
When queried on input
\hat{m}
, the
i
-th oracle proceeds as follows:

•

Compute
\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{Constrain}(\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},f_{=1})\to\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{sigk}_{i}
, where
f_{=1}
is a constant function that always outputs
1
.

•

Compute

.

•

Run
\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{Sign}(\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{sigk}_{i},(\hat{m},\hat{t}))\to\mathsf{CS}.\hat{\sigma}
.

•

Return
\hat{\sigma}=(\hat{t},\mathsf{CS}.\hat{\sigma})
.

4’

For
i\in[n]
, the challenger sets the marked signing keys
\mathsf{sigk}_{i}\coloneqq(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{key}^{*}_{i},\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{sigk}^{*}_{i})
and sends
\{\mathsf{sigk}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
to the adversary, where

\displaystyle\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{sigk}^{*}_{i}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{Constrain}(\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},f[\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{key}_{i}^{*}]).

(97)

By a straightforward reduction to the equivocality of
\mathsf{WUPF}
, we have
|\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{0}]-\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{1}]|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
:

In this hybrid, we change how we handle signing queries.
We move the sampling of
\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{sigk}^{*}_{i}
as per
Equation

97
to Step
1
, which is possible since it is not dependent on
x_{i}
any more, and then use it for answering the signing queries.
Namely, we replace Step
1’
, Step
2’
, and Step
4’
with the following:

1”

The challenger runs
\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})\to(\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{vk}_{i},\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{msk}_{i})
,

\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{Sim}(1^{\lambda})\to\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{key}^{*}_{i}
, and

\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{sigk}^{*}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{Constrain}(\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},f[\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{key}_{i}^{*}])
for
i\in[n]
.
Then,
\mathpzc{A}
is given
\{\mathsf{vk}_{i}=\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{vk}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
.

2”

Throughout the game, the adversary has an oracle access to the signing oracles, until it receives the challenge messages in Step
7
.
When queried on input
\hat{m}
, the
i
-th oracle proceeds as follows:

•

Compute
\hat{t}=\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{key}^{*}_{i},\hat{m})
.

•

Run
\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{Sign}(\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{sigk}^{*}_{i},(\hat{m},\hat{t}))\to\mathsf{CS}.\hat{\sigma}
.

•

Return
\hat{\sigma}=(\hat{t},\mathsf{CS}.\hat{\sigma})
.

4”

For
i\in[n]
, the challenger sets the marked signing keys
\mathsf{sigk}_{i}\coloneqq(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{key}^{*}_{i},\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{sigk}^{*}_{i})
and sends
\{\mathsf{sigk}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
to the adversary.

We claim that this hybrid is statistically indistinguishable from the previous one, due to the function privacy of
\mathsf{CS}
.
We recall that
\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{sigk}_{i}
in this hybrid encodes a function
f[\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{key}^{*}_{i}]
and thus we have
f[\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{key}^{*}_{i}](\hat{m},\hat{t})=1=f_{=1}(\hat{m},\hat{t})
.
Therefore, the distribution of the returned
\mathsf{CS}.\hat{\sigma}
is statistically close to that of the previous hybrid.
We therefore have
|\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{1}]-\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{2}]|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
:

In this hybrid, we change the winning condition of
\mathpzc{A}
.
Namely, we replace Step
8
with the following, where additional check regarding
t_{i}
is inserted.

8’

Given
\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
, the challenger parses it as
\sigma_{i}=(t_{i},\mathsf{CS}.\sigma_{i})
for
i\in[n]
and proceeds as follows.

•

It checks whether
t_{i}=\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{key}^{*}_{i},m_{i})
holds for all
i\in[n]
. If not, it outputs
0
.

•

It then checks whether
\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{vk},(m_{i},t_{i}),\mathsf{CS}.\sigma_{i})=\top
holds for all
i\in[n]
.
If it holds, then it sets the output of the game to be
1
(indicating that the adversary wins). Otherwise, it outputs
0
.

We claim that this change does not reduce the winning probability of
\mathpzc{A}
more than negligibly due to the selective single key unforgeability of
\mathsf{CS}
.
To see this, we observe that the only case where
\mathpzc{A}
wins the game in the previous hybrid, but does not in the current one occurs when
\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{vk},(m_{i},t_{i}),\mathsf{CS}.\sigma_{i})=\top
holds for all
i\in[n]
,
but there exists
i^{*}\in[n]
such that

t_{i^{*}}\neq\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{key}^{*}_{i^{*}},m_{i^{*}})
.
However, the probability that such an event occurs is negligible due to the selective single-key security of
\mathsf{CS}
.
To see this, we consider a reduction that guesses
i^{*}
and embeds the parameter of the
\mathsf{CS}
into this instance, while the other instances as well as the parameters related to
\mathsf{WUPF}
are generated by itself.
We observe that the reduction algorithm can choose its target function
f[\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{key}^{*}_{i^{*}}]
at the beginning of the game, since
\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{key}^{*}_{i^{*}}
can be chosen by
\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{Sim}(1^{\lambda})
, without depending on any parameter other than the security parameter.
Furthermore, by the changes we introduced so far, all the signing queries made by
\mathpzc{A}
can be handled by the single key
\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{sigk}^{*}_{i^{*}}
.
Finally, since
t_{i^{*}}\neq\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{key}^{*}_{i^{*}},m_{i^{*}})
implies
f[\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{key}^{*}_{i^{*}}](m_{i^{*}},t_{i^{*}})=0
,
the reduction breaks the selective single-key security if such an event occurs.
We therefore have

\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{3}]\geq\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{2}]-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
:

In this hybrid, we switch Step
1”
, Step
2”
, and Step
4”
back to that in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
, i.e., Step
1’
, Step
2’
, and Step
4’
.
In particular, we use
\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{sigk}_{i}
derived as

\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{Constrain}(\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},f_{=1})\to\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{sigk}_{i}
to sign on
(\hat{m},\hat{t})
.
Similarly to the change from
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
, we have
|\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{3}]-\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{4}]|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
by the function privacy of
\mathsf{CS}
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}
:

In this hybrid, we switch Step

, Step

, and Step
4’
back to that in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
, i.e., Step

, Step

, and Step

.
Namely, the simulated key
\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{key}_{i}^{*}
is not generated any more.
Accordingly, we also change Step

so that it uses
\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{key}_{i}(x_{i})
instead of
\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{key}_{i}^{*}
.
Concretely, we replace Step

with the following.

8”

Given
\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
, the challenger parses it as
\sigma_{i}=(t_{i},\mathsf{CS}.\sigma_{i})
for

and proceeds as follows.

•

It checks whether

holds for all

. If not, it outputs
0
.

•

It then checks whether
\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{CS}.\mathsf{vk},(m_{i},t_{i}),\mathsf{CS}.\sigma_{i})=\top
holds for all
i\in[n]
.
If it holds, then it sets the output of the game to be
1
(indicating that the adversary wins). Otherwise, it outputs
0
.

Similarly to the change from
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
, we have

|\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{4}]-\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{5}]|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
by the equivocality of
\mathsf{WUPF}
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}
:

In this hybrid, we further change the winning condition so that it does not check the validity of
\mathsf{CS}
signatures.
Namely, we replace Step
8”
with the following:

8”’

Given
\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
, the challenger parses it as
\sigma_{i}=(t_{i},\mathsf{CS}.\sigma_{i})
for
i\in[n]
and proceeds as follows.

•

It checks whether
t_{i}=\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{key}_{i}(x_{i}),m_{i})
holds for all
i\in[n]
. If not, it outputs
0
.

This change relaxes the winning condition for
\mathpzc{A}
and only increases the chance of it winning. We therefore have
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{6}]\geq\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{5}]
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{7}
:

In this hybrid, we further change the winning condition so that the check of the
\mathsf{WUPF}
values are done using
\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}
.
Namely, we replace Step
8”’
with the following:

8
enumi

Given
\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
, the challenger parses it as
\sigma_{i}=(t_{i},\mathsf{CS}.\sigma_{i})
for
i\in[n]
and proceeds as follows.

•

It checks whether
t_{i}=\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},m_{i})
holds for all
i\in[n]
. If not, it outputs
0
.

By the correctness against adversarially chosen mark and input of
\mathsf{WUPF}
, which is implied by the equivocality, we have
\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},m_{i})=\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}(x_{i}),m_{i})
with overwhelming probability for each
i\in[n]
, even if
m_{i}
was adversarially chosen by
\mathpzc{A}
.
We therefore have
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\event_{7}] - \Pr[\event_{6}]}\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{8}
:

In this hybrid, we further change the winning condition.
Here, we use
\mathpzc{A}
as a predictor for
\mathsf{WUPF}
and then extract the marks using the corresponding extractor of
\mathsf{WUPF}
.
Concretely, we replace Step
6
, Step
7
, and Step
8enumi
with the following:

6
enumienumi

The challenger parses the adversary
\mathpzc{A}
right before Step
7
of the game as a unitary circuit
U{A}
and a quantum state
\rho{A}
.
It then constructs a quantum predictor
\mathpzc{P}
for
\mathsf{WUPF}
that takes as input
\{m_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
as input and outputs
\{t_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}

from
\mathpzc{A}
as follows.

\mathpzc{P}(\{m_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})
:

It runs
\mathpzc{A}
on input
\{m_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
to obtain
\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
.
It then parses

\sigma_{i}=(t_{i},\mathsf{CS}.\sigma_{i})
for
i\in[n]

and outputs
\{t_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
.

7
enumienumi

The challenger parses

\mathpzc{P}
as a pair of unitary
U{P}
and a quantum state
\rho{P}
.
Then, it runs the extraction algorithm as

\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathpzc{Ext}(\{\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{xk}_{i}\}_{i\in S},(U{P},\rho{P}))\to\{x^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in S}
.

8
enumienumi

If
x_{i}=x^{\prime}_{i}
holds for all
i\in S
,
the challenger sets the output of the game to be
1
.
Otherwise, it sets it to be
0
.

Let us construct an adversary
\mathpzc{B}
against the parallel prediction experiment of
\mathsf{WUPF}

by running
\mathpzc{A}
internally.

\mathpzc{B}
samples the parameters related to
\mathsf{CS}
by itself and simulates the computation of
\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},\cdot)
necessary for answering the signing queries from
\mathpzc{A}
by accessing its evaluation oracles.

\mathpzc{B}
then predicts the PRF values by constructing
\mathpzc{P}
from
\mathpzc{A}
as above and running it on the challenge input.
It is straightforward to see that the advantage of
\mathpzc{B}
against parallel predictability game of
\mathsf{WUPF}
is the same as
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{7}]
.
It is also easy to see that the advantage of
\mathpzc{B}
against the parallel mark extraction experiment equals to
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{8}]
.
Therefore, by the parallel mark extractability of
\mathsf{WUPF}
, we have
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{8}]\geq\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{7}]-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

From the above discussion, we can see that
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{8}]\geq\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{0}]-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
holds.
Furthermore, one can see by inspection that
\mathsf{Hyb}_{8}
corresponds to the parallel extraction experiment
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{WDS},\mathpzc{A},\mathpzc{Ext},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ext}}(\lambda)
for
\mathpzc{A}
with respect to
\mathpzc{Ext}
we defined.
The theorem therefore follows.
∎

4
Special Dual-Mode Secure Function Evaluation

In this section, we introduce special dual-mode secure function evaluation (SFE), which is used as a building block of our secure key leasing schemes with classical lessors. We show that special dual-mode SFE exists assuming the LWE assumption.

Below, we define special dual-mode secure SFE.

Definition 4.1 (Special Dual-mode SFE).

A special dual-mode SFE scheme
\mathsf{SFE}
is a tuple of four algorithms
\mathsf{SFE}=\mathsf{SFE}.(\mathsf{CRSGen},\mathsf{Rec}_{1},\allowbreak\mathsf{Send},\mathsf{Rec}_{2})
.
Below, let
\{0,1\}^{w}
be the message space of
\mathsf{SFE}
.

\mathsf{CRSGen}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{mode})\rightarrow(\mathsf{crs},\mathsf{td})
:

The CRS generation algorithm is a PPT algorithm that takes a security parameter
1^{\lambda}
and
\mathsf{mode}\in\{1,2\}
, and outputs a CRS
\mathsf{crs}
and a trapdoor
\mathsf{td}
.
\mathsf{mode}=1
indicates that the system in the “extractable mode", while
\mathsf{mode}=2
indicates “hiding mode".

\mathsf{Rec}_{1}(\mathsf{crs},x)\rightarrow(\mathsf{msg}^{(1)},\mathsf{st})
:

This is a classical PPT algorithm supposed to be run by a receiver to generate the first message of the protocol.
It takes as input the CRS
\mathsf{crs}
and an input
x\in\{0,1\}^{w}
and outputs the first message
\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}
and a secret state
\mathsf{st}
.

\mathsf{Send}(\mathsf{crs},\mathsf{msg}^{(1)},C)\rightarrow\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}
:

This is a classical PPT algorithm supposed to be run by a sender to generate the second message of the protocol.
It takes as input the CRS
\mathsf{crs}
, a message
\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}
from the receiver,
and a circuit
C
with input length
w
and outputs the second message
\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}
.

\mathsf{Rec}_{2}(\mathsf{crs},x,\mathsf{st},\mathsf{msg}^{(2)})\rightarrow y
:

This is a classical PPT algorithm supposed to be run by a receiver to derive the output.
It takes as input the CRS
\mathsf{crs}
, an input
x\in\{0,1\}^{w}
,
a state
\mathsf{st}
, and a message
\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}
from the sender,
and outputs a string
y
.

We require special dual-mode SFE to satisfy the following properties.

Evaluation correctness:

For all
x\in\{0,1\}^{w}
,
\mathsf{mode}\in\{1,2\}
, and a circuit
C
with input length
w
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathsf{Rec}_{2}(\mathsf{crs},x,\mathsf{st},\mathsf{msg}^{(2)})=C(x)\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{crs},\mathsf{td})\leftarrow\mathsf{CRSGen}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{mode})\\
(\mathsf{msg}^{(1)},\mathsf{st})\leftarrow\mathsf{Rec}_{1}(\mathsf{crs},x)\\
\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}\leftarrow\mathsf{Send}(\mathsf{crs},\mathsf{msg}^{(1)},C)\end{array}\right]=1.

Unique state:

For all

\mathsf{mode}\in\{1,2\}
,

(\mathsf{crs},\mathsf{td})\in\mathrm{Supp}(\mathsf{CRSGen}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{mode}))
,
x\in\{0,1\}^{w}
, and
(\mathsf{msg}^{(1)},\mathsf{st})\in\mathrm{Supp}(\mathsf{Rec}_{1}(\mathsf{crs},x))
,
\mathsf{st}
is the unique state that corresponds to

\mathsf{crs}
,
x
, and
\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}
, that is, there is no
\mathsf{st}^{\prime}\neq\mathsf{st}
such that
(\mathsf{msg}^{(1)},\mathsf{st}^{\prime})\in\mathrm{Supp}(\mathsf{Rec}_{1}(\mathsf{crs},x))
.
We denote the unique state
\mathsf{st}
such that
(\mathsf{msg}^{(1)},\mathsf{st})\in\mathrm{Supp}(\mathsf{Rec}_{1}(\mathsf{crs},x))
by
\mathsf{st}_{x\to\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}}
.
15
15

15

The state also depends on
\mathsf{crs}
, but we omit it from the notation.

Mode indistinguishability:

For all QPT algorithm
\mathcal{A}
, we have

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{
\Pr[\cA(\crs)=1: (\crs,\td) \la\CRSGen(1^\secp,1) ]-
\Pr[\cA(\crs)=1: (\crs,\td) \la\CRSGen(1^\secp,2) ]
}\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(101)

Statistical security against malicious senders in the hiding mode:

For all
\mathsf{crs}
output by
\mathsf{CRSGen}(1^{\lambda},2)
and for all
x_{0},x_{1}\in\{0,1\}^{w}
, we require the following statistical indistinguishability:

\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{0}\approx_{s}\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{1}\quad\mbox{where}\quad(\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{b},\mathsf{st})\leftarrow\mathsf{Rec}_{1}(\mathsf{crs},x_{b})\quad\mbox{for}\quad b\in\{0,1\}.

(102)

State recoverability in the hiding mode:

We require that there exists a classical PPT algorithm
\mathsf{StaRcv}
that takes as input the trapdoor
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}
and the first message of the receiver
\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}
and outputs a tuple of strings
\{\alpha_{j}^{b}\}_{j\in[w],b\in\{0,1\}}
or
\bot
, where the latter indicates that the state recovery failed.
We require that for all
x=x[1]\|\cdots\|x[w]\in\{0,1\}^{w}
, the following holds:

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathsf{st}=\alpha_{1}^{x[1]}\|\alpha_{1}^{x[2]}\|\cdots\|\alpha_{w}^{x[w]}\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{crs},\mathsf{td})\leftarrow\mathsf{CRSGen}(1^{\lambda},2)\\
(\mathsf{msg}^{(1)},\mathsf{st})\leftarrow\mathsf{Rec}_{1}(\mathsf{crs},x)\\
\{\alpha_{j}^{b}\}_{j\in[w],b\in\{0,1\}}\leftarrow\mathsf{StaRcv}(\mathsf{td},\mathsf{msg}^{(1)})\end{array}\right]=1.

That is, we can recover the corresponding
\mathsf{st}
from
\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}
and
x
using
\mathsf{td}
, and moreover, the state is in a specific form, where each block only depends on the bit of
x
at the corresponding position.

Extractability in the extraction mode:

For classical algorithms
\mathsf{Ext}
and
\mathsf{Sim}
, let us consider the following experiment formalized by the experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{SFE},\mathpzc{A},\mathsf{Ext},\mathsf{Sim}}^{\mathsf{ext}\mbox{-}\mathsf{sim}}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{coin})
between an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
and the challenger:

1.

The challenger runs
(\mathsf{crs},\mathsf{td})\leftarrow\mathsf{CRSGen}(1^{\lambda},1)
and sends
(\mathsf{crs},\mathsf{td})
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

2.

\mathpzc{A}
sends
