\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}
and a circuit
C
with input length
w
to the challenger.

3.

The challenger runs
\mathsf{Ext}(\mathsf{td},\mathsf{msg}^{(1)})\to x
and
it computes

\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}\leftarrow\begin{cases}\mathsf{Send}(\mathsf{crs},\mathsf{msg}^{(1)},C),&\text{if }\mathsf{coin}=0\\
\mathsf{Sim}(\mathsf{crs},\mathsf{msg}^{(1)},C(x))&\text{if }\mathsf{coin}=1.\end{cases}

(106)

Then, it gives
\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

4.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs a guess
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
for
\mathsf{coin}
. The challenger outputs
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
as the final output of the experiment.

We require that there exist PPT algorithms
\mathsf{Ext}
and
\mathsf{Sim}
such that
for any QPT
\mathpzc{A}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{SFE},\mathpzc{A},\mathsf{Ext},\mathsf{Sim}}^{\mathsf{ext}\mbox{-}\mathsf{sim}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\absolutevalue{\Pr[\expb{\SFE,\qA,\Extract, \Sim}{ext}{sim} (1^\secp,0) = 1] - \Pr[\expb{\SFE,\qA,\Extract, \Sim}{ext}{sim} (1^\secp,1) = 1] }\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(107)

Efficient state superposition:

We require that there exists a QPT “coherent version"
\mathpzc{Rec}_{1}
of
\mathsf{Rec}_{1}
that is given a quantum state
\ket{\psi}=\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{w}}\alpha_{x}\ket{x}
and
\mathsf{crs}
and works as follows:

It first generates a state that is within negligible trace distance of the following state:

\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{w}}\alpha_{x}\sum_{\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}\in\mathrm{Supp}(\mathsf{Rec}_{1}(\mathsf{crs},x))}\sqrt{p_{x\to\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}}}\ket{x}\ket{\st_{x\to\msg^{(1)}} }\ket{\msg^{(1)}}

(108)

where
p_{x\to\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}}
is the probability that
\mathsf{Rec}_{1}(\mathsf{crs},x)
outputs
\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}
.
Then, it measures the last register to obtain
\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}
and outputs the residual quantum state
\ket{\psi'}
.

We remark that implementing the above by simply running the classical algorithm in the superposition does not work, since it may leave the entanglement with the randomness for
\mathsf{Rec}_{1}
.

We observe that a combination of the dual-mode oblivious transfer of
[
PVW08
]
and garbled circuits gives special dual-mode SFE satisfying the above definition.

Theorem 4.2.

If the LWE assumption holds, then there exists a special dual-mode SFE scheme.

See
Appendix

A
for the proof of
Theorem

4.2
.

5
Definitions of Secure Key Leasing with Classical Lessor

Here, we define secure key-leasing cryptographic primitives with a classical lessor.
Specifically, we consider the cases of PKE, PRF, and digital signatures.
Our definitions closely follow those of
[
KMY25
]
, but with two key differences.
First, in our setting, quantum keys are generated through classical communication between the lessor and the lessee, rather than being generated directly by a quantum lessor.
Second, we strengthen the security notions: in our definitions, the adversary is additionally granted access to an evaluation oracle (for PRFs) or a signing oracle (for signatures), in contrast to
[
KMY25
]
.

5.1
Public Key Encryption with Secure Key Leasing

In this subsection, we define public key encryption with secure key leasing (PKE-SKL), where the lessor is entirely classical and thus all the communication is classical as well. The lessee operates quantum computations only internally.

Definition 5.1 (PKE-SKL with classical lessor).

A PKE-SKL scheme
\mathsf{PKESKL}
with classical lessor is a tuple of five algorithms
(\mathpzc{IntKeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec},\mathpzc{Dec},\mathpzc{Del},\mathsf{DelVrfy})
.
Below, let
\mathcal{M}
be the message space of
\mathsf{PKESKL}
.

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow(\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{dvk})
:

The setup algorithm is a classical PPT algorithm that takes as input the security parameter
1^{\lambda}
and outputs an encryption key
\mathsf{ek}
, a master secret key
\mathsf{msk}
, and a deletion verification key
\mathsf{dvk}
.

\mathpzc{IntKeyGen}(\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{msk})\rightarrow\mathpzc{dk}\mbox{ or }\bot
:

The interactive key generation algorithm is an interactive protocol with classical communication run between a classical PPT lessor on input
\mathsf{msk}
and QPT lessee on input
\mathsf{ek}
.
This either outputs a decryption key
\mathpzc{dk}
or
\bot
, which indicates that the protocol failed because the lessee cheats.
After the protocol, the lessee obtains
\mathpzc{dk}
.

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek},m)\rightarrow\mathsf{ct}
:

The encryption algorithm is a PPT algorithm that takes an encryption key
\mathsf{ek}
and a message
m\in\mathcal{M}
, and outputs a ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}
.

\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{ct})\rightarrow\mathsf{ct}
:

The lessor’s decryption algorithm is a PPT algorithm that takes a master secret key
\mathsf{msk}
and a ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}
, and outputs a value
\tilde{m}
.

\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathpzc{dk},\mathsf{ct})\rightarrow\tilde{m}
:

The lessee’s decryption algorithm is a QPT algorithm that takes a decryption key
\mathpzc{dk}
and a ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}
, and outputs a value
\tilde{m}
.

\mathpzc{Del}(\mathpzc{dk})\rightarrow\mathsf{cert}
:

The deletion algorithm is a QPT algorithm that takes a decryption key
\mathpzc{dk}
, and outputs a classical string
\mathsf{cert}
.

\mathsf{DelVrfy}(\mathsf{dvk},\mathsf{cert})\rightarrow\top/\bot
:

The deletion verification algorithm is a deterministic classical polynomial-time algorithm that takes a deletion verification key
\mathsf{dvk}
and a deletion certificate
\mathsf{cert}
, and outputs
\top
or
\bot
.

Decryption correctness:

For every
m\in\mathcal{M}
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathpzc{dk},\mathsf{ct})\allowbreak=m\\
\qquad\quad\wedge\\
\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{ct})\allowbreak=m\end{array}\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{dvk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
\mathpzc{dk}\leftarrow\mathpzc{IntKeyGen}(\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{msk})\\
\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek},m)\end{array}\right]=1-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Deletion verification correctness:

We have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathsf{DelVrfy}(\mathsf{dvk},\mathsf{cert})=\top\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{dvk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
\mathpzc{dk}\leftarrow\mathpzc{IntKeyGen}(\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{msk})\\
\mathsf{cert}\leftarrow\mathpzc{Del}(\mathpzc{dk})\end{array}\right]=1-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Remark
5.2 (Syntax).

One could consider the syntax where
\mathsf{Setup}
and
\mathpzc{IntKeyGen}
are merged.
In such a setting,
\mathsf{Setup}
is run by the lessor and
\mathsf{ek}
is then sent to the lessee as the first message.
We chose the above syntax as the main one because it allows the encryption key to be generated even without the interaction with the lessee, which seems to be more aligned with the concept of the secure key leasing.

Remark
5.3 (On the number of rounds in key generation).

For a PKE-SKL as per the above syntax, at least two rounds of communication are necessary for
\mathpzc{IntKeyGen}
. Otherwise, it becomes possible for an adversary to copy the quantum decryption key
\mathpzc{dk}
and compromise the security (as defined below). Our construction consists of two rounds and is thus round-optimal in this sense.

Remark
5.4.

We can assume without loss of generality that a decryption key of a PKE-SKL scheme is reusable. This means that it can be reused to decrypt arbitrarily many polynomial number of ciphertexts.
This is because the decryption result of
\mathsf{ct}
using
\mathpzc{dk}
is almost deterministic by the decryption correctness and thus measuring the decryption result does not disturb the input state by the gentle measurement lemma
[
Win99
]
.

We next introduce the security notions for PKE-SKL with classical lessor.
Similarly to
[
KMY25
]
, we allow the adversary to obtain the verification key after submitting a valid certificate that passes the verification, which is stronger than many existing security definitions of PKE-SKL
[
AKN+23
]
(or key-revocable PKE
[
APV23
]
).

Definition 5.5 (IND-VRA security).

We say that a PKE-SKL scheme
\mathsf{PKESKL}
with classical lessor for the message space
\mathcal{M}
is IND-VRA secure,
16
16

16

”VRA” stands for ”
V
erifiation key
R
evealing
A
ttack”
if it satisfies the following requirement, formalized by the experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{PKESKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{vra}}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{coin})
between an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
and the challenger:

1.

The challenger runs
\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow(\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{dvk})
and sends
\mathsf{ek}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

2.

Then, the challenger
plays the role of the lessor on input
\mathsf{msk}
and
runs
\mathpzc{IntKeyGen}
with the adversary
\mathpzc{A}
.
If the protocol outputs
\bot
, the challenger outputs
0
as the output of the game.

3.

\mathpzc{A}
sends
\mathsf{cert}
and
(m_{0}^{*},m_{1}^{*})\in\mathcal{M}^{2}
to the challenger. If
\mathsf{DelVrfy}(\mathsf{dvk},\mathsf{cert})=\bot
, the challenger outputs
0
as the final output of this experiment. Otherwise, the challenger generates
\mathsf{ct}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek},m_{\mathsf{coin}}^{*})
, and sends
\mathsf{dvk}
and
\mathsf{ct}^{*}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

4.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs a guess
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
for
\mathsf{coin}
. The challenger outputs
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
as the final output of the experiment.

For any QPT
\mathpzc{A}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{PKESKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{vra}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\absolutevalue{\Pr[\expb{\PKESKL,\qA}{ind}{vra} (1^\secp,0) = 1] - \Pr[\expb{\PKESKL,\qA}{ind}{vra} (1^\secp,1) = 1] }\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(118)

We also define the one-way variant of the above security.

Definition 5.6 (OW-VRA security).

We say that a PKE-SKL scheme
\mathsf{PKESKL}
with classical lessor for the message space
\mathcal{M}
is OW-VRA secure, if it satisfies the following requirement, formalized by the experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{PKESKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ow}\mbox{-}\mathsf{vra}}(1^{\lambda})
between an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
and the challenger:

1.

The challenger runs
\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow(\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{dvk})
and sends
\mathsf{ek}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

2.

Then, the challenger
plays the role of the lessor on input
\mathsf{msk}
and
runs
\mathpzc{IntKeyGen}
with the adversary
\mathpzc{A}
.
If the protocol outputs
\bot
, the challenger outputs
0
as the output of the game.

3.

\mathpzc{A}
sends
\mathsf{cert}
to the challenger. If
\mathsf{DelVrfy}(\mathsf{dvk},\mathsf{cert})=\bot
, the challenger outputs
0
as the final output of this experiment.
Otherwise, the challenger chooses
m^{*}\leftarrow\mathcal{M}
, generates
\mathsf{ct}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek},m^{*})
, and sends
\mathsf{dvk}
and
\mathsf{ct}^{*}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

4.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs
m^{\prime}
. The challenger outputs
1
if
m^{\prime}=m^{*}
and otherwise outputs
0
as the final output of the experiment.

For any QPT
\mathpzc{A}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{PKESKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ow}\mbox{-}\mathsf{vra}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\Pr[\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{PKESKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ow}\mbox{-}\mathsf{vra}}(1^{\lambda})=1]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(119)

Remark
5.7 (Security as plain PKE).

It is straightforward to see that IND-VRA (resp. OW-VRA) security implies IND-CPA (resp. OW-CPA) security as a plain PKE scheme with quantum decryption keys.

By the quantum Goldreich-Levin lemma
[
AC02
,
CLLZ21
]
we have the following lemma. The proof is almost identical to that of
[
AKN+23
, Lemma 3.12]
, and thus we omit it.

Lemma 5.8.

If there exists a OW-VRA secure PKE-SKL scheme with classical lessor, then there exists an IND-VRA secure PKE-SKL scheme with classical lessor.

As we discuss in
Remark

5.2
, we can consider an alternative syntax where
\mathsf{Setup}
is merged into
\mathpzc{IntKeyGen}
and
\mathsf{ek}
,
\mathsf{msk}
,
\mathsf{dvk}
, and
\mathpzc{dk}
are jointly generated through the interaction between the lessee and lessor.
If we consider such an alternative syntax, our main construction in
Section

6.1
requires
3
rounds of interaction, which is not optimal.
However, as we show in
Section

6.3
, we can achieve optimal
2
rounds of interaction even in this setting as well by adding a small modification to our main construction.
To capture the scheme, we define the modified version of the syntax for PKE-SKL with classical lessor and
non-interactive key generation
.

Definition 5.9 (PKE-SKL with classical lessor and non-interactive key generation).

We define PKE-SKL with classical lessor with non-interactive key generation by replacing
\mathsf{Setup}
and
\mathpzc{IntKeyGen}
in PKE-SKL as per
Definition

5.1
with the following 2 algorithms:

\mathsf{Setup}_{\mathsf{NI}}(1^{\lambda})\to(\mathsf{pp},\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{dvk})
:

The lessor runs the setup algorithm on input
1^{\lambda}
and outputs the public parameter
\mathsf{pp}
, master secret key
\mathsf{msk}
, and the deletion verification key
\mathsf{dvk}
.

\mathpzc{KG}_{\mathsf{NI}}(\mathsf{pp})\to(\mathsf{ek},\mathpzc{dk})
:

The key generation algorithm is a QPT algorithm that takes as input a public parameter
\mathsf{pp}
and outputs an encryption key
\mathsf{ek}
and a decryption key
\mathpzc{dk}
.
We assume that
\mathsf{pp}
is included in
\mathsf{ek}
.

One can define decryption correctness and deletion verification correctness similarly to
Definition

5.1
by replacing
\mathsf{Setup}
and
\mathpzc{IntKeyGen}
with the above two algorithms.
The IND-VRA and OW-VRA security are defined similarly to
Definition

5.5
and
5.6
, where the adversary receives
\mathsf{pp}
from the challenger at the outset of the game and then sends
\mathsf{ek}
to the challenger.
The rest of the games are the same.

In the above modified syntax, one can consider
\mathsf{Setup}_{\mathsf{NI}}
combined with
\mathpzc{KG}_{\mathsf{NI}}(\mathsf{pp})
as an interactive key generation protocol with 2 rounds of communication.
We say the key generation of the scheme is non-interactive, since the lessor does not have to do anything after running
\mathsf{Setup}_{\mathsf{NI}}(1^{\lambda})
and publicizing
\mathsf{pp}
.
The drawback of PKE-SKL as per
Definition

5.9
is that it requires interaction between the lessor and lessee to generate the encryption key
\mathsf{ek}
, unlike
Definition

5.1
.

Analogue of
Lemma

5.8
in the non-interactive key generation setting holds. We therefore can focus on the construction of OW-VRA secure scheme.
We omit the proof for the same reason as
Lemma

5.8
.

Lemma 5.10.

If there exists a OW-VRA secure PKE-SKL scheme with classical lessor and non-interactive key generation, then there exists an IND-VRA secure PKE-SKL scheme with classical lessor and non-interactive key generation.

5.2
Pseudorandom and Unpredictable Functions with Secure Key Leasing

In this subsection, we define pseudorandom functions with secure key leasing (PRF-SKL) with classical lessor.
Similarly to the case of PKE-SKL, everything is classical except that the internal computation by the lessee is quantum.

Definition 5.11 (PRF-SKL with classical revocation).

A PRF-SKL scheme
\mathsf{PRFSKL}
with classical revocation for the domain
D_{\mathsf{prf}}
and the range
R_{\mathsf{prf}}
is a tuple of five algorithms
(\mathsf{Setup},\mathpzc{IntKeyGen},\mathsf{Eval},\mathpzc{LEval},\mathpzc{Del},\mathsf{DelVrfy})
.

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow(\mathsf{pp},\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{dvk})
:

The setup algorithm takes a security parameter
1^{\lambda}
and outputs a public parameter
\mathsf{pp}
, master secret key
\mathsf{msk}
, and a deletion verification key
\mathsf{dvk}
.

\mathpzc{IntKeyGen}(\mathsf{pp},\mathsf{msk})\rightarrow\mathpzc{sk}\mbox{ or }\bot
:

The interactive key generation algorithm is an interactive protocol with classical communication run between a classical PPT lessor who takes as input
\mathsf{msk}
and QPT lessee on input
\mathsf{pp}
.
This either outputs a secret key
\mathpzc{sk}
or
\bot
, which indicates that the protocol failed because the lessee cheats.
After the protocol, the lessee obtains
\mathpzc{sk}
.

\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{msk},s)\rightarrow t
:

The evaluation algorithm is a deterministic classical polynomial-time algorithm that takes a master secret key
\mathsf{msk}
and an input
s\in D_{\mathsf{prf}}
, and outputs a value
t
.

\mathpzc{LEval}(\mathpzc{sk},s)\rightarrow t
:

The leased evaluation algorithm is a QPT algorithm that takes a secret key
\mathpzc{sk}
and an input
s\in D_{\mathsf{prf}}
, and outputs a value
t
.

\mathpzc{Del}(\mathpzc{sk})\rightarrow\mathsf{cert}
:

The deletion algorithm is a QPT algorithm that takes a secret key
\mathpzc{sk}
, and outputs a classical string
\mathsf{cert}
.

\mathsf{DelVrfy}(\mathsf{dvk},\mathsf{cert})\rightarrow\top/\bot
:

The deletion verification algorithm is a deterministic classical polynomial-time algorithm that takes a deletion verification key
\mathsf{dvk}
and a deletion certificate
\mathsf{cert}
, and outputs
\top
or
\bot
.

Evaluation correctness:

For every
s\in D_{\mathsf{prf}}
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathpzc{LEval}(\mathpzc{sk},s)\allowbreak=\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{msk},s)\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{pp},\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{dvk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
\mathpzc{sk}\leftarrow\mathpzc{IntKeyGen}(\mathsf{pp},\mathsf{msk})\end{array}\right]=1-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Deletion verification correctness:

We have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathsf{DelVrfy}(\mathsf{dvk},\mathsf{cert})=\top\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{pp},\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{dvk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
\mathpzc{sk}\leftarrow\mathpzc{IntKeyGen}(\mathsf{pp},\mathsf{msk})\\
\mathsf{cert}\leftarrow\mathpzc{Del}(\mathpzc{sk})\end{array}\right]=1-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Remark
5.12 (Syntax).

Similarly to the case of PKE-SKL, one could consider the syntax where
\mathsf{Setup}
and
\mathpzc{IntKeyGen}
are merged.
However, we chose the above syntax because it allows the PRF key to be generated without the interaction with the lessee.

Remark
5.13 (On the number of rounds in key generation).

Similarly to the case of PKE-SKL, at least two rounds of communication are necessary for
\mathpzc{IntKeyGen}
. Otherwise, it cannot be secure as an adversary can generate multiple copies of the quantum secret key.
Our construction only requires 2 rounds and thus is round-optimal in this sense.

Remark
5.14 (Reusability).

We can assume without loss of generality that a key of a PRF-SKL scheme is reusable, i.e., it can be reused to evaluate on (polynomially) many inputs. This is because the output of the leased evaluation algorithm is almost unique by evaluation correctness, and thus such an operation can be done without almost disturbing the input state by the gentle measurement lemma
[
Win99
]
.

Definition 5.15 (PR-VRA security).

We say that a PRF-SKL scheme
\mathsf{PRFSKL}
with classical lessor for the domain
D_{\mathsf{prf}}
and the range
R_{\mathsf{prf}}
is PR-VRA secure, if it satisfies the following requirement, formalized by the experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{PRFSKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{pr}\mbox{-}\mathsf{vra}}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{coin})
between an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
and the challenger:

1.

The challenger runs
\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow(\mathsf{pp},\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{dvk})
and sends
\mathsf{pp}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

2.

Given
\mathsf{pp}
from the challenger,
\mathpzc{A}
starts to run.
Throughout the game,
\mathpzc{A}
has an oracle access to the evaluation oracle
\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)
, which takes as input
s\in D_{\mathsf{prf}}
and returns
\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{msk},s)
, until it receives the challenge input at Step
5
below.

\mathpzc{A}
can access the oracle arbitrarily many times at any timings.

3.

The challenger plays the role of the lessor and
runs
\mathpzc{IntKeyGen}(\mathsf{pp},\mathsf{msk})
with
\mathpzc{A}
. If the output is
\bot
,
the challenger outputs
0
as the output of the game.

4.

\mathpzc{A}
continues to have an oracle access to the evaluation oracle.
At some point,

\mathpzc{A}
sends
\mathsf{cert}
to the challenger.

5.

If
\mathsf{DelVrfy}(\mathsf{dvk},\mathsf{cert})=\bot
, the challenger outputs
0
as the final output of this experiment. Otherwise, the challenger generates
s^{*}\leftarrow D_{\mathsf{prf}}
,
t^{*}_{0}\leftarrow\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{msk},s^{*})
, and
t^{*}_{1}\leftarrow R_{\mathsf{prf}}
, and sends
(\mathsf{dvk},s^{*},t^{*}_{\mathsf{coin}})
to
\mathpzc{A}
.
After receiving the challenge input
s^{*}
,
\mathpzc{A}
looses its access to the evaluation oracle.

6.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs a guess
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
for
\mathsf{coin}
. The challenger outputs
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
as the final output of the experiment.

For any QPT
\mathpzc{A}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{PRFSKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{pr}\mbox{-}\mathsf{vra}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\absolutevalue{\Pr[\expb{\PRFSKL,\qA}{pr}{vra} (1^\secp,0) = 1] - \Pr[\expb{\PRFSKL,\qA}{pr}{vra} (1^\secp,1) = 1] }\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(125)

Remark
5.16.

While PR-VRA security defined as above
does
imply security as weak PRF, it does not imply the security as a standard PRF.
However, as observed in
[
KMY25
]
, we can add the security by a very simple conversion: run a standard PRF scheme in parallel and take the XOR of the outputs.
Thus, we focus on PR-VRA secure construction.

As an intermediate goal towards constructing PRF-SKL, we introduce a primitive which we call unpredictable functions with secure key leasing (UPF-SKL).
17
17

17

Though UPF-SKL and PRF-SKL are syntactically identical, we treat them as different primitives for clarity.

Definition 5.17 (UPF-SKL).

A UPF-SKL scheme
\mathsf{UPFSKL}
with classical revocation has the same syntax as PRF-SKL with classical revocation and satisfies the following security, which we call UP-VRA security,
formalized by the experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{UPFSKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{up}\mbox{-}\mathsf{vra}}(1^{\lambda})
between an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
and the challenger:

1.

The challenger runs
\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow(\mathsf{pp},\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{dvk})
and sends
\mathsf{pp}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

2.

Given
\mathsf{pp}
from the challenger,
\mathpzc{A}
starts to run.
Throughout the game,
\mathpzc{A}
has an oracle access to the evaluation oracle
\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)
, which takes as input
s\in D_{\mathsf{prf}}
and returns
\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{msk},s)
, until it receives the challenge input at Step
5
below.

\mathpzc{A}
can access the oracle arbitrarily many times at any timing.

3.

The challenger plays the role of the lessor and
runs
\mathpzc{IntKeyGen}(1^{\lambda})
with
\mathpzc{A}
. If the output is
\bot
,
the challenger outputs
0
as the output of the game.

4.

\mathpzc{A}
continues to have an oracle access to the evaluation oracle.
At some point,

\mathpzc{A}
sends
\mathsf{cert}
to the challenger.

5.

If
\mathsf{DelVrfy}(\mathsf{dvk},\mathsf{cert})=\bot
, the challenger outputs
0
as the final output of this experiment. Otherwise, the challenger generates
s^{*}\leftarrow D_{\mathsf{prf}}
, and sends
(\mathsf{dvk},s^{*})
to
\mathpzc{A}
.
After receiving the challenge input
s^{*}
,
\mathpzc{A}
looses its access to the evaluation oracle.

6.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs a guess
t^{\prime}
for the output on
s^{*}
. The challenger outputs
1
if
t^{\prime}=\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{msk},s^{*})
and otherwise outputs
0
.

For any QPT
\mathpzc{A}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{UPFSKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{up}\mbox{-}\mathsf{vra}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\Pr[\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{UPFSKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{up}\mbox{-}\mathsf{vra}}(1^{\lambda})=1]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(126)

By the quantum Goldreich-Levin lemma
[
AC02
,
CLLZ21
]
we have the following theorem.
The proof is almost identical to that of
[
KMY25
, Lemma 4.14]
, and thus we omit it.

Lemma 5.18.

If there exists a UP-VRA secure UPF-SKL scheme with classical revocation, then there exists a PR-VRA secure PRF-SKL scheme with classical revocation.

5.3
Digital Signatures with Secure Key Leasing

In this subsection, we define digital signatures with secure key leasing (DS-SKL) with classical lessors. .

Definition 5.19 (DS-SKL with classical lessors).

A DS-SKL scheme
\mathsf{DSSKL}
with classical lessors
is a tuple of algorithms
(\mathsf{Setup},\mathpzc{IntKeyGen},\mathsf{Sign},\mathpzc{Sign},\mathsf{SigVrfy},\mathpzc{Del},\mathsf{DelVrfy})
.
Below, let
\mathcal{M}
be the message space of
\mathsf{DSSKL}
.

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow(\mathsf{svk},\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{dvk})
:

The setup algorithm takes a security parameter
1^{\lambda}
and outputs a signature verification key
\mathsf{svk}
, master secret key
\mathsf{msk}
, and a deletion verification key
\mathsf{dvk}
.

\mathpzc{IntKeyGen}(\mathsf{svk},\mathsf{msk})\rightarrow\mathpzc{sigk}\mbox{ or }\bot
:

The interactive key generation algorithm is an interactive protocol with classical communication run between a classical PPT lessor who takes as input
\mathsf{msk}
and QPT lessee on input
\mathsf{svk}
.
This either outputs a secret key
\mathpzc{sigk}
or
\bot
, which indicates that the protocol failed because the lessee cheats.
After the protocol, the lessee obtains
\mathpzc{sigk}
.

\mathsf{Sign}(\mathsf{msk},m)\to\sigma
:

The (lessor’s) signing algorithm is a PPT algorithm that takes a master secret key
\mathsf{msk}
and a message
m\in\mathcal{M}
, and outputs a signature
\sigma
.

\mathpzc{Sign}(\mathpzc{sigk},m)\rightarrow(\mathpzc{sigk}^{\prime},\sigma)
:

The (lessee’s) signing algorithm is a QPT algorithm that takes a signing key
\mathpzc{sigk}
and a message
m\in\mathcal{M}
, and outputs a subsequent signing key
\mathpzc{sigk}^{\prime}
and a signature
\sigma
.

\mathsf{SigVrfy}(\mathsf{svk},m,\sigma)\rightarrow\top/\bot
:

The signature verification algorithm is a deterministic classical polynomial-time algorithm that takes a signature verification key
\mathsf{svk}
, a message
m\in\mathcal{M}
, and a signature
\sigma
, and outputs
\top
or
\bot
.

\mathpzc{Del}(\mathpzc{sigk})\rightarrow\mathsf{cert}
:

The deletion algorithm is a QPT algorithm that takes a signing key
\mathpzc{sigk}
, and outputs a deletion certificate
\mathsf{cert}
.

\mathsf{DelVrfy}(\mathsf{dvk},\mathsf{cert})\rightarrow\top/\bot
:

The deletion verification algorithm is a deterministic classical polynomial-time algorithm that takes a deletion verification key
\mathsf{dvk}
and a deletion certificate
\mathsf{cert}
, and outputs
\top
or
\bot
.

Signature verification correctness:

For every
m\in\mathcal{M}
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathsf{SigVrfy}(\mathsf{svk},m,\sigma)\allowbreak=\top\\
\qquad\qquad\wedge\\
\mathsf{SigVrfy}(\mathsf{svk},m,\sigma^{\prime})\allowbreak=\top\end{array}\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{svk},\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{dvk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
\mathpzc{sigk}\leftarrow\mathpzc{IntKeyGen}(\mathsf{svk},\mathsf{msk})\\
(\mathpzc{sigk}^{\prime},\sigma)\leftarrow\mathpzc{Sign}(\mathpzc{sigk},m)\\
\sigma^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{Sign}(\mathsf{msk},m)\end{array}\right]=1-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Deletion verification correctness:

We have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathsf{DelVrfy}(\mathsf{dvk},\mathsf{cert})=\top\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{svk},\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{dvk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
\mathpzc{sigk}\leftarrow\mathpzc{IntKeyGen}(\mathsf{svk},\mathsf{msk})\\
\mathsf{cert}\leftarrow\mathpzc{Del}(\mathpzc{sigk})\end{array}\right]=1-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Reusability with static signing keys:

Let
\mathpzc{sigk}
be an honestly generated signing key and
m\in\mathcal{M}
be any message.
Suppose that we run
(\mathpzc{sigk}^{\prime},\sigma)\leftarrow\mathpzc{Sign}(\mathpzc{sigk},m)
.
Then we have

\displaystyle\|\mathpzc{sigk}-\mathpzc{sigk}^{\prime}\|_{tr}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(137)

Remark
5.20 (Syntax).

Similarly to the case of PRF-SKL, one could consider the syntax where
\mathsf{Setup}
and
\mathpzc{IntKeyGen}
are merged.
However, we chose the above syntax because it allows the signature to be generated without the interaction with the lessee.

Remark
5.21 (On the number of rounds in key generation).

Similarly to the case of PRF-SKL, at least two rounds of communication are necessary for
\mathpzc{IntKeyGen}
. Otherwise, it cannot be secure as an adversary can generate multiple copies of the quantum signing key.
Our construction only requires 2 rounds and thus is round-optimal in this sense.

Remark
5.22 (Reusability).

Following
[
KMY25
]
, our syntax makes the quantum signing algorithm output
\mathpzc{sigk}^{\prime}
and requires that it should be close to
\mathpzc{sigk}
.
We call this property reusability with static signing keys.
This is stronger than the notion of reusability of defined in
[
MPY24
]
, where
\mathpzc{sigk}^{\prime}
is not required to be close to
\mathpzc{sigk}
as long as it can be still used to generate signatures on further messages.
Note that in contrast to PKE-SKL and PRF-SKL scenarios, we cannot take for granted the (even weaker form of) reusability without a loss of generality because the signing algorithm may not produce unique signature.

We next introduce the security notions for DS-SKL with classical lessors.

Definition 5.23 (RUF-VRA security).

We say that a DS-SKL scheme
\mathsf{DSSKL}
with classical revocation for the message space
\mathcal{M}
is RUF-VRA secure,
18
18

18

”RUF” stands for ”
R
andom message
U
n
F
orgeability”.
if it satisfies the following requirement, formalized by the experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{DSSKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ruf}\mbox{-}\mathsf{vra}}(1^{\lambda})
between an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
and the challenger:

1.

The challenger runs
\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow(\mathsf{svk},\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{dvk})
and sends
\mathsf{svk}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

2.

Given
\mathsf{svk}
from the challenger,
\mathpzc{A}
starts to run.
Throughout the game,
\mathpzc{A}
has an oracle access to the signing oracle
\mathsf{Sign}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)
, which takes as input
m\in\mathcal{M}
and returns
\sigma\leftarrow\mathsf{Sign}(\mathsf{msk},m)
, until it receives the challenge message
m^{*}
at Step
5
below.

\mathpzc{A}
can access the oracle arbitrary many times at any timing.

3.

The challenger plays the role of the lessor and
runs
\mathpzc{IntKeyGen}(\mathsf{svk},\mathsf{msk})
with
\mathpzc{A}
. If the output is
\bot
,
the challenger outputs
0
as the output of the game.

4.

\mathpzc{A}
continues to have an oracle access to the evaluation oracle.
At some point,

\mathpzc{A}
sends
\mathsf{cert}
to the challenger.

5.

If
\mathsf{DelVrfy}(\mathsf{dvk},\mathsf{cert})=\bot
, the challenger outputs
0
as the final output of this experiment. Otherwise, the challenger chooses
m^{*}\leftarrow\mathcal{M}
, and sends
\mathsf{dvk}
and
m^{*}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.
After receiving the challenge input
m^{*}
,
\mathpzc{A}
looses its access to the signing oracle.

6.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs a signature
\sigma^{\prime}
. The challenger outputs
1
if
\mathsf{SigVrfy}(\mathsf{svk},m^{*},\sigma^{\prime})=\top
and otherwise outputs
0
.

For any QPT
\mathpzc{A}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{DSSKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ruf}\mbox{-}\mathsf{vra}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\Pr[\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{DSSKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ruf}\mbox{-}\mathsf{vra}}(1^{\lambda})=1]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(138)

Remark
5.24.

We note that RUF-VRA security defined as above does not imply the standard EUF-CMA security as a plain digital signature.
However, as observed in
[
KMY25
]
, we can add EUF-CMA security by a very simple conversion: run the EUF-CMA secure signature scheme in parallel, signing the message with both signature schemes, and only accept verification when both signatures are valid. Thus, we focus on constructing RUF-VRA.

6
PKE-SKL with Classical Lessor

The construction of PKE-SKL with a classical lessor is presented in
Section

6.1
, following the syntax in
Definition

5.1
.
We further show how this scheme can be modified to support non-interactive key generation, as defined in
Definition

5.9
.
Both constructions are proven secure under the LWE assumption.

6.1
Construction with Interactive Key Generation

We construct a PKE-SKL scheme with a classical lessor that satisfies OW-VRA security.
Roughly, it requires that if an adversary submits a valid deletion certificate, then it can no longer break the one-wayness of the scheme even given the deletion verification key.
As shown by
[
KMY25
]
,
OW-VRA security can be easily upgraded into IND-VRA security, its indistinguishability-based counterpart, by the quantum Goldreich-Levin lemma (
Lemma

5.8
).
For the construction, we use the following ingredients:

•

NTCF generators
\mathsf{NTCF}=\mathsf{NTCF}.(\mathsf{FuncGen},\mathpzc{StateGen},\mathsf{Check},\mathsf{Invert})
defined as in
Section

2.2
.
It is associated with a deterministic algorithm
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{GoodSet}
(See
Definition

2.10
).
We assume that
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Check}
is a deterministic algorithm.
We denote the input space by
\mathcal{X}
and assume
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\{0,1\}^{w}
for some polynomial
w=w(\lambda)
.
It is known that NTCF generators can be constructed from LWE.

•

Special Dual-mode SFE
\mathsf{SFE}=\mathsf{SFE}.(\mathsf{Rec}_{1},\mathsf{Send},\mathsf{Rec}_{2})
that supports circuits with input space
\{0,1\}^{w}
as defined in
Section

4
.
It is associated with an extraction algorithm
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Ext}
, a simulation algorithm
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Sim}
, a state recovery algorithm
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{StaRcv}
,
and a ”coherent version"
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathpzc{Rec}_{1}
of
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Rec}_{1}
, which has the efficient state superposition property.
We require that
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Rec}_{2}
and
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Ext}
are deterministic.
We denote the length of each block
\alpha_{i,j}^{b}
output by
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{StaRcv}
by
u
.
As we show in
Appendix

A
, this can be constructed from LWE.

•

Watermarkable PKE (with parallel extraction):

\mathsf{WPKE}=\mathsf{WPKE}.(\mathsf{KG},\mathsf{Mark},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
as defined in
Section

3.1
.
It is associated with a parallel mark extraction algorithm
\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathpzc{Ext}
.
We require that
\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{Mark}
and
\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{Dec}
are deterministic algorithms.
We need the mark space to be
\{0,1\}^{w}
and denote the message space by
\{0,1\}^{\ell}
.
The length of the decryption key is denoted by
v
.
As we show in
Section

3.1
, this can be constructed from any PKE.

Below is the description of a OW-VRA secure scheme.
The message space of the scheme is
\{0,1\}^{2n\ell}
.

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
:

On input the security parameter, it proceeds as follows.

•

For
i\in[2n]
, run
\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})\to(\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{msk}_{i})
.

•

Choose a uniform subset
S\subseteq[2n]
such that
|S|=n
.
In the following, we denote
[2n]\backslash S
by
\overline{S}
.

•

For
i\in[2n]
, run
(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{FuncGen}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{mode})
and
(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{CRSGen}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{mode})
, where

\mathsf{mode}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $i\in S$}\\
2&\text{if $i\in\overline{S}$}.\end{cases}

•

Output
\mathsf{ek}=\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
,

\mathsf{dvk}=(S,\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{S}})
,
and
\mathsf{msk}=\{\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{msk}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
.

\mathpzc{IntKeyGen}(\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{msk})
:

The lessor and lessee run the following interactive protocol to generate a decryption key
\mathpzc{dk}
.

Lessee’s first operation:

On input
\mathsf{ek}=\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
, the lessee runs the following algorithms.

•

For each
i\in[2n]
, run
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathpzc{StateGen}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i})\rightarrow(y_{i},\ket{\psi_i})
.

•

For each
i\in[2n]
, run
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathpzc{Rec}_{1}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\ket{\psi_i})\to(\ket{\psi'_i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}_{i}^{(1)})
.

•

Send
\{y_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to the lessor.

Lessor’s operation:

Given the message
\{y_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
from the lessee, the lessor proceeds as follows.

•

For
i\in[2n]
, define
C[\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},y_{i}]
to be a circuit that takes
x
as input and computes the following:

\displaystyle C[\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},y_{i}](x)=\begin{cases}\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},x)&\text{if $\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Check}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},x,y_{i})=\top$}\\
\bot&\text{if $\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Check}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},x,y_{i})=\bot$}.\\
\end{cases}

(139)

•

For
i\in[2n]
, run
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Send}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i},C[\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},y_{i}])\rightarrow\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}
.

•

Send
\{\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to the lessee.

Lessee’s second operation:

Given the message
\{\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
, the lessee proceeds as follows.
It uses the secret state
\ket{\psi'_i}
in the following.

•

Construct the unitary
U[\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}_{i}^{(2)}]
defined by the following map:

\ket{x}\ket{\SFE.\st}\ket{0^{\lenmsg}}\mapsto\ket{x}\ket{\SFE.\st}\ket{ \SFE.\Receive{2}(\SFE.\crs_i, x, \SFE.\st, \SFE.\msg_i^{(2)}) }.

(140)

•

For
i\in[2n]
, apply the unitary to compute

\ket{\phi_i}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}}U[\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}_{i}^{(2)}]\left(\ket{\psi'_i}\ket{0^{\lenmsg}}\right).

(141)

Final output:

At the end of the protocol,
the lessee privately obtains a decryption key

\mathpzc{dk}=\bigotimes_{i\in[2n]}\ket{\phi_i}\ket{\SFE.\crs_i}\ket{\SFE.\msg^{(2)}_{i}}.

(142)

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek},m):

Upon receiving
\mathsf{ek}=\{\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
and a message
m=m_{1}\|m_{2}\|\ldots\|m_{2n}\in\{0,1\}^{2n\ell}
,
generate
\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},m_{i})
for
i\in[2n]
and output
\mathsf{ct}=\{\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
.

\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{ct}):

Given
\mathsf{msk}=\{\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{msk}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
and a ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}=\{\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
, the decryption algorithm runs

\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ct}_{i})=m_{i}
for
i\in[2n]
and outputs
m=m_{1}\|m_{2}\|\ldots\|m_{2n}
.

\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathpzc{dk},\mathsf{ct})
:

Given
\mathpzc{dk}=\bigotimes_{i\in[2n]}\ket{\phi_i}\ket{\SFE.\crs_i}\ket{\SFE.\msg^{(2)}_{i}}
and a ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}=\{\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
, the decryption algorithm performs the following for each of
i\in[2n]
.

•

Construct the unitary
V[\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}]
defined by the following map:

\ket{x}\ket{\SFE.\st}\ket{\WPKE.\dk}\ket{0^{\lenm}}\mapsto\ket{x}\ket{\SFE.\st}\ket{\WPKE.\dk}\ket{ \WPKE.\Dec(\WPKE.\dk,\WPKE.\ct_i) }.

(143)

•

Compute
V[\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}]\left(\ket{\phi_i}\ket{0^{\lenm}}\right)

and measure the last
\ell
qubits to obtain
m_{i}
.

It finally outputs
m=m_{1}\|m_{2}\|\ldots\|m_{2n}
.

\mathsf{Del}(\mathpzc{dk})
:

Given
\mathpzc{dk}=\bigotimes_{i\in[2n]}\ket{\phi_i}\ket{\SFE.\crs_i}\ket{\SFE.\msg^{(2)}_{i}}
, the deletion algorithm performs the following for each of
i\in[2n]
.

•

Measure the corresponding registers to recover the classical strings
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i}
and
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}
.

•

Compute
U[\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}_{i}^{(2)}]^{\dagger}\ket{\phi_i}

and remove the last
v
qubits to obtain
\ket{\psi''_i}
.

•

Measure
\ket{\psi''_i}
in Hadmard basis to obtain
(d_{i},c_{i})
, where each
d_{i}\in\{0,1\}^{w}
is obtained by measuring the register corresponding to an element in
\mathcal{X}

and each
c_{i}\in\{0,1\}^{uw}
is obtained by measuring the register corresponding to the SFE state.

The final output of the algorithm is given by
\mathsf{cert}=\{d_{i},c_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
,

\mathsf{DelVrfy}(\mathsf{dvk},\mathsf{cert})
:

Given the deletion verification key
\mathsf{dvk}=\left(S,\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i}\}_{i\notin S}\right)
and the deletion certificate
\mathsf{cert}=\{d_{i},c_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
,
the deletion verification algorithm works as follows.

•

For
i\in\overline{S}
, run
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Invert}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},y_{i})\to(x_{i,0},x_{i,1})
.
If the inversion fails for any of
i
, output
\bot
.

•

For
i\in\overline{S}
, run
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{StaRcv}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i})\to\{\alpha_{i,j}^{b}\}_{j\in[w],b\in\{0,1\}}
, where
\alpha_{i,j}^{b}\in\{0,1\}^{u}
.

•

For
i\in\overline{S}
, compute the vector
d^{*}_{i}=(d^{*}_{i}[1],\ldots,d^{*}_{i}[w])\in\{0,1\}^{w}
as

d^{*}_{i}[j]\coloneqq\langle c_{i,j},(\alpha^{0}_{i,j}\oplus\alpha^{1}_{i,j})\rangle,

(144)

where
c_{i}
is parsed as
c_{i}=c_{i,1}\|\cdots\|c_{i,w}
, where each block is in
\{0,1\}^{u}
.

•

For
i\in\overline{S}
, check whether

\langle d_{i}\oplus d^{*}_{i},(x_{i,0}\oplus x_{i,1})\rangle=0~\land~\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{GoodSet}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},y_{i},d_{i}\oplus d^{*}_{i})=\top

(145)

holds.
If it holds for all
i\in\overline{S}
, it outputs
\top
.
Otherwise, it outputs
\bot
.

The following theorem asserts the decryption correctness and the deletion verification correctness of the above scheme.

Theorem 6.1.

The above construction satisfies the decryption correctness if

\mathsf{NTCF}
satisfies the state generation correctness,
\mathsf{SFE}
satisfies evaluation correctness, and
\mathsf{WPKE}
satisfies the decryption correctness.
Furthermore, the above construction satisfies the deletion verification correctness if
\mathsf{NTCF}
satisfies the state generation correctness,
\mathsf{SFE}
satisfies the statistical security against malicious senders,
and
\mathsf{SFE}
supports state recovery in the hiding mode.

Proof.

To show the decryption correctness and deletion correctness,
we first observe that all the algorithms run the subsystems indexed by
i\in[2n]
, which run in parallel.
Therefore, it suffices to show that each subsystem succeeds in decryption (i.e.,
m^{\prime}_{i}=m_{i}
) and deletion verification (i.e.,
(d_{i},c_{i})
passes the verification) with overwhelming probability assuming that the lessee follows the protocol honestly.
Here, we show this for the case of
i\in\overline{S}
. The case of
i\in S
is easier to handle, since in this case,
\ket{\phi_i}
collapses to a classical state corresponding to
x_{i}=\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Invert}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},y_{i})
with overwhelming probability and repeating the same discussion as the following in its collapsed form suffices.

We first analyze the quantum state that the lessee has right after the interactive key generation protocol ends.
We observe that by the correctness of state generation of
\mathsf{NTCF}
,
with overwhelming probability,
y_{i}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathsf{pp}}
.
In addition, by the same property,

\ket{\psi_i}
is negligibly close to
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\ket{x_{i,0}}+\ket{x_{i,1}})
in the trace distance, with overwhelming probability,
where
(x_{i,0},x_{i,1})=\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Invert}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},y_{i})
.
In the following, we replace
\ket{\psi_i}
with
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\ket{x_{i,0}}+\ket{x_{i,1}})
and continue the analysis, since this change alters the probability of the decryption/verification succeeds only with negligible amount.
Then,
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathpzc{Rec}_{1}
is applied to the state.
In the first step, we obtain a state that is negligibly close to the state obtained by applying the isometry defined by the following map to
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\ket{x_{i,0}}+\ket{x_{i,1}})
:

\ket{x_{i,b}}\mapsto\sum_{\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i}\in\mathrm{Supp}(\mathsf{Rec}_{1}(\mathsf{crs}_{i},x_{i,b}))}\sqrt{p_{x_{i,b}\to\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}_{i}^{(1)}}}\ket{x_{i,b}}\ket{\SFE.\st_{x_{i,b}\to\SFE.\msg_i^{(1)}} }\ket{\SFE.\msg_i^{(1)}},

(146)

where
p_{x_{i,b}\to\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}_{i}^{(1)}}
is the probability that
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Rec}_{1}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},x_{i,b})
outputs
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}_{i}^{(1)}
and
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{st}_{x_{i,b}\to\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i}}
is the unique state corresponding to
x_{i,b}
and
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i}
.
We again ignore this negligible difference and proceed with the analysis, as this difference affects the probability of decryption/verification succeeding only by a negligible amount.
We then have the following state:

\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\sqrt{\frac{p_{i,0,z}}{2}}\ket{x_{i,0}}\ket{\st_{i,0,z}}\ket{z}+\sqrt{\frac{p_{i,1,z}}{2}}\ket{x_{i,1}}\ket{\st_{i,1,z}}\ket{z},

(147)

where we simplify the notation by denoting
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i}
by
z
,

\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{st}_{x_{i,b}\to\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i}}
by
\mathsf{st}_{i,b,z}
,

p_{x_{i,b}\to\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i}}
by
p_{i,b,z}
,
and
\mathrm{Supp}(\mathsf{Rec}_{1}(\mathsf{crs}_{i},x_{i,0}))\cup\mathrm{Supp}(\mathsf{Rec}_{1}(\mathsf{crs}_{i},x_{i,1}))
by
\mathcal{Z}
.
We then measure the last register to obtain
z^{*}
, where the residual state is

\ket{\psi'_i}=q_{i,0,z^{*}}\ket{x_{i,0}}\ket{\st_{i,0,z^*}}+q_{i,1,z^{*}}\ket{x_{i,1}}\ket{\st_{i,1,z^*}}.

(148)

In the above, we define

q_{i,b,z^{*}}\coloneqq\sqrt{p_{i,b,z^{*}}/(p_{i,0,z^{*}}+p_{i,1,z^{*}})}
.
We note that the probability that we obtain
z^{*}
by the measurement is
(p_{i,0,z^{*}}+p_{i,1,z^{*}})/2
.

We then prove the decryption correctness and the deletion verification correctness separately.

Decryption correctness:

We continue the analysis of the state that the lessee obtains.
After
z^{*}
is sent to the lessor,
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Send}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},z^{*},C[\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},y_{i}])\rightarrow\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}
is sent back to the lessee.
We observe that by the evaluation correctness of
\mathsf{SFE}
, we have

\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Rec}_{2}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},x_{i,b},\mathsf{st}_{i,b,z^{*}},\allowbreak\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}_{i}^{(2)})=\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{dk}(x_{i,b})
.
By the definition of
\ket{\phi_i}
, we have

\displaystyle\ket{\phi_i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}}q_{i,b,z^{*}}\ket{x_{i,b}}\ket{\st_{i,b,z^*}}\ket{ \SFE.\Receive{2}(\SFE.\crs_i, x_{i,b}, \st_{i,b,z^*}, \SFE.\msg_i^{(2)}) }

\displaystyle=\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}}q_{i,b,z^{*}}\ket{x_{i,b}}\ket{\st_{i,b,z^*}}\ket{ \WPKE.\dk_i(x_{i,b}) }.

(149)

After applying
V[\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}]
to
\ket{\phi_i}\ket{0^{\lenm}}
, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}}q_{i,b,z^{*}}\ket{x_{i,b}}\ket{\st_{i,b,z^*}}\ket{ \WPKE.\dk_i(x_{i,b}) }\ket{
\WPKE.\Dec(\WPKE.\dk_i(x_{i,b}),\WPKE.\ct_i)
}

(150)

\displaystyle=\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}}q_{i,b,z^{*}}\ket{x_{i,b}}\ket{\st_{i,b,z^*}}\ket{ \WPKE.\dk_i(x_{i,b}) }\ket{
m_i
}

(151)

by the decryption correctness of
\mathsf{WPKE}
.
We can therefore obtain the decryption result
m_{i}
by measuring the last register.

Deletion verification correctness:

We first observe that
\ket{\psi''_i}=\ket{\psi'_i}
, since
\ket{\psi''_i}
is obtained by applying a unitary and then its conjugate to
\ket{\psi'_i}
.
The rest of the proof involves 3 main steps: (i) We first show that the lessee obtains
(d_{i},c_{i})
such that

\displaystyle\langle d_{i},(x_{i,0}\oplus x_{i,1})\rangle\oplus\langle c_{i},(\mathsf{st}_{i,0,z^{*}}\oplus\mathsf{st}_{i,1,z^{*}})\rangle=0

(152)

with overwhelming probability,
(ii)
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{GoodSet}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},y_{i},d_{i}\oplus d^{*}_{i})=\top
with overwhelming probability,
and then (iii)
Equation

152
implies
(d_{i}\oplus d^{*}_{i})\cdot(x_{i,0}\oplus x_{i,1})=0
.

We first show (i). By applying the Hadamard gates to
\ket{\psi'_i}
,
we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2^{L/2}}\sum_{(d_{i},c_{i})\in\{0,1\}^{L}}\left(q_{i,0,z^{*}}\,(-1)^{\langle d_{i},x_{i,0}\rangle\oplus\langle c_{i},\mathsf{st}_{i,0,z^{*}}\rangle}+q_{i,1,z^{*}}\,(-1)^{\langle d_{i},x_{i,1}\rangle\oplus\langle c_{i},\mathsf{st}_{i,1,z^{*}}\rangle}\right)\ket{d_i}\ket{c_i}

(153)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{L/2}}

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{(d_{i},c_{i})\in T}(-1)^{\langle d_{i},x_{i,0}\rangle\oplus\langle c_{i},\mathsf{st}_{i,0,z^{*}}\rangle}\left(q_{i,0,z^{*}}+q_{i,1,z^{*}}\right)\ket{d_i}\ket{c_i}

(154)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{(d_{i},c_{i})\not\in T}(-1)^{\langle d_{i},x_{i,0}\rangle\oplus\langle c_{i},\mathsf{st}_{i,0,z^{*}}\rangle}\left(q_{i,0,z^{*}}-q_{i,1,z^{*}}\right)\ket{d_i}\ket{c_i}\bigg),

(155)

where

L=uw+w
is the length of each string
(d_{i},c_{i})
and

T
is the set of
(d_{i},c_{i})
that satisfies
Equation

152
.
Note that since
x_{i,0}\neq x_{i,1}
, we have
|T|=2^{L-1}
.
Therefore,
the probability that
Equation

152
does not hold,
where the probability is taken over the choice of
z^{*}
, is:

\displaystyle\Pr[z^{*}\not\in T]

\displaystyle=\sum_{z^{*}\in\mathcal{Z}}\frac{p_{i,0,z^{*}}+p_{i,1,z^{*}}}{2}\sum_{(d_{i},c_{i})\in T}\frac{1}{2^{L}}(q_{i,0,z^{*}}-q_{i,1,z^{*}})^{2}

(156)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{z^{*}\in\mathcal{Z}}(p_{i,0,z^{*}}+p_{i,1,z^{*}})\cdot\frac{(\sqrt{p_{i,0,z^{*}}}-\sqrt{p_{i,1,z^{*}}})^{2}}{p_{i,0,z^{*}}+p_{i,1,z^{*}}}

(157)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4}\sum_{z^{*}\in\mathcal{Z}}|p_{i,0,z^{*}}-p_{i,1,z^{*}}|

(158)

where the second line follows from
|T|=2^{L-1}
and by the definition of
q_{i,b,z^{*}}
and
the third line holds since we have
|a-b|^{2}\leq|a^{2}-b^{2}|
for any
a,b\geq 0
.
Finally, the last quantity is negligible by the statistical security of
\mathsf{SFE}
against the malicious senders, since the statistical distance between the first output
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}
of

\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Rec}_{1}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},x_{i,0})
and that of
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Rec}_{1}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},x_{i,1})

is
\frac{1}{2}\sum_{z^{*}\in\mathcal{Z}}|p_{i,0,z^{*}}-p_{i,1,z^{*}}|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

We then prove (ii).
We first fix
y_{i}
and
z^{*}
. These fix the values of
(x_{i,0},x_{i,1})
and
\{\alpha_{i,j}^{b}\}_{j\in[w],b\in\{0,1\}}
.
We then assume
(d_{i},c_{i})\in T
, since this happens with overwhelming probability as we saw in (i).
We then fix arbitrary
c_{i}
. This determines the value of
d^{*}_{i}
.
By
Equation

155
, we can see that
d_{i}
is distributed uniformly at random under the condition that
(c_{i},d_{i})\in T
.
Furthermore, for any
c_{i}
, half of
d_{i}\in\{0,1\}^{w}
satisfies
(c_{i},d_{i})\in T
.
We therefore have

\displaystyle\Pr_{d_{i}\text{ s.t. }(d_{i},c_{i})\in T}\left[\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{GoodSet}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},y_{i},d_{i}\oplus d^{*}_{i})=\bot\right]

(159)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\cdot\Pr_{d_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{w}}\left[\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{GoodSet}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},y_{i},d_{i}\oplus d^{*}_{i})=\bot\right]

(160)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\cdot\Pr_{d_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{w}}\left[\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{GoodSet}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},y_{i},d_{i})=\bot\right]

(161)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(162)

We finally prove (iii).
Recall that by the state recovery property in the hiding mode of
\mathsf{SFE}
, we have

\mathsf{st}_{i,b,z^{*}}=\alpha_{i,1}^{x_{i,b}[1]}\|\cdots\|\alpha_{i,w}^{x_{i,b}[w]},

(163)

where
x_{i,b}[j]
is the
j
-th bit of
x_{i,b}
.
We therefore have

\displaystyle\left\langle c_{i},\mathsf{st}_{i,0,z^{*}}\oplus\mathsf{st}_{i,1,z^{*}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in[w]}\left\langle c_{i,j},\alpha_{i,j}^{x_{i,0}[j]}\oplus\alpha_{i,j}^{x_{i,1}[j]}\right\rangle

(164)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in[w]}\left\langle c_{i,j},(x_{i,0}[j]\oplus x_{i,1}[j])(\alpha_{i,j}^{0}\oplus\alpha_{i,j}^{1})\right\rangle

(165)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in[w]}(x_{i,0}[j]\oplus x_{i,1}[j])d_{i}^{*}[j]

(166)

\displaystyle=\left\langle d_{i}^{*},x_{i,0}\oplus x_{i,1}\right\rangle,

(167)

where the second equation follows from
\alpha_{i,j}^{x_{i,0}[j]}\oplus\alpha_{i,j}^{x_{i,1}[j]}
equals to
0^{u}
if
x_{i,0}[j]=x_{i,1}[j]

and
\alpha_{i,j}^{0}\oplus\alpha_{i,j}^{1}
otherwise.
Therefore,
Equation

152
implies
\langle d_{i}^{*}\oplus d_{i},x_{i,0}\oplus x_{i,1}\rangle=0
.

This completes the proof of
Theorem

6.1
.
∎

6.2
Security Proof

Theorem 6.2.

If
\mathsf{NTCF}
satisfies cut-and-choose adaptive hardcore bit security,

\mathsf{SFE}
satisfies the mode-indistinguishability and the extractability in the extraction mode, and
\mathsf{WPKE}
satisfies one-wayness and parallel mark extractability,
then our construction is OW-VRA secure.

Proof.

We prove the theorem by a sequence of hybrids.
For the sake of the contradiction, let us assume an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
that wins the OW-VRA security game with non-negligible probability
\epsilon
.
In the following, the event that
\mathpzc{A}
wins in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{\rm xx}
is denoted by
\mathsf{E}_{\rm xx}
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
:

This is the original OW-VRA security game between the challenger and an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
.
Namely, the game proceeds as follows.

1.

The challenger computes
\mathsf{ek}
as follows.

•

It samples
(\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{msk}_{i})
for
i\in[2n]
.

•

It chooses random subset
S
of
[2n]
with size
n
.

•

It runs
(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{FuncGen}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{mode})
and
(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{CRSGen}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{mode})
for
i\in[2n]
, where
\mathsf{mode}=1
if
i\in S
and
\mathsf{mode}=2
if
i\in\overline{S}
.

Finally, it sends
\mathsf{ek}=\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

2.

The adversary
\mathpzc{A}
sends
\{y_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to the challenger.

3.

The challenger computes the message to the adversary as follows.
It first runs

\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Send}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i},C[\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},y_{i}])\rightarrow\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}

(168)

for
i\in[2n]
.
Then, it sends
\{\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

4.

Then,
\mathpzc{A}
submits the deletion certificate

\mathsf{cert}=\{d_{i},c_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
.

5.

Then, the challenger runs
\mathsf{DelVrfy}
as follows.

•

For
i\in\overline{S}
, it runs
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{StaRcv}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i})\to\{\alpha_{i,j}^{b}\}_{j\in[w],b\in\{0,1\}}
.

•

For
i\in\overline{S}
, it computes

d^{*}_{i}=(d^{*}_{i}[1],\ldots,d^{*}_{i}[w])\in\{0,1\}^{w}
as

d^{*}_{i}[j]\coloneqq c_{i,j}\cdot(\alpha^{0}_{i,j}\oplus\alpha^{1}_{i,j})
.

•

For
i\in\overline{S}
, it runs
(x_{i,0},x_{i,1})\leftarrow\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Invert}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},y_{i})
.
If the inversion fails for any of
i\in\overline{S}
, the output of
\mathsf{DelVrfy}
is
\bot
.

•

For
i\in\overline{S}
, it checks whether

(d_{i}\oplus d^{*}_{i})\cdot(x_{i,0}\oplus x_{i,1})=0~\land~\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{GoodSet}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},y_{i},d_{i}\oplus d^{*}_{i})=\top

(169)

holds using
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i}
.
If
Equation

169
holds for all
i\in\overline{S}
, the output is
\top
and otherwise,
\bot
.

If the output of the algorithm is
\bot
, then it indicates that the output of the game is
0
(i.e.,
\mathpzc{A}
failed to win the game).
Otherwise, the challenger returns

\mathsf{dvk}=\left(S,\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{S}}\right)
to
\mathpzc{A}

and continues the game.

6.

Then, the challenger chooses a random message
m=m_{1}\|m_{2}\|\cdots\|m_{2n}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{2n\ell}
and computes
\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},m_{i})
for
i\in[2n]
. Then, it sends
\mathsf{ct}=\{\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

7.

\mathpzc{A}
then outputs
m^{\prime}=m^{\prime}_{1}\|\cdots\|m^{\prime}_{2n}
.
The adversary wins the game if
m^{\prime}=m
.

By the definition, we have
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{0}]=\epsilon
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
:

In this hybrid, the challenger computes
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}
differently for
i\in S
in Step
3
of the game. Namely, it is replaced with the following:

3’

The challenger computes the message to the adversary as follows.

•

It runs

\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Ext}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i})\to x_{i}

and
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Check}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},x_{i},y_{i})=\mathsf{vrd}_{i}
for
i\in S
.

•

It computes

\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}\leftarrow\begin{cases}\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Sim}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i},\gamma_{i})&\text{if }i\in S\\
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Send}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i},C[\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},y_{i}])&\text{if }i\in\overline{S}\end{cases},

(170)

where

\gamma_{i}=\begin{cases}\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},x_{i})=\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{dk}_{i}(x_{i})&\text{if }\mathsf{vrd}_{i}=\top\\
\bot&\text{if }\mathsf{vrd}_{i}=\bot.\end{cases}

(171)

Finally, it sends
\{\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

Since we have
\gamma_{i}=C[\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},y_{i}](x_{i})
, it follows that this game is indistinguishability from the previous game by a straightforward reduction to the extractability of
\mathsf{SFE}
in the extraction mode.
Thus, we have
|\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{0}]-\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{1}]|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.
Note that in this game,
\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{msk}_{i}
is not used by the game for
i
such that
i\in S
and
\mathsf{vrd}_{i}=\bot
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
:

In this hybrid, the challenger aborts the game and sets the output of the game to be
0
(i.e.,
\mathpzc{A}
looses the game), once it turns out that there is
i\in S
such that
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Check}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},x_{i},y_{i})=\bot
.
Namely, we replace Step
3’
in the previous hybrid with the following:

3”

The challenger computes the message to the adversary as follows.

•

It runs

\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Ext}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i})\to x_{i}

and
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Check}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},x_{i},y_{i})=\mathsf{vrd}_{i}
for
i\in S
.

•

If
\mathsf{vrd}_{i}=\bot
for any of
i\in S
, it aborts the game and sets the output of the game to be
0
.

•

It computes

\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}\leftarrow\begin{cases}\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Sim}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i},\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{dk}_{i}(x_{i}))&\text{if }i\in S\\
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Send}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i},C[\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},y_{i}])&\text{if }i\in\overline{S}.\end{cases}

(172)

Finally, it sends
\{\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

We claim
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{2}]\geq\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{1}]-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.
To show this, we observe that the only scenario where the adversary
\mathpzc{A}
wins the game in the previous hybrid but not in the current one occurs when
\mathpzc{A}
chooses
y_{i}
such that
\mathsf{vrd}_{i}=\bot
for some
i\in S
, yet still succeeds in inverting the ciphertext to get
\{m_{j}\}_{j\in[2n]}
.
However, this implies that
\mathpzc{A}
has inverted
\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}
to obtain
m_{i}
without getting any secret information corresponding to
\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}
.
By a straightforward reduction to the one-wayness of
\mathsf{WPKE}
, the claim follows.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
:

In this hybrid, we change the winning condition of the adversary.
Namely, we replace Step
6
and Step
7
of the game with the following.

6’

The challenger parses the adversary
\mathpzc{A}
right before Step
6
of the game as a unitary circuit
U{A}
and a quantum state
\rho{A}
.
It then constructs a quantum decryption algorithm
\mathpzc{D}
that takes as input
\{\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}\}_{i\in S}
as input and outputs
\{m^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in S}

from
\{\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{S}}
,
S
, and

\mathpzc{A}
as follows.

\mathpzc{D}(\{\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}\}_{i\in S})
:

It chooses
m_{i}\leftarrow\mathcal{M}
for
i\in\overline{S}
and computes
\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},m_{i})
for
i\in\overline{S}
.
Then, it runs
\mathpzc{A}
on input
\{\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to obtain
m^{\prime}=m^{\prime}_{1}\|\cdots\|m^{\prime}_{2n}
.
Finally, it outputs
\{m^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in S}
.

7’

The challenger parses

\mathpzc{D}
as a pair of unitary
U{D}
and a quantum state
\rho{D}
.
Then, it runs the extraction algorithm as

\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathpzc{Ext}(\{\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}\}_{i\in S},(U{D},\rho{D}))\to\{x^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in S}
.

8’

If
x_{i}=x^{\prime}_{i}
holds for all
i\in S
,
the challenger sets the output of the game to be
1
.
Otherwise, it sets it to be
0
.

As we show in
Lemma

6.3
,
we have
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{3}]\geq\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{2}]-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)

by the parallel extractability of
\mathsf{WPKE}
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
:

In this game, we replace Step
8’
of the above game with the following:

8”

If
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Check}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},x^{\prime}_{i},y_{i})=\top
holds for all
i\in S
,
the challenger sets the output of the game to be
1
.
Otherwise, it sets it to be
0
.

We observe that
x^{\prime}_{i}=x_{i}
in Step
8’
implies
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Check}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},x^{\prime}_{i},y_{i})=\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Check}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},x_{i},y_{i})=\top
.
To see the latter equality holds, recall that the game is set to abort if
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Check}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},x_{i},y_{i})=\bot
for any
i\in S

in Step
3”
. Thus, if the game continues to Step
8’
, the equality holds.
We therefore have
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{4}]\geq\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{3}]
.
(Actually,
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{4}]=\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{3}]
holds due to the uniqueness of
x_{i}
that passes the check. However, showing the inequality suffices for our proof.)

\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}
:

In this game, we switch Step
3”
in the previous hybrid back to Step
3’
.
The difference from the previous hybrid is that it continues the game even if
\mathsf{vrd}_{i}=\bot
for some
i\in S
.
Since the adversary had no chance in winning the game in such a case in the previous hybrid, this change only increases the chance of the adversary winning.
We therefore have
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{5}]\geq\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{4}]
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}
:

In this hybrid, we switch Step
3’
in the previous hybrid back to Step
3
.
Namely, it computes
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Send}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\allowbreak\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i},C[\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},y_{i}])\rightarrow\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}
for all
i\in[2n]
again.
Similarly to the change from
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
, the winning probability of
\mathpzc{A}
in this game does not change significantly by the extractability of
\mathsf{SFE}
in the extraction mode. Namely, we have
|\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{6}]-\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{5}]|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.
Note that we do not need
\{\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i}\}_{i\in S}
any more to run the game.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{7}
:

In this hybrid, we change the way
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i}
is sampled for
i\in S
.
Namely, it is sampled as
(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{CRSGen}(1^{\lambda},2)
for all
i\in[2n]
.
Concretely, we replace Step
1
with the following:

1’

The challenger computes the first message to the adversary as follows.

•

It samples
(\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{msk}_{i})
for
i\in[2n]
.

•

It chooses random subset
S
of
[2n]
with size
n
.

•

It runs
(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{FuncGen}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{mode})
and
(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{CRSGen}(1^{\lambda},2)
for
i\in[2n]
, where
\mathsf{mode}=1
if
i\in S
and
\mathsf{mode}=2
if
i\in\overline{S}
.

Finally, it sends
\mathsf{ek}=\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

Since we do not need
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i}
for
i\in S
to run
\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{7}
, a straightforward reduction to the mode indistinguishability of
\mathsf{SFE}
implies that
\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{7}
are computationally indistinguishable.
Therefore, we have
|\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{6}]-\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{7}]|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{8}
:

In this game, we change the way how the challenger runs
\mathsf{DelVrfy}
.
In particular, the challenger computes
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{StaRcv}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{ct}_{i})\to\{\alpha_{i,j}^{b}\}_{j,b}
and
d^{*}_{i}
for all
i\in[2n]
,
instead of only for
i\in\overline{S}
.
Still, the inversion of
y_{i}
and
the check of
Equation

169
are performed only for
i\in\overline{S}
.
Concretely, we replace Step
5
with the following.

5’

Then, the challenger runs (the modified version of)
\mathsf{DelVrfy}
as follows.

•

For
i\in[2n]
, it runs
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{StaRcv}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i})\to\{\alpha_{i,j}^{b}\}_{j\in[w],b\in\{0,1\}}
.

•

For
i\in[2n]
, it computes

d^{*}_{i}=(d^{*}_{i}[1],\ldots,d^{*}_{i}[w])\in\{0,1\}^{w}
as

d^{*}_{i}[j]\coloneqq c_{i,j}\cdot(\alpha^{0}_{i,j}\oplus\alpha^{1}_{i,j})
.

•

For
i\in\overline{S}
, it runs
(x_{i,0},x_{i,1})\leftarrow\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Invert}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},y_{i})
.
If the inversion fails for any of
i\in\overline{S}
, the output of
\mathsf{DelVrfy}
is
\bot
.

•

For
i\in\overline{S}
, it checks whether

(d_{i}\oplus d^{*}_{i})\cdot(x_{i,0}\oplus x_{i,1})=0~\land~\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{GoodSet}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},y_{i},d_{i}\oplus d^{*}_{i})=\top

(173)

holds using
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i}
.
If
Equation

173
holds for all
i\in\overline{S}
, the output is
\top
and otherwise,
\bot
.

If the output of the algorithm is
\bot
, then it indicates that the output of the game is
0
(i.e.,
\mathpzc{A}
failed to win the game).
Otherwise, the challenger returns

\mathsf{dvk}=\left(S,\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{S}}\right)
to
\mathpzc{A}

and continues the game.

We can see that this change is only conceptual, since the outcome of the verification is unchanged.
We may therefore have

\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{8}]=\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{7}]
.

Based on the above discussion, it is established that

\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{8}]\geq\epsilon-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
, which is non-negligible by our assumption.
However, as we show in
Lemma

6.4
, we have
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{8}]={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
assuming cut-and-choose adaptive hardcore bit property of
\mathsf{NTCF}
.
This results in a contradiction as desired.
∎

It remains to prove
Lemma

6.3
and
Lemma

6.4
.

Lemma 6.3.

If
\mathsf{WPKE}
satisfies parallel extractability, we have
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{3}]\geq\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{2}]-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

Proof.

We consider an adversary
\mathpzc{B}
who plays the role of the challenger in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
for
\mathpzc{A}
, while working as an adversary against the parallel one-way inversion experiment with the number of instances
n
. It proceeds as follows:

1.

Given
\{\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ek}^{\prime}_{j}\}_{j\in[n]}
from the challenger,
\mathpzc{B}
proceeds as follows.

•

\mathpzc{B}
chooses
S=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}\}\subset[2n]
and samples
\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
as specified in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
.

•

It sets
\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i_{j}}\coloneqq\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ek}^{\prime}_{j}
for
j\in[n]
.

•

It then samples
(\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{msk}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})
for
i\in\overline{S}
.

•

Then, it sends
\mathsf{ek}=\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

•

Then, the adversary
\mathpzc{A}
sends
\{y_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to
\mathpzc{B}
.

•

\mathpzc{B}
then computes
x_{i}
for
i\in S
and
\mathsf{vrd}_{i}
using the trapdoors for
\mathsf{SFE}
and
\mathsf{NTCF}
as in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
. If
\mathsf{vrd}_{i}=\bot
for any of
i\in S
, it aborts.

•

It then sets
\{x^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{n}\}\coloneqq\{x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}}\}
and sends it to its challenger.

2.

Given
\{\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{dk}^{\prime}_{j}(x^{\prime}_{j})\}_{j\in[n]}
from the challenger,
\mathpzc{B}
proceeds as follows.

•

It sets
\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{dk}_{i_{j}}(x_{i_{j}})\coloneqq\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{dk}^{\prime}_{j}(x^{\prime}_{j})
for
j\in[n]
.

•

It computes
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}
as follows:

\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}\leftarrow\begin{cases}\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Sim}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i},\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{dk}_{i}(x_{i}))&\text{if }i\in S\\
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Send}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i},C[\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},y_{i}])&\text{if }i\in\overline{S}.\end{cases}

(174)

•

\mathpzc{B}
then sends
\{\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

•

Then,
\mathpzc{A}
submits the deletion certificate
\mathsf{cert}
to
\mathpzc{B}
.

\mathpzc{B}
runs
\mathsf{DelVrfy}
using the trapdoors for
\mathsf{NTCF}
and
\mathsf{SFE}
.
If the output is
\bot
,
\mathpzc{B}
also outputs
\bot
and aborts.
Otherwise,
\mathpzc{B}
returns

\mathsf{dvk}=\left(S,\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{S}}\right)
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

3.

Then, the challenger sends
\{\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ct}^{\prime}_{j}\}_{j\in[n]}
to
\mathpzc{B}
.

4.

\mathpzc{B}
computes its output as follows.

•

It sets
\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ct}_{i_{j}}\coloneqq\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ct}^{\prime}_{j}
for
j\in[n]
.

•

It samples
m_{i}\leftarrow\mathcal{M}
and computes
\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},m_{i})
for
i\in\overline{S}
.

•

It then sends
\{\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

•

\mathpzc{A}
output
(m^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,m^{\prime}_{2n})
.
It then outputs
(m^{\prime}_{i_{1}},\ldots,m^{\prime}_{i_{n}})
as its output.

We can see that
\mathpzc{B}
defined above perfectly simulates
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
for
\mathpzc{A}
and
\mathpzc{B}
wins the game if so does
\mathpzc{A}
.
We therefore have
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{WPKE},\mathpzc{B},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ow}}(\lambda)\geq\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{2}]
.
Furthermore, by the parallel extractability of
\mathsf{WPKE}
, we have

\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{WPKE},\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{Ext},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ext}}(\lambda)\geq\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{WPKE},\mathpzc{B},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ow}}(\lambda)-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.
We can also see that
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{3}]=\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{WPKE},\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{Ext},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ext}}(\lambda)
holds, because the way
\mathpzc{B}
inverts the ciphertexts is exactly the same as the way
\mathpzc{D}
does in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
.
By combining these equations, the lemma follows.
∎

Lemma 6.4.

If
\mathsf{NTCF}
satisfies the cut-and-choose adaptive hardcore bit property, we have
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{8}]={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

Proof.

To show the lemma, we construct an adversary
\mathpzc{B}
who plays the role of the challenger in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{8}
for
\mathpzc{A}
and tries to break the cut-and-choose adaptive hardcore bit property of
\mathsf{NTCF}
.

\mathpzc{B}
proceeds as follows.

1.

Given
\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
from its challenger,
\mathpzc{B}
computes the first message to its challenger as follows.

•

It runs

(\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{msk}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{WPKE}(1^{\lambda})
and

(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{CRSGen}(1^{\lambda},2)
for
i\in[2n]
and sends
\mathsf{ek}=\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

•

\mathpzc{A}
returns
\{y_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to
\mathpzc{B}
.

•

\mathpzc{B}
computes
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Send}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i},C[\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},y_{i}])\rightarrow\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}
for
i\in[2n]
.
It then sends
\{\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

•

\mathpzc{A}
then submits the deletion certificate
\mathsf{cert}=\{d_{i},c_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to
\mathpzc{B}
.

•

\mathpzc{B}
runs
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{StaRcv}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i})\to\{\alpha_{i,j}^{b}\}_{j\in[w],b\in\{0,1\}}
for
i\in[2n]
.
It then computes

d^{*}_{i}
from
c_{i}
and
\{\alpha_{i,j}^{b}\}_{j\in[w],b\in\{0,1\}}
for
i\in[2n]
and sends
\{y_{i},d_{i}\oplus d^{*}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to the challenger.

2.

If
\mathpzc{B}
passes the verification, the challenger gives

S
and
\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{S}}
to
\mathpzc{B}
.
Then,
\mathpzc{B}
gives
\mathsf{dvk}=(S,\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{S}})
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

3.

\mathpzc{B}
then constructs a quantum decryption algorithm
\mathpzc{D}=(U{D},\rho{D})

from
\{\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{S}}
,
S
, and

\mathpzc{A}
as specified in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
. Then, it runs the extraction algorithm as
\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathpzc{Ext}(\{\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}\}_{i\in S},(U{D},\rho{D}))\to\{x^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in S}
.
Then,
\mathpzc{B}
submits
\{x^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in S}
to its challenger.

We can see that
\mathpzc{B}
perfectly simulates
\mathsf{Hyb}_{8}
, where a part of the deletion verification algorithm (i.e., inversion of
y_{i}
and checking of
Equation

169
for
i\in\overline{S}
) is delegated to its challenger.
Furthermore, it can be checked that
\mathpzc{B}
wins the cut-and-choose adaptive hardcore bit game if and only if
\mathpzc{A}
wins in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{8}
.
We therefore have
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{NTCF},\mathpzc{B}}^{\mathsf{cut}\mbox{-}\mathsf{and}\mbox{-}\mathsf{choose}}(\lambda,n)=\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{8}]
.
Hence, the lemma follows.
∎

6.3
Construction with Non-Interactive Key Generation

Here, we discuss that the construction of PKE-SKL with interactive key generation that we showed in
Section

6.1
can be readily modified into a construction with non-interactive key generation as per
Definition

5.9
.
The idea is to merging the lessor’s operation with the encryption algorithm.
For this change to be possible, we need that the encryptor has to know the decryption keys of
\mathsf{WPKE}
. To accomplish this, we postpone the generation of the
\mathsf{WPKE}
encryption keys until the execution of the encryption algorithm.

More concretely, we give the description of the construction in the following. Let
\mathsf{PKESKL}_{\mathsf{int}}=\mathsf{PKESKL}_{\mathsf{int}}.(\mathsf{Setup},\allowbreak\mathpzc{IntKeyGen},\allowbreak\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec},\mathpzc{Dec},\mathsf{Del},\mathsf{DelVrfy})
be the construction in
Section

6.1
.

\mathsf{Setup}_{\mathsf{NI}}(1^{\lambda})
:

It samples
\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
as in
\mathsf{PKESKL}_{\mathsf{int}}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
. However, it does
not
sample
\mathsf{WPKE}
encryption keys.
It sets
\mathsf{pp}=\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
,
\mathsf{msk}=\bot
and

\mathsf{dvk}=\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{S}}
.

\mathpzc{KG}_{\mathsf{NI}}(\mathsf{pp})
:

Upon receiving the input
\mathsf{pp}=\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
, it runs the first operation of the lessee in
\mathsf{PKESKL}_{\mathsf{int}}.\mathpzc{IntKeyGen}
to obtain

\{y_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
and
\ket{\psi'}
.
Note that it is possible to run the lessee’s algorithm even though encryption keys of
\mathsf{WPKE}
are not generated.
It sets
\mathsf{ek}=\{y_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
and
\mathpzc{dk}=\ket{\psi'}
.

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek},m)
:

It takes as input
\mathsf{ek}=\{y_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
and proceeds as follows.

•

It first samples
\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})\to(\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{msk}_{i})
for
i\in[2n]
.

•

It then runs the lessor’s operation of
\mathsf{PKESKL}_{\mathsf{int}}.\mathpzc{IntKeyGen}
to obtain
\{\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
. Note that it is possible to run the lessor’s operation since it knows
\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{msk}_{i}
for all
i
.

•

It then runs
\mathsf{PKESKL}_{\mathsf{int}}.\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{PKESKL}_{\mathsf{int}}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{PKESKL}_{\mathsf{int}}.\mathsf{ek},m)
.

•

Finally, it outputs the ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}=(\{\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]},\mathsf{PKESKL}_{\mathsf{int}}.\mathsf{ct})
.

\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathpzc{dk},\mathsf{ct})
:

It takes as input a ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}=(\{\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]},\mathsf{PKESKL}_{\mathsf{int}}.\mathsf{ct})
and the decryption key
\mathpzc{dk}=\ket{\psi'}
, it runs the lessee’s second operation on
\{\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
and
\ket{\psi'}
to obtain
\otimes_{i\in[2n]}\ket{\phi_i}
.
It then sets
\mathsf{PKESKL}_{\mathsf{int}}.\mathpzc{dk}=\bigotimes_{i\in[2n]}\ket{\phi_i}\ket{\SFE.\crs_i}\ket{\SFE.\msg^{(2)}_{i}}
and runs
\mathsf{PKESKL}_{\mathsf{int}}.\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathsf{PKESKL}_{\mathsf{int}}.\mathpzc{dk},\allowbreak\mathsf{PKESKL}_{\mathsf{int}}.\mathsf{ct})=m
.
It finally outputs
m
.

\mathsf{Del}(\mathpzc{dk})
:

It measures
\mathpzc{dk}=\ket{\psi'}
in Hadamard basis to obtain
\{d_{i},c_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
.
It then outputs
\mathsf{cert}=\{d_{i},c_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
.

\mathsf{DelVrfy}(\mathsf{dvk},\mathsf{cert})
:

It runs
\mathsf{PKESKL}_{\mathsf{int}}.\mathsf{DelVrfy}(\mathsf{dvk},\mathsf{cert})
and outputs what it outputs.

The decryption correctness and deletion verification correctness of the scheme readily follows from that of
\mathsf{PKESKL}_{\mathsf{int}}
.
20
20

20

The above scheme does not support the decryption algorithm
\mathsf{Dec}
using
\mathsf{msk}
. However, adding this is straightforward: We have
\mathsf{Setup}_{\mathsf{NI}}
generate an encryption key
\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{ek}
and a decryption key
\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{dk}
for the plain PKE. We then include
\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{ek}
in
\mathsf{pp}
, and
\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{dk}
in
\mathsf{msk}
. The encryption algorithm encrypts
m
using
\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{ek}
, and incorporates the resulting ciphertext
\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{ct}
into
\mathsf{ct}
. Consequently, the decryption algorithm can decrypt
\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{ct}
when provided with
\mathsf{msk}
.

The following theorem asserts the security of the above construction.

Theorem 6.5.

If
