\mathsf{PKESKL}_{\mathsf{int}}
is OW-VRA secure, then so is the above construction.

Proof.

We observe that the OW-VRA security game for the above construction is the same as that for
\mathsf{PKESKL}_{\mathsf{int}}
, except that the adversary has to submit the deletion certificate
in the former before getting
\{\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
.
Since the former restricts the adversary more than the latter, the security of
\mathsf{PKESKL}_{\mathsf{int}}
immediately implies that of the above construction.
∎

Using
Lemma

5.10
, we can upgrade the construction to be IND-VRA secure.

7
PRF-SKL with Classical Lessor

In this section, we show the construction of PRF-SKL with a classical lessor from the LWE assumption.

7.1
Construction

We construct a UPF-SKL scheme with a classical lessor that satisfies UPF-VRA security as per
Definition

5.17
.
Roughly, it requires that if an adversary submits a valid deletion certificate, then it can no longer break unpredictability of the scheme even given the deletion verification key.
As shown by
[
KMY25
]
,
UPF-VRA security can be easily upgraded into IND-VRA security, its indistinguishability-based counterpart, by the quantum Goldreich-Levin lemma (
Lemma

5.8
).
The construction is very similar to our PKE-SKL construction, where we replace the watermarkable PKE with watermarkable PRF.
Concretely, we use the following ingredients:

•

NTCF generators
\mathsf{NTCF}=\mathsf{NTCF}.(\mathsf{FuncGen},\mathpzc{StateGen},\mathsf{Check},\mathsf{Invert})
defined as in
Section

2.2
.
It is associated with a deterministic algorithm
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{GoodSet}
(See
Definition

2.10
). We assume that
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Check}
is a deterministic algorithm.
We denote the input space by
\mathcal{X}
and assume
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\{0,1\}^{w}
for some polynomial
w=w(\lambda)
.
It is known that NTCF generators can be constructed from LWE.

•

Special Dual-mode SFE
\mathsf{SFE}=\mathsf{SFE}.(\mathsf{Rec}_{1},\mathsf{Send},\mathsf{Rec}_{2})
that supports circuits with input space
\{0,1\}^{w}
as defined in
Section

4
.
It is associated with an extraction algorithm
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Ext}
, a simulation algorithm
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Sim}
, a state recovery algorithm
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{StaRcv}
,
and a “coherent version"
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathpzc{Rec}_{1}
of
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Rec}_{1}
, which has the efficient state superposition property.
We require that
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Rec}_{2}
and
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Ext}
are deterministic.
We denote that the length of each block
\alpha_{i,j}^{b}
output by
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{StaRcv}
by
u
.
As we show in
Appendix

A
, this can be constructed from LWE.

•

Watermarkable UPF (with parallel extraction):

\mathsf{WUPF}=\mathsf{WUPF}.(\mathsf{KG},\mathsf{Mark},\mathsf{Eval})
as defined in
Section

3.2
.
Without loss of generality,
we assume that it also satisfies security as a plain UPF (See
Remark

3.3
).
It is associated with a parallel mark extraction algorithm
\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathpzc{Ext}
.
We require that
\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{Mark}
and
\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{Eval}
are deterministic algorithms.
We need the mark space to be
\{0,1\}^{w}
and denote the input space (resp., output space) by
\{0,1\}^{\ell}
(resp.,
\{0,1\}^{\ell^{\prime}}
).
The bit-length of the marked key is denoted by
v
.
As we show in
Section

3.2
, this can be constructed from any OWF.

Below is the description of a UP-VRA secure UPF-SKL scheme.
The message space of the scheme is
\{0,1\}^{2n\ell}
.

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
:

On input the security parameter, it proceeds as follows.

•

For
i\in[2n]
, run
\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})\to(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{xk}_{i})
.

•

Choose a uniform subset
S\subseteq[2n]
such that
|S|=n
.
In the following, we denote
[2n]\backslash S
by
\overline{S}
.

•

For
i\in[2n]
, run
(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{FuncGen}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{mode})
and
(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{CRSGen}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{mode})
, where

\mathsf{mode}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $i\in S$}\\
2&\text{if $i\in\overline{S}$}.\end{cases}

•

Output
\mathsf{pp}=\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
,

\mathsf{msk}=\{\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
, and

\mathsf{dvk}=(S,\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{S}})
.

\mathpzc{IntKeyGen}(\mathsf{pp},\mathsf{msk})
:

The lessor and lessee run the following interactive protocol to generate
\mathpzc{sk}
.
Here, the lessor takes
\mathsf{msk}
as input and the lessee takes
\mathsf{pp}
as input.

Lessee’s first operation:

The lessee parses
\mathsf{pp}=\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
and runs the following algorithms.

•

For each
i\in[2n]
, run
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathpzc{StateGen}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i})\rightarrow(y_{i},\ket{\psi_i})
.

•

For each
i\in[2n]
, run
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathpzc{Rec}_{1}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\ket{\psi_i})\to(\ket{\psi'_i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}_{i}^{(1)})
.

•

Send
\{y_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to the lessor.

Lessor’s operation:

Given the message
\{y_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
from the lessee, the lessor proceeds as follows.

•

For
i\in[2n]
, define
C[\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},y_{i}]
to be a circuit that takes
x
as input and computes the following:

\displaystyle C[\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},y_{i}](x)=\begin{cases}\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},x)&\text{if $\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Check}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},x,y_{i})=\top$}\\
\bot&\text{if $\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Check}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},x,y_{i})=\bot$}.\\
\end{cases}

(175)

•

For
i\in[2n]
, run
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Send}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i},C[\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},y_{i}])\rightarrow\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}
.

•

Send
\{\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to the lessee.

Lessee’s second operation:

Given the message
\{\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
, the lessee proceeds as follows.
It uses the secret state
\ket{\psi'_i}
in the following.

•

Construct the unitary
U[\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}_{i}^{(2)}]
defined by the following map:

\ket{x}\ket{\SFE.\st}\ket{0^{\lenmsg}}\mapsto\ket{x}\ket{\SFE.\st}\ket{ \SFE.\Receive{2}(\SFE.\crs_i, x, \SFE.\st, \SFE.\msg_i^{(2)}) }.

(176)

•

For
i\in[2n]
, apply the unitary to compute

\ket{\phi_i}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}}U[\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}_{i}^{(2)}]\left(\ket{\psi'_i}\ket{0^{\lenmsg}}\right).

(177)

Final output:

The lessee privately obtains a secret key

\mathpzc{sk}=\bigotimes_{i\in[2n]}\ket{\phi_i}\ket{\SFE.\crs_i}\ket{\SFE.\msg^{(2)}_{i}}.

(178)

\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{msk},s):

Upon receiving
\mathsf{msk}=\{\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
and an input
s=s_{1}\|s_{2}\|\ldots\|s_{2n}\in\{0,1\}^{2n\ell}
,
compute
t_{i}\coloneqq\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},s_{i})
for
i\in[2n]
and output
t=t_{1}\|\cdots\|t_{2n}
.

\mathpzc{LEval}(\mathpzc{sk},s)
:

Given
\mathpzc{sk}=\bigotimes_{i\in[2n]}\ket{\phi_i}\ket{\SFE.\crs_i}\ket{\SFE.\msg^{(2)}_{i}}
and an input
s=s_{1}\|s_{2}\|\ldots\|s_{2n}
, the evaluation algorithm performs the following for each of
i\in[2n]
.

•

Construct the unitary
V[s_{i}]
defined by the following map:

\ket{x}\ket{\SFE.\st}\ket{\WPRF.\sk}\ket{0^{\ell'}}\mapsto\ket{x}\ket{\SFE.\st}\ket{\WPRF.\sk}\ket{ \WPRF.\Eval(\WPRF.\sk,s_i) }.

(179)

•

Compute
V[s_{i}]\left(\ket{\phi_i}\ket{0^{\ell'}}\right)

and measure the last
\ell^{\prime}
qubits to obtain
t_{i}
.

It finally outputs
t=t_{1}\|t_{2}\|\ldots\|t_{2n}
.

\mathsf{Del}(\mathpzc{dk})
:

Given
\mathpzc{dk}=\bigotimes_{i\in[2n]}\ket{\phi_i}\ket{\SFE.\crs_i}\ket{\SFE.\msg^{(2)}_{i}}
, the deletion algorithm performs the following for each of
i\in[2n]
.

•

Measure the corresponding registers to recover the classical strings
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i}
and
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}
.

•

Compute
U[\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}_{i}^{(2)}]^{\dagger}\ket{\phi_i}

and remove the last
v
qubits to obtain
\ket{\psi''_i}
.

•

Measure
\ket{\psi''_i}
in Hadmard basis to obtain
(d_{i},c_{i})
, where each
d_{i}\in\{0,1\}^{w}
is obtained by measuring the register corresponding to an element in
\mathcal{X}

and each
c_{i}\in\{0,1\}^{uw}
is obtained by measuring the register corresponding to the SFE state.

The final output of the algorithm is given by
\mathsf{cert}=\{d_{i},c_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
,

\mathsf{DelVrfy}(\mathsf{dvk},\mathsf{cert})
:

Given the deletion verification key
\mathsf{dvk}=\left(S,\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{S}}\right)
and the deletion certificate
\mathsf{cert}=\{d_{i},c_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
,
the deletion verification algorithm works as follows.

•

For
i\in\overline{S}
, run
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Invert}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},y_{i})\to(x_{i,0},x_{i,1})
.
If the inversion fails for any of
i
, output
\bot
.

•

For
i\in\overline{S}
, run
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{StaRcv}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i})\to\{\alpha_{i,j}^{b}\}_{j\in[w],b\in\{0,1\}}
, where
\alpha_{i,j}^{b}\in\{0,1\}^{u}
.

•

For
i\in\overline{S}
, compute the vector
d^{*}_{i}=(d^{*}_{i}[1],\ldots,d^{*}_{i}[w])\in\{0,1\}^{w}
as

d^{*}_{i}[j]\coloneqq\langle c_{i,j},(\alpha^{0}_{i,j}\oplus\alpha^{1}_{i,j})\rangle,

(180)

where
c_{i}
is parsed as
c_{i}=c_{i,1}\|\cdots\|c_{i,w}
, where each block is in
\{0,1\}^{u}
.

•

For
i\in\overline{S}
, check whether

\langle d_{i}\oplus d^{*}_{i},(x_{i,0}\oplus x_{i,1})\rangle=0~\land~\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{GoodSet}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},y_{i},d_{i}\oplus d^{*}_{i})=\top

(181)

holds.
If it holds for all
i\in\overline{S}
, it outputs
\top
.
Otherwise, it outputs
\bot
.

Correctness

The following theorem asserts the evaluation correctness and the deletion verification correctness of the above scheme.

Theorem 7.1.

The above construction satisfies the evaluation correctness if

\mathsf{NTCF}
satisfies the state generation correctness,
\mathsf{SFE}
satisfies evaluation correctness, and
\mathsf{WUPF}
satisfies the evaluation correctness.
Furthermore, the above construction satisfies the deletion verification correctness if
\mathsf{NTCF}
satisfies the state generation correctness,
\mathsf{SFE}
satisfies the statistical security against malicious senders,
and
\mathsf{SFE}
supports state recovery in the hiding mode.

Proof.

We omit the proof of deletion verification correctness, since it is completely the same as that for PKE-SKL shown in

Theorem

6.1
.
We then prove the evaluation correctness.
As observed in the proof for
Theorem

6.1
,
all the algorithms run the subsystems indexed by
i\in[2n]
, which run in parallel.
Therefore, it suffices to show that each subsystem succeeds in evaluation with overwhelming probability assuming that the lessee follows the protocol honestly.
By the same argument as
Theorem

6.1
, we can represent

\ket{\psi'_i}
as

\ket{\psi'_i}=q_{i,0,z^{*}}\ket{x_{i,0}}\ket{\st_{i,0,z^*}}+q_{i,1,z^{*}}\ket{x_{i,1}}\ket{\st_{i,1,z^*}}
,
where
(x_{i,0},x_{i,1})=\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Invert}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},y_{i})
,
z^{*}=\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i}
, and
\mathsf{st}_{i,b,z^{*}}
is the unique state corresponding to

x_{i,b}
and
z^{*}=\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i}
.
The definition of
q_{i,b,z^{*}}
is not relevant here, but it can be found in the proof of
Theorem

6.1
.
By the evaluation correctness of
\mathsf{SFE}
, we have
22
22

22

Precisely, the equation does not hold in strict sense, since we ignore negligible difference in trace distance in the proof of
Theorem

6.1
. However, this can be ignored as this difference only negligibly affects the probability that the verification passes.

\displaystyle\ket{\phi_i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}}q_{i,b,z^{*}}\ket{x_{i,b}}\ket{\st_{i,b,z^*}}\ket{ \SFE.\Receive{2}(\SFE.\crs_i, x_{i,b}, \st_{i,b,z^*}, \SFE.\msg_i^{(2)}) }

(182)

\displaystyle=\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}}q_{i,b,z^{*}}\ket{x_{i,b}}\ket{\st_{i,b,z^*}}\ket{
\WPRF.\sk_i(x_{i,b})
}.

(183)

After applying
V[s_{i}]
, the above state becomes

\displaystyle\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}}q_{i,b,z^{*}}\ket{x_{i,b}}\ket{\st_{i,b,z^*}}\ket{
\WPRF.\sk_i(x_{i,b})
}\ket{ \WPRF.\Eval(\WPRF.\sk_i(x_{i,b}),s_i) }

(184)

\displaystyle=\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}}q_{i,b,z^{*}}\ket{x_{i,b}}\ket{\st_{i,b,z^*}}\ket{
\WPRF.\sk_i(x_{i,b})
}\ket{ \WPRF.\Eval(\WPRF.\msk,s_i) }.

(185)

Here, we use the fact that
\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}(x_{i,b}),s_{i})={\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk},s_{i})}
holds for
all

x
with overwhelming probability over the randomness of
\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{KG}
by the evaluation correctness of
\mathsf{WUPF}
.
By measuring the last register, one can get
\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk},s_{i})
as desired.
∎

7.2
Security Proof

Theorem 7.2.

If
\mathsf{NTCF}
satisfies cut-and-choose adaptive hardcore bit security,

\mathsf{SFE}
satisfies the mode-indistinguishability and the extractability in the extraction mode, and
\mathsf{WUPF}
satisfies unpredictability as a plain UPF and parallel mark extractability,
then our construction is UPF-VRA secure.

Proof.

We prove the theorem by a sequence of hybrids.
For the sake of the contradiction, let us assume an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
that wins the UPF-VRA security game with non-negligible probability
\epsilon
.
In the following, the event that
\mathpzc{A}
wins in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{\rm xx}
is denoted by
\mathsf{E}_{\rm xx}
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
:

This is the original UPF-VRA security game between the challenger and an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
.
Namely, the game proceeds as follows.

1.

The challenger computes the first message to the adversary as follows.

•

For
i\in[2n]
, it runs
\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})\to(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{xk}_{i})
.

•

It chooses random subset
S
of
[2n]
with size
n
.

•

It runs
(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{FuncGen}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{mode})
and
(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{CRSGen}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{mode})
for
i\in[2n]
, where
\mathsf{mode}=1
if
i\in S
and
\mathsf{mode}=2
if
i\in\overline{S}
.

Finally, it sends
\mathsf{pp}=\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.
It privately keeps the master secret key
\mathsf{msk}=\{\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
and the deletion verification key
\mathsf{dvk}=\left(S,\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{S}}\right)
.

2.

Until
\mathpzc{A}
receives the challenge input at Step
7
of the game,
\mathpzc{A}
has an access to the evaluation oracle
\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)
, which takes as input
\hat{s}=\hat{s}_{1}\|\cdots\|\hat{s}_{2n}\in\{0,1\}^{2n\ell}
and returns
\hat{t}=\hat{t}_{1}\|\cdots\|\hat{t}_{2n}
, where
\hat{t}_{i}=\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},\hat{s}_{i})
.

3.

The adversary
\mathpzc{A}
sends
\{y_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to the challenger.

4.

The challenger computes the message to the adversary as follows.
It first runs

\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Send}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i},C[\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},y_{i}])\rightarrow\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}

(186)

for
i\in[2n]
. Then, it sends
\{\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

5.

Then,
\mathpzc{A}
submits the deletion certificate

\mathsf{cert}=\{d_{i},c_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
.

6.

Then, the challenger runs
\mathsf{DelVrfy}
as follows.

•

For
i\in\overline{S}
, it runs
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{StaRcv}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i})\to\{\alpha_{i,j}^{b}\}_{j\in[w],b\in\{0,1\}}
.

•

For
i\in\overline{S}
, it computes

d^{*}_{i}=(d^{*}_{i}[1],\ldots,d^{*}_{i}[w])\in\{0,1\}^{w}
as

d^{*}_{i}[j]\coloneqq c_{i,j}\cdot(\alpha^{0}_{i,j}\oplus\alpha^{1}_{i,j})
.

•

For
i\in\overline{S}
, it runs
(x_{i,0},x_{i,1})\leftarrow\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Invert}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},y_{i})
.
If the inversion fails for any of
i\in\overline{S}
, the output of
\mathsf{DelVrfy}
is
\bot
.

•

For
i\in\overline{S}
, it checks whether

\langle d_{i}\oplus d^{*}_{i},x_{i,0}\oplus x_{i,1}\rangle=0~\land~\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{GoodSet}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},y_{i},d_{i}\oplus d^{*}_{i})=\top

(187)

holds using
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i}
.
If
Equation

187
holds for all
i\in\overline{S}
, the output is
\top
and otherwise,
\bot
.

If the output of the algorithm is
\bot
, then it indicates that the output of the game is
0
(i.e.,
\mathpzc{A}
failed to win the game).
Otherwise, the challenger returns

\mathsf{dvk}
to
\mathpzc{A}

and continues the game.

7.

Then, the challenger chooses a random input
s=s_{1}\|s_{2}\|\cdots\|s_{2n}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{2n\ell}
and sends
s
to
\mathpzc{A}
.
After receiving the challenge input,
\mathpzc{A}
looses its oracle access to the evaluation oracle.

8.

\mathpzc{A}
then outputs
t^{\prime}=t^{\prime}_{1}\|\cdots\|t^{\prime}_{2n}
.
The adversary wins the game if
t^{\prime}=t
.

By the definition, we have
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{0}]=\epsilon
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
:

In this hybrid, the challenger computes
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}
differently for
i\in S
in Step
4
of the game. Namely, it is replaced with the following:

4’

The challenger computes the second message to the adversary as follows.

•

It runs

\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Ext}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i})\to x_{i}

and
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Check}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},x_{i},y_{i})=\mathsf{vrd}_{i}
for
i\in S
.

•

It computes

\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}\leftarrow\begin{cases}\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Sim}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i},\gamma_{i})&\text{if }i\in S\\
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Send}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i},C[\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},y_{i}])&\text{if }i\in\overline{S}\end{cases},

(188)

where

\gamma_{i}=\begin{cases}\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},x_{i})=\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}(x_{i})&\text{if }\mathsf{vrd}_{i}=\top\\
\bot&\text{if }\mathsf{vrd}_{i}=\bot.\end{cases}

(189)

Finally, it sends
\{\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

Since we have
\gamma_{i}=C[\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},y_{i}](x_{i})
, it follows that this game is indistinguishability from the previous game by the straightforward reduction to the extractability of
\mathsf{SFE}
in the extraction mode.
Thus, we have
|\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{0}]-\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{1}]|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.
Note that in this hybrid, no marked key of
\mathsf{WUPF}
is necessary for running the game for
i
such that
i\in S
and
\mathsf{vrd}_{i}=\bot
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
:

In this hybrid, the challenger aborts the game and sets the output of the game to be
0
(i.e.,
\mathpzc{A}
looses the game), once it turns out that there is
i\in S
such that
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Check}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},x_{i},y_{i})=\bot
.
Namely, we replace Step
4’
in the previous hybrid with the following:

4”

The challenger computes the second message to the adversary as follows.

•

It runs

\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Ext}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i})\to x_{i}

and
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Check}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},x_{i},y_{i})=\mathsf{vrd}_{i}
for
i\in S
.

•

If
\mathsf{vrd}_{i}=\bot
for any of
i\in S
, it aborts the game and sets the output of the game to be
0
.

•

It computes

\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}\leftarrow\begin{cases}\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Sim}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i},\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}(x_{i}))&\text{if }i\in S\\
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Send}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i},C[\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},y_{i}])&\text{if }i\in\overline{S}.\end{cases}

(190)

Finally, it sends
\{\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

We claim
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{2}]\geq\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{1}]-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.
To show this, we observe that the only scenario where the adversary
\mathpzc{A}
wins the game in the previous hybrid but not in the current one occurs when
\mathpzc{A}
chooses
y_{i}
such that
\mathsf{vrd}_{i}=\bot
for some
i\in S
, yet still succeeds in predicting
t
.
However, this implies that
\mathpzc{A}
has predicted the value of
\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},s_{i})
without getting any marked key for the
i
-th instance of
\mathsf{WUPF}
.
Note that the probability that
\mathpzc{A}
has made an evaluation query to
\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},\cdot)
on input
s_{i}
is negligible, since
s_{i}
is chosen uniformly at random and
\mathpzc{A}
is not allowed to make evaluation queries once it receives
s_{i}
.
By a straightforward reduction to the unpredicatability of
\mathsf{WUPF}
, the claim follows.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
:

In this hybrid, we change the winning condition of the adversary.
Namely, we replace Step
7
and Step
8
of the game with the following.

7’

The challenger parses the adversary
\mathpzc{A}
right before Step
7
of the game as a unitary circuit
U{A}
and a quantum state
\rho{A}
.
It then constructs a quantum predictor
\mathpzc{P}
for
\mathsf{WUPF}
that takes as input
\{s_{i}\}_{i\in S}
as input and outputs
\{t^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in S}

from
\mathpzc{A}
as follows.

\mathpzc{P}(\{s_{i}\}_{i\in S})
:

It chooses
s_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}
for
i\in\overline{S}
and runs
\mathpzc{A}
on input
\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to obtain
\{t^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in 2n}
.
Finally, it outputs
\{t^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in S}
.

8’

The challenger parses

\mathpzc{P}
as a pair of unitary
U{P}
and a quantum state
\rho{P}
.
Then, it runs the extraction algorithm as

\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathpzc{Ext}(\{\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{xk}_{i}\}_{i\in S},(U{P},\rho{P}))\to\{x^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in S}
.

9’

If
x_{i}=x^{\prime}_{i}
holds for all
i\in S
,
the challenger sets the output of the game to be
1
.
Otherwise, it sets it to be
0
.

As we show in
Lemma

7.3
,
we have
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{3}]\geq\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{2}]-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)

by the parallel extractability of
\mathsf{WUPF}
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
:

In this game, we replace Step
9’
of the above game with the following:

9”

If
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Check}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},x^{\prime}_{i},y_{i})=\top
holds for all
i\in S
,
the challenger sets the output of the game to be
1
.
Otherwise, it sets it to be
0
.

We observe that
x^{\prime}_{i}=x_{i}
in Step
9’
implies
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Check}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},x^{\prime}_{i},y_{i})=\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Check}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},x_{i},y_{i})=\top
.
To see the latter equality holds, recall that the game is set to abort if
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Check}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},x_{i},y_{i})=\bot
for any
i\in S

in Step
4”
. Thus, if the game continues to Step
9’
, the equality holds.
We therefore have
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{4}]\geq\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{3}]
.
(Actually,
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{4}]=\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{3}]
holds due to the uniqueness of
x_{i}
that passes the check. However, showing the inequality suffices for our proof.)

\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}
:

In this game, we switch Step
4”
in the previous hybrid back to Step
4’
.
The difference from the previous hybrid is that it continues the game even if
\mathsf{vrd}_{i}=\bot
for some
i\in S
.
Since the adversary had no chance in winning the game in such a case in the previous hybrid, this change only increases the chance of the adversary winning.
We therefore have
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{5}]\geq\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{4}]
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}
:

In this hybrid, we switch Step
4’
in the previous hybrid back to Step
4
.
Namely, it computes
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Send}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\allowbreak\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i},C[\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},y_{i}])\rightarrow\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}
for all
i\in[2n]
again.
Similarly to the change from
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
, the winning probability of
\mathpzc{A}
in this game does not change significantly by the extractability of
\mathsf{SFE}
in the extraction mode. Namely, we have
|\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{6}]-\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{5}]|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.
Note that we do not need
\{\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i}\}_{i\in S}
any more to run the game.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{7}
:

In this hybrid, we change the way
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i}
is sampled for
i\in S
.
Namely, it is sampled as
(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{CRSGen}(1^{\lambda},2)
for all
i\in[2n]
.
Concretely, we replace Step
1
with the following:

1’

The challenger computes the first message to the adversary as follows.

•

For
i\in[2n]
, it runs
\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})\to(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{xk}_{i})
.

•

It chooses random subset
S
of
[2n]
with size
n
.

•

It runs
(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{FuncGen}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{mode})
and
(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{CRSGen}(1^{\lambda},2)
for
i\in[2n]
, where
\mathsf{mode}=1
if
i\in S
and
\mathsf{mode}=2
if
i\in\overline{S}
.

Finally, it sends
\mathsf{pp}=\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.
It privately keeps the master secret key
\mathsf{msk}=\{\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
and the deletion verification key
\mathsf{dvk}=\left(S,\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{S}}\right)
.

Since we do not need
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i}
for
i\in S
to run
\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{7}
, a straightforward reduction to the mode indistinguishability of
\mathsf{SFE}
implies that
\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{7}
are computationally indistinguishable.
Therefore, we have
|\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{6}]-\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{7}]|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{8}
:

In this game, we change the way how the challenger runs
\mathsf{DelVrfy}
.
In particular, the challenger computes
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{StaRcv}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{ct}_{i})\to\{\alpha_{i,j}^{b}\}_{j,b}
and
d^{*}_{i}
for all
i\in[2n]
,
instead of only for
i\in\overline{S}
.
Still, the inversion of
y_{i}
and
the check of
Equation

187
are performed only for
i\in\overline{S}
.
Concretely, we replace Step
6
with the following.

6’

Then, the challenger runs (the modified version of)
\mathsf{DelVrfy}
as follows.

•

For
i\in[2n]
, it runs
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{StaRcv}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i})\to\{\alpha_{i,j}^{b}\}_{j\in[w],b\in\{0,1\}}
.

•

For
i\in[2n]
, it computes

d^{*}_{i}=(d^{*}_{i}[1],\ldots,d^{*}_{i}[w])\in\{0,1\}^{w}
as

d^{*}_{i}[j]\coloneqq c_{i,j}\cdot(\alpha^{0}_{i,j}\oplus\alpha^{1}_{i,j})
.

•

For
i\in\overline{S}
, it runs
(x_{i,0},x_{i,1})\leftarrow\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Invert}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},y_{i})
.
If the inversion fails for any of
i\in\overline{S}
, the output of
\mathsf{DelVrfy}
is
\bot
.

•

For
i\in\overline{S}
, it checks whether

e_{i}=(d_{i}\oplus d^{*}_{i})\cdot(x_{i,0}\oplus x_{i,1})~\land~\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{GoodSet}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},y_{i},d_{i}\oplus d^{*}_{i})=\top

(191)

holds using
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i}
.
If the above holds for all
i\in\overline{S}
, the output is
\top
and otherwise,
\bot
.

If the output of the algorithm is
\bot
, then it indicates that the output of the game is
0
(i.e.,
\mathpzc{A}
failed to win the game).
Otherwise, the challenger returns

\mathsf{dvk}=\left(S,\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{S}}\right)
to
\mathpzc{A}

and continues the game.

We can see that this change is only conceptual, since the outcome of the verification is unchanged.
We may therefore have

\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{8}]=\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{7}]
.

Based on the above discussion, it is established that

\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{8}]\geq\epsilon-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
, which is non-negligible by our assumption.
However, we can show
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{8}]={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
assuming the cut-and-choose adaptive hardcore bit property of
\mathsf{NTCF}
, by a very similar reduction to
Lemma

6.4
.
This results in a contradiction as desired.
∎

It remains to prove
Lemma

7.3
.

Lemma 7.3.

If
\mathsf{WUPF}
satisfies parallel extractability, we have
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{3}]\geq\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{2}]-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

Proof.

We consider an adversary
\mathpzc{B}
who plays the role of the challenger in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
for
\mathpzc{A}
, while working as an adversary against the parallel predictability experiment with the number of instances
n
. It proceeds as follows:

1.

At the beginning of the game, the challenger runs
\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}^{\prime}_{j},\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{xk}^{\prime}_{j})
for
j\in[n]
.

\mathpzc{B}
is then given oracle access to
\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}^{\prime}_{j},\cdot)
for each
j\in[n]
.

\mathpzc{B}
proceeds as follows:

•

\mathpzc{B}
chooses random subset
S=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}\}
of
[2n]
with size
n
.

•

It implicitly sets

\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}_{i_{j}}=\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}^{\prime}_{j}
and
\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{xk}_{i_{j}}=\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{xk}^{\prime}_{j}
for
j\in[n]
.

•

It runs
\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{xk}_{i})
for
i\in\overline{S}
.

•

It chooses
\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
and
\{\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
as in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
.
Finally,
\mathpzc{B}
sends
\mathsf{pp}=\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

2.

Until
\mathpzc{A}
receives the challenge input from
\mathpzc{B}
,
\mathpzc{A}
has an access to the evaluation oracle
\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)
throughout the game.
To answer the query on input
\hat{s}=\hat{s}_{1}\|\cdots\|\hat{s}_{2n}\in\{0,1\}^{2n\ell}
,

\mathpzc{B}
computes
\hat{t}_{i}=\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},\hat{s}_{i})
by itself if
i\in\overline{S}
and by making an evaluation query to the oracle
\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},\cdot)
on input
\hat{s}_{i}
if
i\in S
.
Then it returns
\hat{t}=\hat{t}_{1}\|\cdots\|\hat{t}_{2n}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

3.

At some point,
\mathpzc{A}
sends
\{y_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to
\mathpzc{B}
.

\mathpzc{B}
then chooses its initial message to its challenger by the following steps.

•

It then computes
x_{j}
and
\mathsf{vrd}_{j}
for
j\in S
using the trapdoors for
\mathsf{NTCF}
and
\mathsf{SFE}
as in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
. If
\mathsf{vrd}_{j}=\bot
for any of
j\in S
, it aborts.

•

It then submits
\{x^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{n}\}\coloneqq\{x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}}\}
to its challenger.

4.

Given
\{\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{dk}^{\prime}_{j}(x^{\prime}_{j})\}_{j\in[n]}
from the challenger,
\mathpzc{B}
proceeds as follows.

•

It sets
\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{sk}_{i_{j}}(x_{i_{j}})\coloneqq\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{dk}^{\prime}_{j}(x^{\prime}_{j})
for
j\in[n]
.

•

It computes
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}
as follows:

\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}\leftarrow\begin{cases}\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Sim}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i},\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{dk}_{i}(x_{i}))&\text{if }i\in S\\
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Send}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i},C[\mathsf{WPKE}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},y_{i}])&\text{if }i\in\overline{S}.\end{cases}

(192)

•

\mathpzc{B}
then sends
\{\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

•

Then,
\mathpzc{A}
submits the deletion certificate
\mathsf{cert}
to
\mathpzc{B}
.

\mathpzc{B}
runs
\mathsf{DelVrfy}
using the trapdoors for
\mathsf{NTCF}
and
\mathsf{SFE}
.
If the output is
\bot
,
\mathpzc{B}
also outputs
\bot
and aborts.
Otherwise,
\mathpzc{B}
returns

\mathsf{dvk}=\left(S,\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{S}}\right)
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

5.

Then, the challenger sends
\{s^{\prime}_{j}\}_{j\in[n]}
to
\mathpzc{B}
.

6.

\mathpzc{B}
computes its output as follows.

•

It sets
s_{i_{j}}\coloneqq s^{\prime}_{j}
for
j\in[n]
and samples
s_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{v}
for
i\in\overline{S}
.

•

It then sends
\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

•

\mathpzc{A}
output
(t^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,t^{\prime}_{2n})
.
It then outputs
(t^{\prime}_{i_{1}},\ldots,t^{\prime}_{i_{n}})
as its output.

We can see that
\mathpzc{B}
defined above perfectly simulates
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
for
\mathpzc{A}
above
and
\mathpzc{B}
wins the game if so does
\mathpzc{A}
.
We therefore have
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{WUPF},\mathpzc{B},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{pre}}(\lambda)\geq\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{2}]
.
Furthermore, by the parallel extractability of
\mathsf{WUPF}
, we have

\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{WUPF},\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{Ext},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ext}}(\lambda)\geq\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{WUPF},\mathpzc{B},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{pre}}(\lambda)-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.
We can also see that
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{3}]=\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{WUPF},\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{Ext},n}^{\mathsf{par}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ext}}(\lambda)
holds, because the way
\mathpzc{B}
predicts the outputs is exactly the same as the way
\mathpzc{P}
does in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
.
By combining these equations, the lemma follows.
∎

8
DS-SKL with Classical Lessor

In this section, we show the construction of DS-SKL with a classical lessor from the LWE assumption.

8.1
Construction

We construct a DS-SKL scheme with a classical lessor that satisfies RUF-VRA security as per
Definition

5.23
.
The construction is very similar to our PKE-SKL construction, where we replace the watermarkable PKE with watermarkable DS.
Concretely, we use the following ingredients:

•

NTCF generators
\mathsf{NTCF}=\mathsf{NTCF}.(\mathsf{FuncGen},\mathpzc{StateGen},\mathsf{Check},\mathsf{Invert})
defined as in
Section

2.2
.
It is associated with a deterministic algorithm
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{GoodSet}
(See
Definition

2.10
).
We assume that
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Check}
is a deterministic algorithm.
We denote the input space by
\mathcal{X}
and assume
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\{0,1\}^{w}
for some polynomial
w=w(\lambda)
.
It is known that NTCF generators can be constructed from LWE.

•

Special Dual-mode SFE
\mathsf{SFE}=\mathsf{SFE}.(\mathsf{Rec}_{1},\mathsf{Send},\mathsf{Rec}_{2})
that supports circuits with input space
\{0,1\}^{w}
as defined in
Section

4
.
It is associated with an extraction algorithm
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Ext}
, a simulation algorithm
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Sim}
, a state recovery algorithm
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{StaRcv}
,
and a ”coherent version"
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathpzc{Rec}_{1}
of
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Rec}_{1}
, which has the efficient state superposition property.
We require that
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Rec}_{2}
and
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Ext}
are deterministic.
We denote that the length of each block
\alpha_{i,j}^{b}
output by
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{StaRcv}
by
u
.
As we show in
Appendix

A
, this can be constructed from LWE.

•

Watermarkable DS (with parallel extraction):

\mathsf{WDS}=\mathsf{WDS}.(\mathsf{KG},\mathsf{Mark},\mathsf{Sign},\mathsf{Vrfy})
as defined in
Section

3.3
.
Without loss of generality,
we assume that this satisfies EUF-CMA security as a plain DS (See
Remark

3.9
).
It is associated with a parallel mark extraction algorithm
\mathsf{WDS}.\mathpzc{Ext}
.
We require that
\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{Mark}
be a deterministic algorithm. This can be assumed without loss of generality because the randomness needed to run

\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{Mark}
can be included into
\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{msk}
.
We assume that it has coherent-signability and thus it is associated with a coherent signing quantum algorithm
\mathsf{WDS}.\mathpzc{QSign}
.
We need the mark space to be
\{0,1\}^{w}
and denote the message space by
\{0,1\}^{\ell}
.
The bit length of the marked signing key is denoted by
v
.
As we show in
Section

3.3
, this can be constructed from the SIS assumption.

Below is the description of the construction.

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
:

On input the security parameter, it proceeds as follows.

•

For
i\in[2n]
, run
\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})\to(\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{vk}_{i},\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{xk}_{i})
.

•

Choose a uniform subset
S\subseteq[2n]
such that
|S|=n
.
In the following, we denote
[2n]\backslash S
by
\overline{S}
.

•

For
i\in[2n]
, run
(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{FuncGen}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{mode})
and
(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{CRSGen}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{mode})
, where

\mathsf{mode}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $i\in S$}\\
2&\text{if $i\in\overline{S}$}.\end{cases}

•

Output
\mathsf{svk}=\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{vk}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
,

\mathsf{msk}=\{\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{msk}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
, and

\mathsf{dvk}=(S,\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\allowbreak\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{S}})
.

\mathpzc{IntKeyGen}(\mathsf{svk},\mathsf{msk})
:

The lessor and lessee run the following interactive protocol to generate
\mathpzc{sigk}
.
Here, the lessor takes
\mathsf{msk}
as input and the lessee takes
\mathsf{svk}
as input.

Lessee’s first operation:

The lessee parses
\mathsf{svk}=\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{vk}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
and runs the following algorithms.

•

For each
i\in[2n]
, run
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathpzc{StateGen}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i})\rightarrow(y_{i},\ket{\psi_i})
.

•

For each
i\in[2n]
, run
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathpzc{Rec}_{1}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\ket{\psi_i})\to(\ket{\psi'_i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}_{i}^{(1)})
.

•

Send
\{y_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to the lessor.

Lessor’s operation:

Given the message
\{y_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
from the lessee, the lessor proceeds as follows.

•

For
i\in[2n]
, define
C[\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},y_{i}]
to be a circuit that takes
x
as input and computes the following:

\displaystyle C[\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},y_{i}](x)=\begin{cases}\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},x)&\text{if $\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Check}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},x,y_{i})=\top$}\\
\bot&\text{if $\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Check}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},x,y_{i})=\bot$}.\\
\end{cases}

(193)

•

For
i\in[2n]
, run
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Send}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i},C[\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},y_{i}])\rightarrow\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}
.

•

Send
\{\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to the lessee.

Lessee’s second operation:

Given the message
\{\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
, the lessee proceeds as follows.
It uses the secret state
\ket{\psi'_i}
in the following.

•

Construct the unitary
U[\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}_{i}^{(2)}]
defined by the following map:

\ket{x}\ket{\SFE.\st}\ket{0^{\lenmsg}}\mapsto\ket{x}\ket{\SFE.\st}\ket{ \SFE.\Receive{2}(\SFE.\crs_i, x, \SFE.\st, \SFE.\msg_i^{(2)}) }.

(194)

•

For
i\in[2n]
, apply the unitary to compute

\ket{\phi_i}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}}U[\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}_{i}^{(2)}]\left(\ket{\psi'_i}\ket{0^{\lenmsg}}\right).

(195)

Final output:

The lessee privately obtains a secret key

\mathpzc{sigk}=\bigotimes_{i\in[2n]}\ket{\phi_i}\ket{\SFE.\crs_i}\ket{\SFE.\msg^{(2)}_{i}}.

(196)

\mathsf{Sign}(\mathsf{msk},m):

Upon receiving
\mathsf{msk}=\{\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{msk}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
and an input
m=m_{1}\|m_{2}\|\ldots\|m_{2n}\in\{0,1\}^{2n\ell}
,
compute
\mathsf{WDS}.\sigma_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},m_{i})
for
i\in[2n]
and output
\sigma=(\mathsf{WDS}.\sigma_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{WDS}.\sigma_{2n})
.

\mathpzc{Sign}(\mathpzc{sigk},m)
:

Given
\mathpzc{sigk}=\bigotimes_{i\in[2n]}\ket{\phi_i}\ket{\SFE.\crs_i}\ket{\SFE.\msg^{(2)}_{i}}
and an input
m=m_{1}\|m_{2}\|\ldots\|m_{2n}
, the evaluation algorithm performs the following for each of
i\in[2n]
.
Then, it runs

\mathsf{WDS}.\mathpzc{QSign}(\ket{\phi_i},m_{i})\to(\ket{\phi'_i},\mathsf{WDS}.\sigma_{i})

(197)

for
i\in[2n]
.
It finally outputs

\mathpzc{sigk}^{\prime}=\bigotimes_{i\in[2n]}\ket{\phi'_i}\ket{\SFE.\crs_i}\ket{\SFE.\msg^{(2)}_{i}}

and
\sigma=(\mathsf{WDS}.\sigma_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{WDS}.\sigma_{2n})
.

\mathsf{SigVrfy}(\mathsf{svk},m,\sigma)\rightarrow\top/\bot
:

Given
\mathsf{svk}=\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{vk}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
,
m=m_{1}\|m_{2}\|\ldots\|m_{2n}
, and
\sigma=(\mathsf{WDS}.\sigma_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{WDS}.\sigma_{2n})
,
it outputs
\top
if
\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{vk},m_{i},\mathsf{WDS}.\sigma_{i})=\top
holds for all
i\in[2n]
. Otherwise, it outputs
\bot
.

\mathsf{Del}(\mathpzc{sigk})
:

Given
\mathpzc{sigk}=\bigotimes_{i\in[2n]}\ket{\phi_i}\ket{\SFE.\crs_i}\ket{\SFE.\msg^{(2)}_{i}}
, the deletion algorithm performs the following for each of
i\in[2n]
.

•

Measure the corresponding registers to recover the classical strings
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i}
and
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}
.

•

Compute
U[\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}_{i}^{(2)}]^{\dagger}\ket{\phi_i}

and remove the last
v
qubits to obtain
\ket{\psi''_i}
.

•

Measure
\ket{\psi''_i}
in Hadmard basis to obtain
(d_{i},c_{i})
, where each
d_{i}\in\{0,1\}^{w}
is obtained by measuring the register corresponding to an element in
\mathcal{X}

and each
c_{i}\in\{0,1\}^{uw}
is obtained by measuring the register corresponding to the SFE state.

The final output of the algorithm is given by
\mathsf{cert}=\{d_{i},c_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
,

\mathsf{DelVrfy}(\mathsf{dvk},\mathsf{cert})
:

Given the deletion verification key
\mathsf{dvk}=\left(S,\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{S}}\right)
and the deletion certificate
\mathsf{cert}=\{d_{i},c_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
,
the deletion verification algorithm works as follows.

•

For
i\in\overline{S}
, run
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Invert}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},y_{i})\to(x_{i,0},x_{i,1})
.
If the inversion fails for any of
i
, output
\bot
.

•

For
i\in\overline{S}
, run
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{StaRcv}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i})\to\{\alpha_{i,j}^{b}\}_{j\in[w],b\in\{0,1\}}
, where
\alpha_{i,j}^{b}\in\{0,1\}^{u}
.

•

For
i\in\overline{S}
, compute the vector
d^{*}_{i}=(d^{*}_{i}[1],\ldots,d^{*}_{i}[w])\in\{0,1\}^{w}
as

d^{*}_{i}[j]\coloneqq\langle c_{i,j},(\alpha^{0}_{i,j}\oplus\alpha^{1}_{i,j})\rangle,

(198)

where
c_{i}
is parsed as
c_{i}=c_{i,1}\|\cdots\|c_{i,w}
, where each block is in
\{0,1\}^{u}
.

•

For
i\in\overline{S}
, check whether

\langle d_{i}\oplus d^{*}_{i},(x_{i,0}\oplus x_{i,1})\rangle=0~\land~\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{GoodSet}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},y_{i},d_{i}\oplus d^{*}_{i})=\top

(199)

holds.
If it holds for all
i\in\overline{S}
, it outputs
\top
.
Otherwise, it outputs
\bot
.

Correctness

The following theorem asserts the signature verification correctness,
the reusability with static signing keys, and the deletion verification correctness of the above scheme.

Theorem 8.1.

The above construction satisfies the signature verification correctness if

\mathsf{NTCF}
satisfies the state generation correctness,
\mathsf{SFE}
satisfies evaluation correctness, and
\mathsf{WDS}
satisfies the coherent signability.
Under the same condition, the construction satisfies the reusability with static signing keys.
Furthermore, the construction satisfies the deletion verification correctness if
\mathsf{NTCF}
satisfies the state generation correctness,
\mathsf{SFE}
satisfies the statistical security against malicious senders,
and
\mathsf{SFE}
supports state recovery in the hiding mode.

Proof.

We omit the proof of deletion verification correctness, since it is completely the same as that for PKE-SKL shown in

Theorem

6.1
.
We therefore show the signature verification correctness and the reusability with static signing keys.
It is straightforward to see that a signature generated by
\mathsf{msk}
passes the verification.
We therefore consider the case where a signature is generated by
\mathpzc{sigk}
.
As observed in the proof for
Theorem

6.1
,
all the algorithms run the subsystems indexed by
i\in[2n]
, which run in parallel.
Therefore, it suffices to show that each
\mathsf{WDS}.\sigma_{i}
passes the verification with overwhelming probability and generation of
\mathsf{WDS}.\sigma_{i}
almost does not change the state
\ket{\phi_i}
, assuming that the lessee follows the protocol honestly.

By the same argument as
Theorem

6.1
, we can represent

\ket{\psi'_i}
as

\ket{\psi'_i}=q_{i,0,z^{*}}\ket{x_{i,0}}\ket{\st_{i,0,z^*}}+q_{i,1,z^{*}}\ket{x_{i,1}}\ket{\st_{i,1,z^*}}
,
where
(x_{i,0},x_{i,1})=\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Invert}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},y_{i})
, where
z^{*}=\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i}
, and
\mathsf{st}_{i,b,z^{*}}
is the unique state corresponding to

x_{i,b}
and
z^{*}=\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i}
.
The definition of
q_{i,b,z^{*}}
is not relevant here, but it can be found in the proof of
Theorem

6.1
.
By the evaluation correctness of
\mathsf{SFE}
, we have
24
24

24

Precisely, the equation does not hold in strict sense, since we ignore negligible difference in trace distance in the proof of
Theorem

6.1
. However, this can be ignored as this difference only negligibly affects the probability that the verification passes.

\displaystyle\ket{\phi_i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}}q_{i,b,z^{*}}\ket{x_{i,b}}\ket{\st_{i,b,z^*}}\ket{ \SFE.\Receive{2}(\SFE.\crs_i, x_{i,b}, \st_{i,b,z^*}, \SFE.\msg_i^{(2)}) }

(200)

\displaystyle=\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}}q_{i,b,z^{*}}\ket{x_{i,b}}\ket{\st_{i,b,z^*}}\ket{
\WDS.\sk_i(x_{i,b})
}.

(201)

Then,
\mathsf{WDS}.\mathpzc{QSign}
is applied on
\ket{\phi_i}
and
m_{i}
.
Let us consider
L=u+w
and consider
\{\alpha_{t}\in\mathbb{C}\}_{t\in\{0,1\}^{L}}
and
\{x^{\prime}_{t}\in\{0,1\}^{w}\}_{t\in\{0,1\}^{L}}
indexed by
t
such that

\displaystyle\alpha_{t}=\begin{cases}q_{i,b,z^{*}}&\text{if }t=x_{i,b}\|\mathsf{st}_{i,b,z^{*}}~\text{for some }b\in\{0,1\}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases},\quad x^{\prime}_{t}=\begin{cases}x_{i,b}&\text{if }t=x_{i,b}\|\mathsf{st}_{i,b,z^{*}}~\text{for some }b\in\{0,1\}\\
x^{*}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

(202)

where
x^{*}
is an arbitrary string that is different from
x_{i,0}
and
x_{i,1}
.
Then, we can write
\ket{\phi_i}
as
\ket{\phi_i}=\sum_{t\in\{0,1\}^{L}}\alpha_{t}\ket{t}\ket{\WDS.\sk_i(x'_{t})}
.
By the first property of coherent signability satisfied by
\mathsf{WDS}
,

\mathsf{WDS}.\sigma_{i}
is in
\cup_{t}\mathrm{Supp}(\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{Sign}(\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}(x_{t})))
.
By the correctness of
\mathsf{WDS}
,
\mathsf{WDS}.\sigma_{i}
passes the veirification.
Furthermore, by the second property of coherent signability satisfied by
\mathsf{WDS}
,

\ket{\phi'_i}
obtained by applying
\mathsf{WDS}.\mathpzc{QSign}
is negligibly close to
\ket{\phi_i}
in trace distance.
∎

8.2
Security Proof

The following theorem asserts the security of the above construction.

Theorem 8.2.

If
\mathsf{NTCF}
satisfies cut-and-choose adaptive hardcore bit security,

\mathsf{SFE}
satisfies the mode-indistinguishability and the extractability in the extraction mode, and
\mathsf{WDS}
satisfies parallel mark extractability and EUF-CMA security as a plain DS,
then our construction is RUF-VRA secure.

Proof.

We prove the theorem by a sequence of hybrids.
For the sake of the contradiction, let us assume an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
that wins the RUF-VRA security game with non-negligible probability
\epsilon
.
In the following, the event that
\mathpzc{A}
wins in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{\rm xx}
is denoted by
\mathsf{E}_{\rm xx}
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
:

This is the original RUF-VRA security game between the challenger and an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
.
Namely, the game proceeds as follows.

1.

The challenger computes the first message to the adversary as follows.

•

For
i\in[2n]
, it runs
\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})\to(\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{vk}_{i},\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{xk}_{i})
.

•

It chooses random subset
S
of
[2n]
with size
n
.

•

It runs
(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{FuncGen}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{mode})
and
(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{CRSGen}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{mode})
for
i\in[2n]
, where
\mathsf{mode}=1
if
i\in S
and
\mathsf{mode}=2
if
i\in\overline{S}
.

Finally, it sends
\mathsf{svk}=\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{vk}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.
It privately keeps the master secret key
\mathsf{msk}=\{\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{msk}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
and the deletion verification key
\mathsf{dvk}=\left(S,\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{S}}\right)
.

2.

Until
\mathpzc{A}
receives the challenge input at Step
7
of the game,
\mathpzc{A}
has an access to the signing oracle
\mathsf{Sign}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)
, which takes as input
\hat{m}=\hat{m}_{1}\|\cdots\|\hat{m}_{2n}\in\{0,1\}^{2n\ell}
and returns
\hat{\sigma}=\mathsf{WDS}.\hat{\sigma}_{1}\|\cdots\|\mathsf{WDS}.\hat{\sigma}_{2n}
, where
\mathsf{WDS}.\hat{\sigma}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{Sign}(\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},\hat{m}_{i})
.

3.

The adversary
\mathpzc{A}
sends
\{y_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to the challenger.

4.

The challenger computes the message to the adversary as follows.
It first runs

\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Send}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i},C[\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},y_{i}])\rightarrow\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}

(203)

for
i\in[2n]
.
Then, it sends
\{\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

5.

Then,
\mathpzc{A}
submits the deletion certificate

\mathsf{cert}=\{d_{i},c_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
.

6.

Then, the challenger runs
\mathsf{DelVrfy}
as follows.

•

For
i\in\overline{S}
, it runs
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{StaRcv}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i})\to\{\alpha_{i,j}^{b}\}_{j\in[w],b\in\{0,1\}}
.

•

For
i\in\overline{S}
, it computes

d^{*}_{i}=(d^{*}_{i}[1],\ldots,d^{*}_{i}[w])\in\{0,1\}^{w}
as

d^{*}_{i}[j]\coloneqq c_{i,j}\cdot(\alpha^{0}_{i,j}\oplus\alpha^{1}_{i,j})
.

•

For
i\in\overline{S}
, it runs
(x_{i,0},x_{i,1})\leftarrow\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Invert}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},y_{i})
.
If the inversion fails for any of
i\in\overline{S}
, the output of
\mathsf{DelVrfy}
is
\bot
.

•

For
i\in\overline{S}
, it checks whether

(d_{i}\oplus d^{*}_{i})\cdot(x_{i,0}\oplus x_{i,1})=0~\land~\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{GoodSet}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},y_{i},d_{i}\oplus d^{*}_{i})=\top

(204)

holds using
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i}
.
If
Equation

204
holds for all
i\in\overline{S}
, the output is
\top
and otherwise,
\bot
.

If the output of the algorithm is
\bot
, then it indicates that the output of the game is
0
(i.e.,
\mathpzc{A}
failed to win the game).
Otherwise, the challenger returns

\mathsf{dvk}
to
\mathpzc{A}

and continues the game.

7.

Then, the challenger chooses a random input
m=m_{1}\|m_{2}\|\cdots\|m_{2n}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{2n\ell}
and sends
m
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

After receiving the challenge input,
\mathpzc{A}
looses its oracle access to the signing oracle.

8.

\mathpzc{A}
then outputs
\sigma^{\prime}=\mathsf{WDS}.\sigma^{\prime}_{1}\|\cdots\|\mathsf{WDS}.\sigma^{\prime}_{2n}
.
The challenger sets the output of the game to be
1
(i.e., indicating that
\mathpzc{A}
wins the game) if
\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{vk}_{i},m_{i},\mathsf{WDS}.\sigma^{\prime}_{i})=\top
holds for all
i\in[2n]
. Otherwise, it outputs
\bot
.

By the definition, we have
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{0}]=\epsilon
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
:

In this hybrid, the challenger computes
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}
differently for
i\in S
in Step
4
of the game. Namely, it is replaced with the following:

4’

The challenger computes the second message to the adversary as follows.

•

It runs

\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Ext}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i})\to x_{i}

and
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Check}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},x_{i},y_{i})=\mathsf{vrd}_{i}
for
i\in S
.

•

It computes

\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}\leftarrow\begin{cases}\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Sim}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i},\gamma_{i})&\text{if }i\in S\\
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Send}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i},C[\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},y_{i}])&\text{if }i\in\overline{S}\end{cases},

(205)

where

\gamma_{i}=\begin{cases}\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{Mark}(\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},x_{i})=\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}(x_{i})&\text{if }\mathsf{vrd}_{i}=\top\\
\bot&\text{if }\mathsf{vrd}_{i}=\bot.\end{cases}

(206)

Finally, it sends
\{\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

Since we have
\gamma_{i}=C[\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},y_{i}](x_{i})
, it follows that this game is indistinguishability from the previous game by the straightforward reduction to the extractability of
\mathsf{SFE}
in the extraction mode.
Thus, we have
|\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{0}]-\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{1}]|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.
Note that the simulation of this game does not require any marked signing key for the
i
-th instance of
\mathsf{WDS}
if
i\in S
and
\mathsf{vrd}_{i}=\bot
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
:

In this hybrid, the challenger aborts the game and sets the output of the game to be
0
(i.e.,
\mathpzc{A}
looses the game), once it turns out that there is
i\in S
such that
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Check}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},x_{i},y_{i})=\bot
.
Namely, we replace Step
4’
in the previous hybrid with the following:

4”

The challenger computes the second message to the adversary as follows.

•

It runs

\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Ext}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i})\to x_{i}

and
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Check}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},x_{i},y_{i})=\mathsf{vrd}_{i}
for
i\in S
.

•

If
\mathsf{vrd}_{i}=\bot
for any of
i\in S
, it aborts the game and sets the output of the game to be
0
.

•

It computes

\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}\leftarrow\begin{cases}\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Sim}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i},\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}(x_{i}))&\text{if }i\in S\\
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Send}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i},C[\mathsf{WUPF}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},y_{i}])&\text{if }i\in\overline{S}.\end{cases}

(207)

Finally, it sends
\{\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

We claim
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{2}]\geq\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{1}]-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.
To show this, we observe that the only scenario where the adversary
\mathpzc{A}
wins the game in the previous hybrid but not in the current one occurs when
\mathpzc{A}
chooses
y_{i}
such that
\mathsf{vrd}_{i}=\bot
for some
i\in S
, yet still succeeds in signing on
\{m_{j}\}_{j\in[2n]}
.
However, this implies that
\mathpzc{A}
has successfully forged a signature
\sigma_{i}
satisfying
\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{SigVrfy}(\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{svk}_{i},\sigma_{i})
without getting marked signing key for the
i
-th instance of
\mathsf{WDS}
.
Note that the probability that
\mathpzc{A}
has made a signing query to
\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},\cdot)
on input
s_{i}
is negligible, since
s_{i}
is chosen uniformly at random and
\mathpzc{A}
is not allowed to make signing queries once it receives
s_{i}
.
By a straightforward reduction to the EUF-CMA security of
\mathsf{WDS}
, the claim follows.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
:

In this hybrid, we change the winning condition of the adversary.
Namely, we replace Step
7
and Step
8
of the game with the following.

7’

The challenger parses the adversary
\mathpzc{A}
right before Step
7
of the game as a unitary circuit
U{A}
and a quantum state
\rho{A}
.
It then constructs a quantum forger
\mathpzc{F}
for
\mathsf{WDS}
that takes as input
\{m_{i}\}_{i\in S}
as input and outputs
\{\mathsf{WDS}.\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in S}

from
\mathpzc{A}
as follows.

\mathpzc{F}(\{m_{i}\}_{i\in S})
:

It chooses
m_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}
for
i\in\overline{S}
and runs
\mathpzc{A}
on input
\{m_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to obtain
\{\mathsf{WDS}.\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in 2n}
.
Finally, it outputs
\{\mathsf{WDS}.\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in S}
.

8’

The challenger parses

\mathpzc{F}
as a pair of unitary
U{F}
and a quantum state
\rho{F}
.
Then, it runs the extraction algorithm as

\mathsf{WDS}.\mathpzc{Ext}(\{\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{xk}_{i}\}_{i\in S},(U{F},\rho{F}))\to\{x^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in S}
.

9’

If
x_{i}=x^{\prime}_{i}
holds for all
i\in S
,
the challenger sets the output of the game to be
1
.
Otherwise, it sets it to be
0
.

By the parallel extractability of
\mathsf{WDS}
,
we have
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{3}]\geq\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{2}]-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.
The proof is almost the same as

Lemma

7.3
and thus omitted.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
:

In this game, we replace Step
9’
of the above game with the following:

9”

If
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Check}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},x^{\prime}_{i},y_{i})=\top
holds for all
i\in S
,
the challenger sets the output of the game to be
1
.
Otherwise, it sets it to be
0
.

We observe that
x^{\prime}_{i}=x_{i}
in Step
9’
implies
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Check}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},x^{\prime}_{i},y_{i})=\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Check}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},x_{i},y_{i})=\top
.
To see the latter equality holds, recall that the game is set to abort if
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Check}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},x_{i},y_{i})=\bot
for any
i\in S

in Step
4”
. Thus, if the game continues to Step
9’
, the equality holds.
We therefore have
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{4}]\geq\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{3}]
.
(Actually,
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{4}]=\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{3}]
holds due to the uniqueness of
x_{i}
that passes the check. However, showing the inequality suffices for our proof.)

\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}
:

In this game, we switch Step
4”
in the previous hybrid back to Step
4’
.
The difference from the previous hybrid is that it continues the game even if
\mathsf{vrd}_{i}=\bot
for some
i\in S
.
Since the adversary had no chance in winning the game in such a case in the previous hybrid, this change only increases the chance of the adversary winning.
We therefore have
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{5}]\geq\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{4}]
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}
:

In this hybrid, we switch Step
4’
in the previous hybrid back to Step
4
.
Namely, it computes
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{Send}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\allowbreak\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i},C[\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},y_{i}])\rightarrow\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}_{i}
for all
i\in[2n]
again.
Similarly to the change from
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
, the winning probability of
\mathpzc{A}
in this game does not change significantly by the extractability of
\mathsf{SFE}
in the extraction mode. Namely, we have
|\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{6}]-\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{5}]|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.
Note that we do not need
\{\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i}\}_{i\in S}
any more to run the game.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{7}
:

In this hybrid, we change the way
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i}
is sampled for
i\in S
.
Namely, it is sampled as
(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{CRSGen}(1^{\lambda},2)
for all
i\in[2n]
.
Concretely, we replace Step
1
with the following:

1’

The challenger computes the first message to the adversary as follows.

•

For
i\in[2n]
, it runs
\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})\to(\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{vk}_{i},\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{msk}_{i},\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{xk}_{i})
.

•

It chooses random subset
S
of
[2n]
with size
n
.

•

It runs
(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{FuncGen}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{mode})
and
(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{CRSGen}(1^{\lambda},2)
for
i\in[2n]
, where
\mathsf{mode}=1
if
i\in S
and
\mathsf{mode}=2
if
i\in\overline{S}
.

Finally, it sends
\mathsf{svk}=\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{pp}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{crs}_{i},\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{vk}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.
It privately keeps the master secret key
\mathsf{msk}=\{\mathsf{WDS}.\mathsf{msk}_{i}\}_{i\in[2n]}
and the deletion verification key
\mathsf{dvk}=\left(S,\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{S}}\right)
.

Since we do not need
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i}
for
i\in S
to run
\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{7}
, a straightforward reduction to the mode indistinguishability of
\mathsf{SFE}
implies that
\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{7}
are computationally indistinguishable.
Therefore, we have
|\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{6}]-\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{7}]|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{8}
:

In this game, we change the way how the challenger runs
\mathsf{DelVrfy}
.
In particular, the challenger computes
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{StaRcv}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{ct}_{i})\to\{\alpha_{i,j}^{b}\}_{j,b}
and
d^{*}_{i}
for all
i\in[2n]
,
instead of only for
i\in\overline{S}
.
Still, the inversion of
y_{i}
and
the check of
Equation

187
are performed only for
i\in\overline{S}
.
Concretely, we replace Step
6
with the following.

6’

Then, the challenger runs (the modified version of)
\mathsf{DelVrfy}
as follows.

•

For
i\in[2n]
, it runs
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{StaRcv}(\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{i})\to\{\alpha_{i,j}^{b}\}_{j\in[w],b\in\{0,1\}}
.

•

For
i\in[2n]
, it computes

d^{*}_{i}=(d^{*}_{i}[1],\ldots,d^{*}_{i}[w])\in\{0,1\}^{w}
as

d^{*}_{i}[j]\coloneqq c_{i,j}\cdot(\alpha^{0}_{i,j}\oplus\alpha^{1}_{i,j})
.

•

For
i\in\overline{S}
, it runs
(x_{i,0},x_{i,1})\leftarrow\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{Invert}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},y_{i})
.
If the inversion fails for any of
i\in\overline{S}
, the output of
\mathsf{DelVrfy}
is
\bot
.

•

For
i\in\overline{S}
, it checks whether

(d_{i}\oplus d^{*}_{i})\cdot(x_{i,0}\oplus x_{i,1})=0~\land~\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{GoodSet}(\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},y_{i},d_{i}\oplus d^{*}_{i})=\top

(208)

holds using
\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i}
.
If the above holds for all
i\in\overline{S}
, the output is
\top
and otherwise,
\bot
.

If the output of the algorithm is
\bot
, then it indicates that the output of the game is
0
(i.e.,
\mathpzc{A}
failed to win the game).
Otherwise, the challenger returns

\mathsf{dvk}=\left(S,\{\mathsf{NTCF}.\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{S}}\right)
to
\mathpzc{A}

and continues the game.

We can see that this change is only conceptual, since the outcome of the verification is unchanged.
We may therefore have

\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{8}]=\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{7}]
.

Based on the above discussion, it is established that

\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{8}]\geq\epsilon-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
, which is non-negligible by our assumption.
However, we can show
\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{8}]={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
assuming the cut-and-choose adaptive hardcore bit property of
\mathsf{NTCF}
, by a very similar reduction to
Lemma

6.4
.
This results in a contradiction as desired.
∎

Appendix A
Special Dual-Mode SFE from LWE

In this section, we show a construction of special dual-mode SFE from the LWE assumption.

In general, by relying on well-known Yao’s protocol, SFE can be constructed from oblivious transfer (OT) by additionally using garbled circuits which can be based on OWFs.
We can prove that the resulting scheme become special dual-mode SFE if the underlying OT satisfies an analogous special dual-mode property.
In this section, we first formally define special dual-mode OT and provide its realization assuming the LWE assumption based on the construction by
[
PVW08
]
.
Then, we provide a sketch on how to achieve dual-mode SFE from dual-mode OT using GC.

A.1
Definition of Special Dual-mode OT

In this subsection, we define special dual-mode OT.

Definition A.1 (Special Dual-mode OT).

A special dual-mode OT scheme
\mathsf{OT}
is a tuple of four algorithms
\mathsf{OT}=\mathsf{OT}.(\mathsf{CRSGen},\mathsf{Rec}_{1},\allowbreak\mathsf{Send},\mathsf{Rec}_{2})
.
Below, let
\{0,1\}^{\ell}
be the string space of
\mathsf{OT}
.

\mathsf{CRSGen}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{mode})\rightarrow\mathsf{crs}
:

The CRS generation algorithm is a PPT algorithm that takes a security parameter
1^{\lambda}
and
\mathsf{mode}\in\{1,2\}
, and outputs a string
\mathsf{crs}
.

\mathsf{mode}=1
indicates that the system in the “extractable mode", while
\mathsf{mode}=2
indicates “hiding mode".

\mathsf{Rec}_{1}(\mathsf{crs},b)\rightarrow(\mathsf{msg}^{(1)},\mathsf{st})
:

This is a classical PPT algorithm supposed to be run by a receiver to generate the first message of the protocol.
It takes as input the CRS
\mathsf{crs}
and a bit
b\in\{0,1\}
and outputs the first message
\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}
and a secret state
\mathsf{st}
.

\mathsf{Send}(\mathsf{crs},\mathsf{msg}^{(1)},(z_{0},z_{1}))\rightarrow\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}
:

This is a classical PPT algorithm supposed to be run by a sender to generate the second message of the protocol.
It takes as input the CRS
\mathsf{crs}
, a message
\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}
from the receiver,
and a pair of strings
(z_{0},z_{1})\in\{0,1\}^{\ell\times 2}
and outputs the second message
\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}
.

\mathsf{Rec}_{2}(\mathsf{crs},b,\mathsf{st},\mathsf{msg}^{(2)})\rightarrow z
:

This is a classical PPT algorithm supposed to be run by a receiver to derive the output.
It takes as input the CRS
\mathsf{crs}
, a bit
b\in\{0,1\}
, a state
\mathsf{st}
, and a message
\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}
from the sender, and outputs a string
z
.

We require dual-mode OT to satisfy the following properties.

Evaluation correctness:

For all
b\in\{0,1\}
,
\mathsf{mode}\in\{1,2\}
, and a pair of strings
(z_{0},z_{1})\in\{0,1\}^{\ell\times 2}
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathsf{Rec}_{2}(\mathsf{crs},b,\mathsf{st},\mathsf{msg}^{(2)})=z_{b}\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{crs},\mathsf{td})\leftarrow\mathsf{CRSGen}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{mode})\\
(\mathsf{msg}^{(1)},\mathsf{st})\leftarrow\mathsf{Rec}_{1}(\mathsf{crs},b)\\
\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}\leftarrow\mathsf{Send}(\mathsf{crs},\mathsf{msg}^{(1)},(z_{0},z_{1}))\end{array}\right]=1.

Unique state:

For all

\mathsf{mode}\in\{1,2\}
,

(\mathsf{crs},\mathsf{td})\in\mathrm{Supp}(\mathsf{CRSGen}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{mode}))
,
b\in\{0,1\}
, and
(\mathsf{msg}^{(1)},\mathsf{st})\in\mathrm{Supp}(\mathsf{Rec}_{1}(\mathsf{crs},b))
,
\mathsf{st}
is the unique state that corresponds to

\mathsf{crs}
,
b
, and
\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}
, that is, there is no
\mathsf{st}^{\prime}\neq\mathsf{st}
such that
(\mathsf{msg}^{(1)},\mathsf{st}^{\prime})\in\mathrm{Supp}(\mathsf{Rec}_{1}(\mathsf{crs},b))
.
We denote the unique state
\mathsf{st}
such that
(\mathsf{msg}^{(1)},\mathsf{st})\in\mathrm{Supp}(\mathsf{Rec}_{1}(\mathsf{crs},b))
by
\mathsf{st}_{b\to\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}}
.
25
25

25

The state also depends on
\mathsf{crs}
, but we omit it from the notation.

Mode indistinguishability:

For all QPT algorithm
\mathcal{A}
, we have

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{
\Pr[\cA(\crs)=1: (\crs,\td) \la\CRSGen(1^\secp,1) ]-
\Pr[\cA(\crs)=1: (\crs,\td) \la\CRSGen(1^\secp,2) ]
}\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(212)

Statistical security against malicious senders in the hiding mode:

For all
\mathsf{crs}
output by
\mathsf{CRSGen}(1^{\lambda},2)
, we require the following statistical indistinguishability:

\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{0}\approx_{s}\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{1}\quad\mbox{where}\quad(\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}_{b},\mathsf{st})\leftarrow\mathsf{Rec}_{1}(\mathsf{crs},b)\quad\mbox{for}\quad b\in\{0,1\}.

(213)

State recoverability in the hiding mode:

There exists a classical PPT algorithm
\mathsf{StaRcv}
that takes as input the trapdoor
\mathsf{SFE}.\mathsf{td}
and the first message of the receiver
\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}
and outputs a pair of strings
(\alpha^{0},\alpha^{1})
or
\bot
, where the latter indicates that the state recovery failed.
We require that for all
b\in\{0,1\}
, the following holds:

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathsf{st}=\alpha^{b}\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{crs},\mathsf{td})\leftarrow\mathsf{CRSGen}(1^{\lambda},2)\\
(\mathsf{msg}^{(1)},\mathsf{st})\leftarrow\mathsf{Rec}_{1}(\mathsf{crs},b)\\
(\alpha^{0},\alpha^{1})\leftarrow\mathsf{StaRcv}(\mathsf{td},\mathsf{msg}^{(1)})\end{array}\right]=1.

Extractability in the extraction mode:

For classical algorithms
\mathsf{Ext}
and
\mathsf{Sim}
, let us consider the following experiment formalized by the experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{OT},\mathpzc{A},\mathsf{Ext},\mathsf{Sim}}^{\mathsf{ext}\mbox{-}\mathsf{sim}}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{coin})
between an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
and the challenger:

1.

The challenger runs
(\mathsf{crs},\mathsf{td})\leftarrow\mathsf{CRSGen}(1^{\lambda},1)
and sends
(\mathsf{crs},\mathsf{td})
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

2.

\mathpzc{A}
sends
\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}
and a pair of messages
(z_{0},z_{1})\in\{0,1\}^{\ell\times 2}
.

3.

The challenger runs
\mathrm{Ext}(\mathsf{td},\mathsf{msg}^{(1)})\to b
and
it computes

\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}\leftarrow\begin{cases}\mathsf{Send}(\mathsf{crs},\mathsf{msg}^{(1)},(z_{0},z_{1})),&\text{if }\mathsf{coin}=0\\
\mathsf{Sim}(\mathsf{crs},\mathsf{msg}^{(1)},z_{b})&\text{if }\mathsf{coin}=1.\end{cases}

(217)

Then, it gives
\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

4.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs a guess
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
for
\mathsf{coin}
. The challenger outputs
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
as the final output of the experiment.

For any QPT
\mathpzc{A}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{OT},\mathpzc{A},\mathsf{Ext},\mathsf{Sim}}^{\mathsf{ext}\mbox{-}\mathsf{sim}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\absolutevalue{\Pr[\expb{\OT,\qA, \Extract, \Sim}{ext}{sim} (1^\secp,0) = 1] - \Pr[\expb{\OT,\qA, \Extract, \Sim}{ext}{sim} (1^\secp,1) = 1] }\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(218)

Efficient state superposition:

We require that there exists a QPT “coherent version"
\mathpzc{Rec}_{1}
of
\mathsf{Rec}_{1}
that is given a quantum state
\ket{\psi}=\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}}\alpha_{b}\ket{b}

and
\mathsf{crs}
and works as follows:

It first generates a state that is within negligible trace distance of the following state:

\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}}\alpha_{b}\sum_{\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}\in\mathrm{Supp}(\mathsf{Rec}_{1}(\mathsf{crs},b))}\sqrt{p_{b\to\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}}}\ket{b}\ket{\st_{b\to\msg^{(1)}} }\ket{\msg^{(1)}}

(219)

where
p_{b\to\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}}
is the probability that
\mathsf{Rec}_{1}(\mathsf{crs},b)
outputs
\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}
.
Then, it measures the last register to obtain
\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}
and outputs the residual quantum state
\ket{\psi'}
.

We remark that implementing the above by simply running the classical algorithm in the superposition does not work, since it may leave the entanglement with the randomness for
\mathsf{Rec}_{1}
.

A.2
Dual-mode OT from LWE

In this subsection, we show that the dual-mode OT based on LWE in
[
PVW08
]
satisfies our requirements of special dual-mode OT as in
Definition

A.1
under a certain parameter regime.

Lattice preliminaries

We review definitions and lemmata on lattice-based cryptography.

For
q\in\mathbb{N}
, we write
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
to mean the additive group of integers modulo
q
. We represent elements of
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
by elements in
\mathbb{Z}\cap(-q/2,q/2]
and often identify the elements of
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
and their representatives.
For a vector
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\|\mathbf{x}\|
denotes the Euclidean norm of
\mathbf{x}
and
\|\mathbf{x}\|_{\infty}
denotes the infinity norm of
\mathbf{x}
, that is,
\|\mathbf{x}\|_{\infty}\coloneqq\max_{i\in[n]}|x_{i}|
.

For
m\in\mathbb{N}
, the Gaussian function
\rho_{\sigma}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow[0,1]
with a scaling parameter
\sigma>0
is defined as follows:

\rho_{\sigma}(\mathbf{x})\coloneqq\exp(-\pi\|\vx\|^2/\sigma^2).

(220)

For any
q\in\mathbb{N}
and
\sigma>0
,
we define the truncated discrete Gaussian distribution
D_{\mathbb{Z}_{q},\sigma}
with the support
\{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}:|x|\leq\sigma\sqrt{m}\}

by the following probability density function:

D_{\mathbb{Z}_{q},\sigma}(x)=\frac{\rho_{\sigma}(x)}{\sum_{y\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}:\|y\|\leq\sigma\sqrt{m}}\rho_{\sigma}(y)}.

(221)

More generally, for any
q,m\in\mathbb{N}
and
\sigma>0
, we define the truncated discrete Gaussian distribution
D_{\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\sigma}
with the support
\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}:\|\mathbf{x}\|_{\infty}\leq\sigma\}

by the following probability density function:

D_{\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\sigma}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})=\prod_{i\in[m]}D_{\mathbb{Z}_{q},\sigma}(x_{i})

(222)

Lemma A.2 (Noise smudging
[
BCM+21
]
).

For
q,m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\sigma>0
, and
\mathbf{e}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
such that
\|\mathbf{e}\|\leq\sigma\sqrt{m}
, the statistical distance between
D_{\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\sigma}
and
D_{\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\sigma}+\mathbf{e}
is at most
\sqrt{2\left(1-\exp\left(-2\pi\sqrt{m}\|\mathbf{e}\|/\sigma\right)\right)}

Definition A.3 (Learning with errors).

Let
n,m,q\in\mathbb{N}
and
\chi
be a distribution over
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
, where
n,m,q,\chi
depends on the security parameter
\lambda
.
We say that the learning with errors (LWE) problem
\mathrm{LWE}_{n,m,q,\chi}
is quantumly hard if for any QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}
,

\absolutevalue{\Pr[\qA(\mA,\vs^\trans\mA+\ve^\trans)= 1]-
\Pr[\qA(\mA,\vu)= 1]
}\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)

(223)

where
\mathbf{A}\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
,
\mathbf{s}\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
, and
\mathbf{e}\leftarrow\chi
.

Lemma A.4 (Lattice trapdoors
[
MP12
]
).

Let
n,m,q\in\mathbb{N}
with
m=\Omega(n\log q)
. There exists a pair of PPT algorithms
(\mathsf{TrapGen},\mathsf{Invert})
that satisfy the following.

•

\mathsf{TrapGen}(1^{n},1^{m},q)\rightarrow(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T}):
On input
1^{n}
,
1^{m}
, and
q
, it outputs a matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
and a trapdoor
\mathbf{T}
. The statistical distance between the distribution of
\mathbf{A}
generated by
\mathsf{TrapGen}(1^{n},1^{m},q)
and the uniform distribution over
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
is negligible in
n
.

•

\mathsf{Invert}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T},\mathbf{v})\rightarrow(\mathbf{s}^{\prime},\mathbf{e}^{\prime}):
On input
\mathbf{A}
,
\mathbf{T}
, and
\mathbf{v}
, it returns vectors
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
and
\mathbf{e}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
.
The following holds for some constant
C>0
:
If
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T})
is generated by
\mathsf{TrapGen}(1^{n},1^{m},q)
and
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{e}^{\top}
for
\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
and
\mathbf{e}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
such that
\|\mathbf{e}\|\leq q/(C\sqrt{n\log q})
,
\mathsf{Invert}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T},\mathbf{v})
outputs
(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{e})
with an overwhelming probability in
n
.

Lemma A.5 (
[
GPV08
,
PVW08
]
26
26

26

In
[
GPV08
,
PVW08
]
, they consider the untruncated discrete Gaussian distribution but this does not affect the statement since the truncated and untruncated discrete Gaussian distributions are negligible close.
).

Let
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
and
q
be a prime. For any
\delta,\tau>0
, let
M_{\delta,\tau}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
be the subset consisting of
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{v})\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
such that the distribution
\{(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{t},\mathbf{v}^{\top}\mathbf{t}):\mathbf{t}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\tau}\}
has statistical distance within at most
\delta
from the uniform distribution. There is a PPT algorithm
\mathsf{IsMessy}
with single-bit outputs that satisfies the following.
If
m\geq 2(n+1)\log q
and
\tau\geq\sqrt{qm}\log^{2}m
, there is
\delta={\mathsf{negl}}(n)
such that for an overwhelming fraction of
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T})\leftarrow\mathsf{TrapGen}(1^{n},1^{m},q)
, the following hold.

•

For all but an at most
(1/2\sqrt{q})^{m}
fraction of
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
,
\mathsf{IsMessy}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T},\mathbf{v})
returns
1
with overwhelming probability.

•

For any
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
such that
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{v})\notin M_{\delta,\tau}
,
\mathsf{IsMessy}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T},\mathbf{v})
returns
0
with an overwhelming probability.

Construction

It is easy to see that all the properties required for special dual-mode OT are preserved under parallel repetition. Therefore, it suffices to construct it for the case of
\ell=1
.
The scheme is described below, where the parameters are specified below the description.

\mathsf{CRSGen}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{mode})
:

Given the security parameter
1^{\lambda}
and
\mathsf{mode}\in\{1,2\}
, generate
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T})\leftarrow\mathsf{TrapGen}(1^{n},1^{m},q)
.

•

If
\mathsf{mode}=1
(i.e., in the extractable mode), choose
\mathbf{v}\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
.

•

If
\mathsf{mode}=2
(i.e., in the hiding mode), choose
\mathbf{s}\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
and

\mathbf{e}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\sigma}

and compute
\mathbf{v}^{\top}=\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{e}^{\top}
.

Output
\mathsf{crs}=(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{v})
and
\mathsf{td}=(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T},\mathbf{v})
.

\mathsf{Rec}_{1}(\mathsf{crs},b)
:

On input
\mathsf{crs}=(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{v})
and
b\in\{0,1\}
, choose
\mathbf{r}\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}

and
\mathbf{e}^{\prime}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\sigma^{\prime}}

, compute
\mathbf{u}_{b}^{\top}=\mathbf{r}^{\top}\mathbf{A}+{\mathbf{e}^{\prime}}^{\top}

and
\mathbf{u}_{1-b}^{\top}=\mathbf{u}_{b}^{\top}+(-1)^{b}\mathbf{v}^{\top}

, and output
\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}=\mathbf{u}_{0}
and
\mathsf{st}=\mathbf{r}
.

\mathsf{Send}(\mathsf{crs},\mathsf{msg}^{(1)},(z_{0},z_{1}))\rightarrow\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}
:

On input
\mathsf{crs}
,
\mathsf{msg}^{(1)}=\mathbf{u}_{0}
, and
(z_{0},z_{1})\in\{0,1\}^{2}
, let
\mathbf{u}_{1}=\mathbf{u}_{0}+\mathbf{v}
,
choose
\mathbf{t}_{b}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\tau}
, compute
\mathbf{w}_{b}=\left(\begin{matrix}\mathbf{A}\\
\mathbf{u}_{b}^{\top}\end{matrix}\right)\mathbf{t}_{b}+\left(\begin{matrix}\mathbf{0}\\
z_{b}\lfloor q/2\rfloor\mod q\end{matrix}\right)
for
b\in\{0,1\}
, and output
\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}=(\mathbf{w}_{0},\mathbf{w}_{1})
.

\mathsf{Rec}_{2}(\mathsf{crs},b,\mathsf{st},\mathsf{msg}^{(2)})\rightarrow z
:

On input
\mathsf{crs}
,
b\in\{0,1\}
,
\mathsf{st}=\mathbf{r}
, and
\mathsf{msg}^{(2)}=(\mathbf{w}_{0},\mathbf{w}_{1})
,
let
\mathbf{w}_{b,[1,n]}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
be the vector consisting of the first
m
coordinates of
\mathbf{w}_{b}
and
w_{b,n+1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}
be the remaining coordinate,
output
0
if
\left|w_{b,n+1}-\mathbf{r}^{\top}\mathbf{w}_{b,[1,n]}\mod q\right|<q/4
and output
1
otherwise.

Parameters

We choose the parameters
n,m,q,\sigma,\sigma^{\prime},\tau
so that the following hold.

•

m\geq 2(n+1)\log q

•

\mathrm{LWE}_{n,m,q,D_{\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\sigma}}
is quantumly hard.
We need
